Finitely Correlated States Driven by Topological Dynamics
Eric B. Roon & Jeffrey H. Schenker Department of Mathematics Michigan State University East Lansing, MI., U.S.A

Abstract
Let ( Ω , ℙ ) (\Omega,\mathbb{P}) be a standard probability space and let ϑ : Ω → Ω \vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega be a measure preserving ergodic homeomorphism. Let 𝒜 \mathcal{A} be a C ∗ C^{*} -algebra with a unit and let 𝒜 ℤ \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}} be the quasi-local algebra associated to the spin chain with one-site algebra 𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Equip 𝒜 ℤ \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}} with the group action of translation by k k -units, τ k ∈ A ​ u ​ t ​ ( 𝒜 ℤ ) \tau_{k}\in Aut(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}) for k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} . We study the problem of finding a disordered matrix product state decomposition for disordered states ψ ⁡ ( ω ) \psi(\omega) on 𝒜 ℤ \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}} with the covariance symmetry condition ψ ⁡ ( ω ) ∘ τ k = ψ ⁡ ( ϑ k ​ ω ) \psi(\omega)\circ\tau_{k}=\psi(\vartheta^{k}\omega) . This can be seen as an ergodic generalization of the results of Fannes, Nachtergaele, and Werner [ 30 ] . To reify our structure theory, we present a disordered state ν ω \nu_{\omega} obtained by sampling the AKLT model [ 2 ] in parameter space. We go on to show that ν ω \nu_{\omega} has a nearest-neighbor parent Hamiltonian, its bulk spectral gap closes, but it has almost surely exponentially decaying correlations, and finally, that ν ω \nu_{\omega} is time-reversal symmetry protected with a Tasaki index of − 1 -1 .



0
Introduction

Studying the affects of disorder on quantum many-body systems has been a topic of great interest in recent years yielding a vast array of results (e.g. see
[
1
,
7
,
20
,
38
,
60
]
) and has taken great inspiration from the disordered single-body literature. For example, the study of zero-velocity Lieb-Robinson bounds is analogous to the study of (complete) Anderson localization.
[
3
,
38
]
(see the books and surveys
[
3
,
9
,
13
,
78
,
79
]
).

Understanding the structure of disordered states in the quantum spin system context is a crucial task on the way to gaining deeper insight about disordered interacting systems and their thermodynamic properties. Therefore, obtaining access to a structure theory for disordered quantum spin states would be a large step forward in understanding disordered quantum systems on the whole. In general, however, understanding disordered states has many challenges, even for the single-body case, and there is not a clear analogy for single-particle dynamics to inform what methods one might try.

In the deterministic many-body setting, great progress has been made in the last three decades by utilizing the structure of the underlying lattice to reduce the properties of a translation invariant bulk state to properties witnessed by a smaller ancilla. Specifically, the
matrix product states
formalism, pioneered by
[
30
]
in the early 1990’s, allows one to model important properties of a deterministic, finitely-correlated, translation-invariant state on a quantum spin chain via dynamics on a finite-dimensional ancilla. Out of the MPS theory grew the so-called tensor network theory as a multi-dimensional generalization of this important class of states
[
11
,
16
]
.

Recent steps toward a theory of
disordered
MPS have been made in
[
54
,
55
]
, and
[
62
]
. These works focus on constructing MPS-like states that are
translation covariant
, i.e., translation invariant in distribution. The aim of this paper is to show, conversely that disordered states with finite correlations and translation covariance admit a kind of disordered MPS factorization. Furthermore, we show that states with finite correlations are weak* dense in the set of all translation covariant states. Therefore, we establish a robust machinery to study TCVS that parallels the development of MPS for translation invariant states.

As an example, we perform an in-depth investigation into a disordered deformation of the famed AKLT state
[
2
]
, which is well-known to have a concrete, nearest-neighbor parent Hamiltonian for which the MPS is the ground state, a non-trivial time-reversal index, and a bulk gap. In our disordered deformation, we show that there is a disordered nearest-neighbor parent Hamiltonian for which our disordered state is the ground state and that there is a nontrivial time-reversal index in mean. However, in contrast with the original AKLT state, the correlation structure of the disordered-AKLT type state is such that the bulk gap necessarily vanishes for
any
finite range, translation covariant disordered parent Hamiltonian.

0.1
Background and Relation to Other Works

Since their inception in the early 1990’s
[
30
]
, MPS have served as an important tool to construct or approximate ground states of local Hamiltonians on infinite quantum spin chains. These models provide a rich insight into the structure of quantum states in one spatial dimension, and furthermore such MPS serve as useful approximates of more general states
[
28
,
87
]
. After an observation by Vidal
[
88
]
that finite-chain MPS are computationally efficient, their utility for simulating quantum many body systems in finite chains cemented the MPS formalism as a standard tool for the modern condensed matter theorist. As such, a large volume of work has been devoted to understanding MPS and their properties since then. See the non-exhaustive list
[
4
,
16
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
33
,
40
,
58
,
68
,
76
]
.

Recently, various authors have begun to explore
disordered
MPS on both the finite and infinite chain,
[
14
,
50
,
54
,
55
,
76
]
, as well as random tensor networks (see e.g.
[
39
,
42
]
). A lot of the random MPS literature thus far aims to understand probability distributions on MPS – that is, a translation-invariant MPS selected at random – and their relevant properties up to some large probability
[
44
,
50
]
. Another facet of the probabilistic interpretation of these works is their connection to Hidden Markov Models (see
[
27
,
76
]
).

Recent work by Movassagh and Schenker introduced the
ergodic matrix product state

[
54
,
55
]
which is
not
translation invariant. By contrast to questions of typicality for translation-invariant states, Movassagh and Schenker aimed to study a system with intrinsic disorder, resulting in a thermodynamic limit which does not have translation symmetry. A generalization of
[
55
]
to the case when the on-site algebra is a kind of infinite-dimensional operator algebra was completed by Nelson and Roon in
[
62
]
. One of the key properties that both sets of work centered on is the notion of
translation covariance
with respect to an ergodic map. More specifically, if
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
denotes a probability space equipped with a measure-preserving ergodic map
\vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega
, and
\omega\mapsto\psi_{\omega}^{[-N,N]}
is a disordered state on the interval
[-N,N]\cap\mathbb{Z}
which is generated by an ergodic MPS representation, then in the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
we are interested in studying states which satisfy translation covariance:

\psi^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}\circ\tau_{k}=\psi^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\,.

(0.1)

In words, equation (
0.1
) says that translation of an observable leads to a corresponding shift in the disorder with
statistically
identical expectation values, but not necessarily
trajectory-wise
identical values. Indeed the construction of the relevant thermodynamic limiting state in both
[
55
,
62
]
shows (
0.1
) is an emergent property from the local, ergodic MPS data. This sets ergodic MPS apart from the states studied in
[
44
,
50
]
, since there, disorder is introduced when picking the MPS representation, but the same representation is repeated throughout the lattice. Translation co-variance leads to a distinct type of global symmetry for disordered states.

Both
[
55
]
and
[
62
]
took inspiration from
[
30
]
, but each is only a one-way construction of a translation covariant state from local data. By comparison the authors in
[
30
]
give a complete classification of translation invariant states in terms of the linear dynamics on an auxiliary
C^{*}
-algebra. In the case this auxiliary is finite-dimensional, one recovers the notion of a
C^{*}
FCS.

Part of the appeal of MPS in the first place is that they have a relatively simple description and properties of the thermodynamic limit can be calculated using the matrices from which the state is comprised. For example, so-called
string order parameters
have been used to study the symmetry properties of states in spin chains. More specifically, if
\psi
is a (deterministic state) on a quantum spin chain, then
[
69
]
say it has a string order parameter if there exists a one-site unitary
u\neq\mathbbm{1}
, and a pair of one-site observables
O_{x}
and
O_{y}
so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}|\psi(O_{x}\otimes u^{\otimes n}\otimes O_{y})|\neq 0\,.

For an MPS, the appearance of a non-trivial string order parameter occurs whenever
u
is a local symmetry of the state
[
69
, Theorem 2]
. A specific case of interest to us is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
index for time-reversal symmetry. This symmetry is implemented by a one-site unitary and interacts with the MPS in a specific way
[
71
,
72
]
. From the relations obtained in
[
71
]
, one extracts a quantity
\sigma_{TR}\in\{\pm 1\}
. Ogata
[
63
]
goes on to study this in the thermodynamic limit using an operator algebraic treatment. Tasaki
[
82
]
gives an analytical expression for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index and goes on to show that the index cannot change unless a phase transition takes place. One upshot of the aforementioned works is that they all show that
\sigma_{TR}=-1
for the AKLT ground state, distinguishing it from the “trivial phase” of states with
\sigma_{TR}=-1
.

0.2
Main results

We restrict our attention to models of disorder which can be described by a suitable probability space. Our working assumption for the rest of the paper is as follows.

Assumption 1
.

The an underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a compact Hausdorff space,
\mathcal{F}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by open sets, and
\mathbb{P}
is a Radon measure. Furthermore,
\Omega
is equipped with a measure-preserving homeomorphism
\vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega
.

Remark 0.1
.

For example, one could take
\Omega=K^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
K
a compact set,
\vartheta
the bilateral shift, and
\mathbb{P}=\bigotimes_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu
with
\mu
fixed Radon measure on
K
. This would model independent, identically, distributed disorder at each site
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

That is, we aim to investigate the structure of translation covariant states which are ‘driven’ by a topological dynamical system. This is actually quite a general assumption as we discuss in Section
1.1
in the preliminaries. More specifically, the Jewett-Krieger theorem (see
1.5
below) shows that (up to a.e. equal versions) every ergodic dynamical system on a
standard
probability space can be modeled by a probability space satisfying Assumption
1
. Indeed a specific scenario where Assumption
1
can be used is the case that the relevant quantities are independent, identically distributed random variables with a continuous density function. See Lemma
1.3
below.

To continue our discussion, let us briefly recall the construction of a quantum spin chain. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra with a unit
\mathbbm{1}
, and consider the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}
. To each
z\in\mathbb{Z}
we associate an isomorphic copy
\mathcal{A}_{\{z\}}
of
\mathcal{A}
. To each finite subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
, form the local algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\mathcal{A}_{\{z\}}\,.

For most models of interest, the one-site algebra is a matrix algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\{x\}}\cong\mathbb{M}_{n}
, for which the usual tensor product is sufficient. However, our results in section
2
hold in more generality, so we note that in the case when
\mathcal{A}_{\{x\}}
is a general
C^{*}
-algebra, the tensor product is taken to be the minimal tensor product (see
[
12
]
). By allowing
\Lambda\uparrow\mathbb{Z}
, one forms the quasilocal algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
for which
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
for all
\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}
(see the Section
1.3.2
in the preliminaries for more details). The weakly* compact set of states on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is written
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
. We will consider a bipartition of the system into left
\mathcal{L}
and right-hand
\mathcal{R}
, sides corresponding to observables supported on
\{\dots,-2,-1\}
and
\{0,1,2,\dots\}
respectively. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{R}
.

Consider a function

\begin{split}\psi:&\Omega\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})\\
&\omega\longmapsto\psi_{\omega}\end{split}

(0.2)

which is weakly* continuous in
\omega
. We regard
\psi
as a disordered state supported on the bulk
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Theorem A

(Theorem
2.34
Informal)
.

Let
\psi_{\omega}
be a weakly* continuous function of states. Suppose further that
\psi_{\omega}
is translation covariant. Then the following are equivalent:

a.)

The linear space
\psi_{\omega}(\mathcal{L}\otimes(\,\cdot\,))
is finite-dimensional almost surely.

b.)

There exists an auxiliary bundle
\mathcal{B}=\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
where each
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
is a finite dimensional vector space and a family of linear implementing maps
\{E_{a,\omega}\colon a\in\mathcal{A}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
, and a family of functionals
\varrho_{\omega}
so that for a distinguished element
e(\omega)\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
, the following factorization holds:

\psi_{\omega}(a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\varrho_{\vartheta^{m-1}\omega}\circ E_{a_{m},\vartheta^{m}\omega}\circ\cdots E_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}(e(\vartheta^{n}\omega))

(0.3)

This result can be viewed as a disordered generalization of Proposition 2.1 in
[
30
]
and is the starting point for our analysis. In fact, under Assumption
1
, we show there is a topology on
\mathcal{B}
making the aforementioned
transfer apparatus

(E_{a,\omega},\varrho_{\omega},e(\omega))
continuous in
\omega
(see Theorem
2.34
below). If a state
\psi_{\omega}
satisfies the conditions in Theorem
A
, we say it has
small correlations
.

To prove Theorem
A
, we utilize the theory of Banach bundles which was pioneered by J. M. G. Fell in the 1980’s. These are the Banach-space analog of vector bundles
[
43
,
34
]
, and allow us to define a class of dynamical maps which depend continuously on a disorder parameter. Banach bundles have slightly more flexibility since they do not require local triviality as part of the definition
[
22
,
23
,
32
]
. These properties are actually useful since the Fell-Doran theorem (see Theorem
1.11
below) allows one to construct a Banach bundle from a disjoint set of Banach spaces once a vector space of sections is specified
[
32
]
. In fact the transfer maps defined in part b.) of Theorem
A
are
homomorphisms
of Banach bundles in the sense of
[
35
,
36
]
. The general structure of Banach bundles is still an active topic of investigation (see e.g.
[
15
]
).

The finite-dimensionality assumed in part a.) of Theorem
A
seems to be necessary for the Banach bundle structure to hold with the specific equivalence relation we use to define
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
. In general if the fibers
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
are infinite dimensional, the best we can say is that they form a
normed quotient bundle
in the sense of
[
73
]
.

Another key result of
[
30
]
is that MPS are weakly*-dense in the convex set of translation-invariant states. Our next main result is that the same is true for translation covariant states.

Theorem B

(Theorem
2.41
Informal)
.

By
\mathfrak{C}
, we mean the set of translation covariant states on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and the subset of those translation covariant states with small correlations is denoted by
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{C}
. One has that
\mathfrak{K}
is weakly-* dense in
\mathfrak{C}
uniformly in
\omega
.

In fact, more structure is present as there exists an ordering on the
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
’s for which all maps of the form
E_{a^{*}a,\omega}
are completely positive. This makes each
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
into an
operator system
, and in fact
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
is an
operator system quotient
in the sense of
[
47
]
. Interestingly, one can prove a variant of Theorem
A
for which all the fibers are
C^{*}
-algebras by considering the GNS representations of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with respect to the disorder parameter. We show a similar decomposition to equation (
0.3
) Theorem
A
holds on this
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle
\{\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
(see Proposition
2.44
below). At this stage, it is not clear which presentation of the underlying bundle is more advantageous.

To reify our theoretical results, we also present a disordered model that admits a parent Hamiltonian which is almost surely gapless in the thermodynamic limit. Before we describe our model, we note that due to the work of
[
33
]
in the deterministic case, one can always construct a frustration-free parent Hamiltonian for an injective MPS whose gap closes in the bulk. However unlike
[
33
]
, our disordered parent Hamiltonian arises by following the typical construction as in
[
30
,
68
]
. By contrast,
[
33
]
construct the so-called uncle Hamiltonian as a perturbation of the usual parent Hamiltonian.

For our construction, we make use of the following one-parameter family of isometries
V_{\omega}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}

V_{\omega}^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\cos(\omega)&-\sin(\omega)&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\sin(\omega)&-\cos(\omega)&0\end{bmatrix};\quad\omega\in[0,\pi)

which is introduced in
[
30
]
in order to generalize the AKLT model
[
2
]
. We construct a state as follows. Set
E_{a,\delta}=V_{\delta}^{*}(a\otimes(\,\cdot\,))V_{\delta}:\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}
for
a\in\mathbb{M}_{3}
the
3\times 3
matrices. Then, for any interval
[-m,n]
define

\nu^{[m,n]}_{\vec{\delta}}(a_{m}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\tr\{E_{a_{m},\delta_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{a_{n},\delta_{n}}(\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2})\}

where
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in[0,\pi)^{\mathbb{Z}}
. By Takeda’s theorem (see Theorem
1.23
below), these finite volume data define a global state
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\vec{\delta}}
for each
\vec{\delta}\in[0,\pi)^{\mathbb{Z}}
. By equipping a specific subset of
[0,\pi)^{\mathbb{Z}}
with the Kolmogorov extension of a probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, we can use this space to model a bi-infinite sequence of independent, identically distributed random parameters
(\omega_{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z})
taking values in
[0,\pi)
, which are ergodically dependent in the sense that
\omega_{j}=S\omega_{j-1}
where
S
is the shift operator. We then have the following result.

Theorem C

(Theorem
3.20
, Informal)
.

Suppose
(\omega_{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z})
is a family of IID random variables taking values in
[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]
such that
\mathbb{P}[\omega_{0}=0]=\mathbb{P}[\omega_{0}=\frac{\pi}{4}-\epsilon]=0
, where
\mathbb{P}
denotes the underlying probability measure. Then the following hold for the disordered state
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
as constructed above.

i.)

There is a nearest-neighbor parent Hamiltonian
\{h_{j,j+1}(\omega)\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
for which
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is the bulk ground state of the GNS Hamiltonian.

ii.)

Furthermore,
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. pure.

iii.)

With probability one, there is a sequence of intervals
I_{k}\subset\mathbb{Z}
with
|I_{k}|\geq k
and such that correlations of
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
do not decay over
I_{k}
.

iv.)

Therefore, the GNS Hamiltonian
H^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
associated to
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is almost surely gapless. In particular,
any
translation covariant finite ranged Hamiltonian for which
\nu_{\omega}
is the ground state must have a vanishing spectral gap.

v.)

Nevertheless, with probability one,
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
has exponentially decaying correlations.

In fact, due to the local translation co-variance of the parent Hamiltonian, the spectrum of
H_{\omega}
is constant almost surely. The key step in the proof that the bulk-gap closes, is to isolate rare regions where the values of
\omega
are close to zero for long stretches. This contradicts the existence of a gap because for the spin-z observables
S^{z}
, one can prove a lower bound on the correlation function
|\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}(S_{x_{k}}^{z}S_{x_{k}+k}^{z})|\geq f(k)
for which
\limsup_{k\to\infty}f(k)=1
. We then notice that the parent Hamiltonians
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
are translation co-variant with respect to the disorder:
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)=h_{j+1,j+2}(\vartheta^{-1}\omega)
. One concludes that the spectrum of the GNS Hamiltonian for
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is almost surely constant (Corollary
3.16
below).

Therefore, were there a nonzero bulk gap, the exponential clustering theorem
[
61
]
would imply an upper bound which is decaying exponentially fast with probability one, for a contradiction.

Nevertheless, one can find explicit formulas for vector linear functionals
\langle\Gamma_{L}|\cdot|\Gamma_{L}\rangle
on
\mathcal{A}_{[-L,L+1]}
which approximate
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
as
L\to\infty
almost surely. Given these expansions, one can show explicitly that
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is protected by time-reversal symmetry. Recall
[
63
]
, time-reversal symmetry is an automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
sending local spin matrices
S^{w}_{m}\mapsto-S^{w}_{m}
for all
w\in\{x,y,z\}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. A natural question one may ask is whether or not our disordered deformation has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index. The state-of-the-art methods for matrix product states in the deterministic case, involve computing a unitary that locally implements the symmetry
[
65
,
66
,
63
,
83
]
. However, these results rely on a strong uniqueness theorem for injective matrix product states, namely the Fundamental Theorem of Injective Matrix product states
[
16
]
, which does not yet have a disordered analog.

Therefore, we turn to the computational methods of
[
82
]
, which uses the Affleck-Lieb Twist Operator defined as

T_{L}:=\bigotimes_{x=-L}^{L+1}\exp\left\{-2\pi i\frac{x+L}{2L+1}\,S^{z}_{x}\right\}\in\mathcal{A}_{[-L,L+1]}\,.

In the deterministic case of a gapped ground state
\phi
with time-reversal symmetry,
[
82
]
argues that the sign of the expectation value
\mathscr{O}(\phi):=\lim_{L\to\infty}\sgn(\phi(T_{L}))=\pm 1
, and this determines a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index. Namely,
[
82
]
shows that the AKLT state has an index value equal to
-1
. By contrast, we show the following.

Theorem D

(Theorem
3.36
, Informal)
.

Let
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
be the disordered deformation of the AKLT model described in Theorem
C
. Then
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}
is invariant under time-reversal almost surely. Furthermore,

\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}|\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega}(T_{L})+1|=0

That is, the value of the index
\mathcal{O}(\psi^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\omega})
as defined by
[
82
]
, converges to
-1
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
-norm.

0.3
Organization of the Paper

We will now outline the structure of our paper for the reader’s convenience.

In section
1
, we collect results which are more or less tacitly known by experts in the community, but which we shall use frequently. This section is purely for the reader’s convenience and she/he/they may skip it if desired. In Section
2
we discuss our main results and describe the structure theory of translation covariant states in terms of Banach bundles. In Section
3
, we study a version of the parameterized Hamiltonian introduced in
[
30
]
where the parameter is taken to be an IID random variable at each site. The resulting bulk state is shown to have an almost surely gapless parent Hamiltonian while simultaneously being described by our Banach bundle structure.

1
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some preliminary facts that we will use frequently throughout the rest of the exposition. Some of the results of this section are standard, while others are more specialized to our purposes. We aim to provide proofs when necessary, and otherwise we will provide detailed references for the reader for her/his/their convenience.

1.1
Probability Theory

We will denote by
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
a probability space with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
, and probability measure
\mathbb{P}
. Throughout, we work with the case that
\Omega
is a compact Hausdorff topological space, and we assume that
\mathcal{F}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by open sets. A map
T:\Omega\to\Omega
is
measure preserving
whenever
A\in\mathcal{F}
implies
\mathbb{P}(T^{-1}(A))=\mathbb{P}(A)
. The map
T
is called
ergodic
if
T^{-1}(A)\subset A
implies
\mathbb{P}(A)\in\{0,1\}
. In general, not every ergodic map has an ergodic inverse. However, in the case where
T
is a bijection where both
T
and
T^{-1}
are measure-preserving, then
T
is an
automorphism
of
\Omega
.

Remark 1.1
.

If
T
is an ergodic automorphism of
\Omega
, then
T^{-1}
is necessarily ergodic. This can be seen by noting that for any
A\in\mathcal{F}
with
T(A)\subset A
, the set
B:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}T^{-n}(A)
satisfies
T^{-1}(B)\subset B
and
\mathbb{P}[A]=\mathbb{P}[B]\in\{0,1\}
by continuity from below.

In this paper, we will focus on the case where
T
is a homeomorphism, that is,
T
is a continuous bijection with a continuous inverse. There are two standard examples of this case which motivate our study.

Example 1.2
.

Let
\Omega=[0,\pi)
the unit circle equipped with the normalized Lebesgue measure
\mathbb{P}=\frac{dx}{\pi}
. It is well known (see
[
24
, Example 6.1.3]
) that for any irrational number
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
, there is an associated measure preserving ergodic map
\vartheta:\omega\mapsto\omega+\alpha\mod{\pi}
which is a homeomorphism with an ergodic inverse.

Another standard example comes in the form of independent identically distributed random variables taking values in a compact Hausdorff space.

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a (not necessarily compact Hausdorff) probability space. Let
\{X_{z}:z\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a bi-infinite sequence of IID random variables on
\Omega
. Then, there exist a compact Hausdorff probability space
(S,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{Q})
and a family of random variables
\{Y_{z}:z\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and an ergodic automorphism
\vartheta\in\Aut(S)
so that the following hold

a.)

Each
\{Y_{z}:z\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are IID.

b.)

The automorphism
\vartheta
is a homeomorphism.

c.)

For all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
one has
Y_{z}=Y_{z-1}\circ\vartheta=Y_{z+1}\circ\vartheta^{-1}
.

d.)

The finite dimensional distributions agree

\mathbb{Q}[Y_{z_{1}}\leq x_{1},Y_{z_{2}}\leq x_{2},\dots,Y_{z_{n}}\leq x_{n}]=\mathbb{P}[X_{z_{1}}\leq x_{1},X_{z_{2}}\leq x_{2},\dots,X_{z_{n}}\leq x_{n}]

This proof is a standard application of Kolmogorov’s Extension Theorem.

Remark 1.4
.

In words, the previous lemma means that we can always find a model for a sequence of IID random variables in which the underlying probability space is a compact Hausdorff space with an ergodic homeomorphism.

A much more powerful result holds which characterizes measure-preserving transformations on the Lebesgue space
([0,1],dx)
in terms of ergodic homeomorphisms. Recall the following from
[
26
, Corollary 12.30]
or
[
70
, Section 4.4]
.

Theorem 1.5

(Jewett-Krieger Theorem)
.

If
T
is an ergodic, measure preserving transformation on a Lebesgue space
(X,\mathscr{B},\mu)
where
\mathscr{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mu
is a probability measure, then
(X,T,\mu)
is isomorphic (up to sets of measure zero) to an ergodic homeomorphism of the Cantor set.

The proof of this is deep and difficult, so we will not recount it here. However, in the context of our results, the above theorem says that one can interpret the measure theoretic data of a large array of probabilistic models in terms of topological dynamics, so that (in some sense) topological dynamics are an appropriate setting for the discussion of disorder.

1.2
Banach Bundles

In this section we will recall some facts about Banach Bundles which will be relevant to our quotient construction in section
1.2
below. More specifically, we find that if the fibers are finite dimensional, our family of quotient spaces forms a Banach bundle in Theorem
2.16
below. Thus it is necessary to recount the relevant details.

The theory of Banach bundles goes back to at least the 1960’s with
[
22
]
, and the book by Fell and Doran serves as an informative reference
[
32
]
. As there is some variance in the basic definitions of these objects, we point out that our working definition is that of Fell and Doran given in
[
32
, Definition 13.1]
(also written as (F)-Banach Bundles in
[
15
,
23
]
).

1.2.1
Definition and Properties

Let
X
be an Hausdorff topological space and let
B
be an Hausdorff space equipped with a continuous, open surjection
\pi:B\to X
. If
U\subset X
is open, the subset
B_{U}:=\pi^{-1}(U)
is the
restriction
of
B
. The pre-image
B_{x}:=\pi^{-1}(\{x\})
is called the
fiber
with base-point
x
. It is said that
B
is a
Banach Bundle
whenever each
B_{x}
is equipped with the structure of a complex Banach space and the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

The mapping
e\mapsto\|e\|
, the Banach space norm of
e
in
B_{\pi(e)}
is a continuous map
B\to[0,\infty)
.

(ii)

The binary operation
(e,f)\mapsto e+f
is a continuous map from
\Delta(B^{2}):=\{(e,f):\pi(e)=\pi(f)\}
to
B
, where the domain
\Delta(B^{2})
is taken with the subspace topology inherited from the product topology on
B^{2}
.

(iii)

For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, the mapping
m_{\lambda}:B\to B
via
e\mapsto\lambda e
is continuous.

(iv)

For every
x\in X
, the sets of the form

V_{U,\epsilon}:=\{e\in B\colon\pi(e)\in U,\|e\|<\epsilon\}\quad\text{so that }x\in U\subset X\text{ is open, and }\epsilon>0,

form a neighborhood basis around the origin
0_{x}
of the fiber
B_{x}
.

Remark 1.6
.

Item (iv) is equivalent to the following:

(iv)’ If
x\in X
and
(e_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\subset B
is a net so that
\pi(e_{j})\to x
and
\|e_{j}\|\to 0
, then
e_{j}\to 0_{x}
in
B
.

We recall that a
section
of
\pi
is a continuous function
f:X\to B
so that
\pi\circ f=\id_{X}
. A Banach bundle is said to
have enough sections
if for every
e\in B
, there is a section
f
so that
f(\pi(e))=e
. We denote the set of continuous sections by
\Gamma(B)
. Let us recall a fundamental result.

Theorem 1.7

(Theorem C.16
[
32
]
)
.

Let
B
be a Banach bundle over a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
. Then
B
has enough continuous sections.

When equipped with the uniform norm, the linear space
\Gamma
of continuous sections of
(B,\pi,X)
forms a Banach space equipped with a natural multiplication by the algebra of
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions
C(X)
. Moreover, if
f\in\Gamma(B)
and
\lambda\in C_{0}(X)
, one clearly has
\|\lambda f\|_{\Gamma(B)}\leq\|\lambda\|_{C(X)}\cdot\|f\|_{\Gamma(B)}
. This makes
\Gamma(B)
into a
Banach
C(X)
-module

[
23
, Chapter 2]
(see also
[
15
]
).

Lemma 1.8
.

Let
B
be a Banach bundle over a compact Hausdorff space
X
. Then, a net
(e_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
converges to a point
e
in the topology of
B
if and only if the net of base-points
\pi(e_{j})\to\pi(e)
in
X
and one following equivalent criteria are met

(i)

For every
f\in\Gamma(B)
one has

\|e_{j}-f(\pi(e_{j}))\|\to\|e-f(\pi(e))\|

in
\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

Let
W\subset\Gamma(B)
be a dense
C(X)
-submodule. For every
w\in W
, one has

\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|\to\|e-w(\pi(e))\|\,.

Proof.

(i)
: The forward direction follows from continuity. For the converse, note there is some
g\in\Gamma(B)
with
g(\pi(e))=e
. Now,
\|e-g(\pi(e))\|=0
, and by continuity, this means

\|e_{j}-g(\pi(e_{j}))\|\to 0.
By property (iv)’, this implies that
e_{j}-g(\pi(e_{j}))\to 0_{\pi(e)}
, from which we conclude that
\lim e_{j}=\lim g(\pi(e_{j}))=e
, since addition is continuous.

(iii)
: The only part which is nontrivial is the
(iii)\Rightarrow(ii)
direction. Let
\epsilon>0
and let
f\in\Gamma(B)
. By the assumption, we may find an approximant
w\in W
so that
\|f-w\|<\epsilon/3
. Then, for
j
large enough, one has
\left|\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|-\|e-w(\pi(e))\|\right|<\epsilon/3
.
By utilizing the reverse triangle inequality, the following chain of estimates holds:

\displaystyle\left|\|e_{j}-f(\pi(e_{j}))\|\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\|e-f(\pi(e))\|\right|\leq\left|\|e_{j}-f(\pi(e_{j}))\|-\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|\right|+\left|\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|-\|e-f(\pi(e_{j}))\|\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|w(\pi(e_{j}))-f(\pi(e_{j}))\|+\left|\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|-\|e-w(\pi(e))\|\right|+\|w(\pi(e))-f(\pi(e))\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|f-w\|+\left|\|e_{j}-w(\pi(e_{j}))\|-\|e-w(\pi(e))\|\right|<\epsilon\,.\qed

We will record a useful lemma. The original argument seems to go back to
[
22
]
, but we record the proof below for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
B
be a Banach bundle over
X
with a space of continuous sections
\Gamma
. Then, the set

X_{\geq n}:=\{x\colon:\dim B_{x}\geq n\}

is open for each
n\geq 0

Proof.

Suppose
x_{0}\in X_{n}
and find
s_{1},\dots s_{n}\in\Gamma
so that
\{s_{j}(x_{0})\}_{j=1}^{n}
is linearly independent in
B_{x_{0}}
. Consider the map

s(y,\vec{c}):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}\cdot s_{j}(y)

which is a jointly continuous map from
X\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to B
. Then, the function
\eta(y)=\inf_{\|\vec{c}\|=1}\|s(y,\vec{c})\|
is a continuous mapping from
X\to[0,\infty)
. Since the unit sphere of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is compact,
\eta(x_{0})>0
by linear independence. Therefore, there is a neighborhood
V\ni x_{0}
so that
\eta(y)>0
for all
y\in V
. Equivalently,
\{s_{j}(y)\}_{j=1}^{n}
are linearly independent in
B_{y}
. Thus
V\subset X_{n}
as required.
∎

In other words, the above lemma says that the dimension function is lower semi-continuous in
\omega
. The next theorem is a simplified version of the Stone-Weierstraß theorem for Banach bundles Theorem 4.2 in
[
34
]
.

Theorem 1.10
.

(Stone-Weierstrass)
Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space and let
B
be a Banach bundle over
X
. A
C(X)
-submodule
W
of the continuous sections
\Gamma(B)
is dense in
\Gamma(B)
if and only if for every
x\in X
, the set
\{s(x):s\in W\}\subset B_{x}
is dense.

Proof.

For the forward direction, note that since
X
is compact, it has enough sections
[
32
, Theorem C.11]
. So for any
e\in B_{x}
, there is a continuous section
s\in\Gamma(B)
so that
s(x)=e
. Therefore, for any
\epsilon>0
one may find
w\in W
so that
\|w(x)-s(x)\|\leq\|w-s\|<\epsilon
as required.

Conversely, pick
s\in\Gamma(B)
. Fix
x_{0}\in X
. Since
W
is fiber-wise dense, there exists a section
w_{x_{0}}\in W
so that
\|w_{0}(x_{0})-s(x_{0})\|<\epsilon
. Since the mapping
X\ni x\mapsto\|w(x)-s(x)\|
is continuous, there is a neighborhood
U_{x_{0}}\ni x_{0}
so that for all
y\in U_{x_{0}}
one has
\|w_{0}(y)-s(y)\|<\epsilon
.
Nota bene
that
\{U_{x}:x\in X\}
(constructed as above) forms an open cover of
X
from which we can extract a finite sub-cover
\{U_{x_{1}},U_{x_{2}},\dots,U_{x_{k}}\}
. Find a partition of unity
\{f_{1}:X\to[0,1]\colon 1\leq j\leq k\}\subset C(X)
subordinate to this sub-cover, and define
w=\sum_{1}^{k}f_{j}w_{x_{j}}\in W
since
W
is a
C(X)
-module.

Hence, for any
y\in X
,

\displaystyle\|w(y)-s(y)\|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}f_{j}(y)\|w_{j}(y)-s_{j}(y)\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{*}:\\
y\in U_{x_{j_{*}}}\end{subarray}}|f_{j_{*}}(y)|\|w_{j_{*}}(y)-s_{j_{*}}(y)\|<\epsilon\sum f_{j}(y)=\epsilon.

The claim follows.
∎

Moreover,
[
32
]
give a general means of constructing Banach bundles from families of Banach spaces.

Theorem 1.11

(Theorem 13.18
[
32
]
)
.

Let
X
be an Hausdorff space. Let
A
be a set equipped with a surjection
\varrho:A\to X
, so that
A_{x}:=\varrho^{-1}(\{x\})
is a complex Banach space. Suppose
\Gamma
is a complex linear space of sections of
\varrho
so that

(a)

for all
f\in\Gamma
, the mapping
x\mapsto\|f(x)\|_{A_{x}}
is continuous,

(b)

for all
x\in X
, the set
\{f(x)\}_{f\in\Gamma}\subset A_{x}
is dense.

Then, there exists a unique topology
\tau
making
A
a Banach bundle so that every element of
\Gamma
is a continuous section of
\varrho
. In particular, a topological basis for
\tau
is given by sets of the following form:

W(f,U,\epsilon)=\{a\in A\colon\varrho(a)\in U,\,\|a-f(\varrho(a))\|<\epsilon\}

(1.1)

where
\epsilon>0
,
U\subset X
is an open set, and
f\in\Gamma
.

We will sometimes refer to Theorem
1.11
as the Fell-Doran theorem for simplicity.

Recall that a Banach bundle is
locally trivial
if for every point
x\in X
, there exists a neighborhood
U\ni x
and a fixed Banach space
F
, so that the restriction
B_{U}
is homeomorphic to
F\times U
in the product topology. In contrast to the typical definition of a vector bundle, Banach bundles make
no assumptions
about local triviality, which leads to some interesting behavior.

Example 1.12
.

Let
\Omega=[0,1]
be the unit interval equipped with the usual topology. Define
V_{x}=\mathbb{C}
for all
x\in[0,1]\setminus\{1/2\}
and
V_{1/2}=\{0\}
. Let
\Gamma
be the subspace of
C([0,1])
with
f(1/2)=0
. Then by the Fell-Doran Theorem (Theorem
1.11
) there is a unique topology on
\mathcal{V}:=\{V_{x}\}_{x\in[0,1]}
making
(\mathcal{V},\pi,[0,1])
into a Banach bundle with
\Gamma
as a dense
C([0,1])
-submodule of the continuous sections by the Stone-Weierstraß theorem for Banach bundles.

1.2.2
Homomorphisms

We now recapitulate the theory of mappings between Banach bundles. It seems that the first formulation of notion of a
homomorphism of Banach bundles
is due to
[
35
]
, and these were used in
[
35
,
36
]
to investigate the notion of a
dual bundle
. In view of Theorem
1.11
, one might hypothesize that the natural definition of the ‘dual’ of a Banach bundle
X
would be to take the dual spaces of each fiber
\{X_{\omega}^{*}:\omega\in\Omega\}
together with the natural surjection, and topologize it via the homomorphisms into the trivial bundle which form a natural class of sections thereof. However, as is shown in Examples 2.13 of
[
36
]
, even in the case that the fibers of a given bundle are finite-dimensional, it is
not
the case that the point-wise norm of every homomorphism is continuous – a requirement of Theorem
1.11
.

In general, a drop in dimension allows for the operator norm of a linear functional to have a discontinuity in its base points. The issue of existence of a dual is actually quite subtle for this reason. However, for practical purposes it will suffice to study
homomorphisms
between bundles. In particular, we relax the requirement that the linear functionals form a bundle in and of themselves, to requiring that they are homomorphisms into the trivial bundle.

We introduce a slightly modified version of the definition of a homomorphism.

Definition 1.13
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach bundles over the same topological base space
\Omega
, with modules of sections
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
respectively. Suppose further that
\Omega
is equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega
. A function
H:\Omega\to\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}B(X_{\omega},Y_{\vartheta\omega})
via
\omega\mapsto H_{\omega}
is a (
\vartheta
-)
covariant homomorphism
of
X
to
Y
if the associated mapping of the bundles
H:X\to Y
via
e\mapsto H_{\pi(e)}e\in Y_{\vartheta\omega}
is continuous. If
\vartheta
is simply the identity map, then
H
is simply referred to as a
homomorphism
.

Notation 1.14
.

The
C(\Omega)
-module of covariant homomorphisms will be written
\hom^{\vartheta}(X,Y)
, with the
\vartheta
-dropped if we are referring to the identity map on
\Omega
. If the pointwise norm function
\omega\mapsto\|H_{\omega}\|_{B(X_{\omega},Y_{\vartheta\omega})}
is bounded, then we say
H
is a
bounded homomorphism
. The submodule of bounded homomorphisms will be written
\hom^{\vartheta}_{b}(X,Y)
. Note that every covariant isometric isomorphism of Banach bundles is a covariant homomorphism.

There is a natural action of the homomorphisms
\hom^{\vartheta}(X,Y)
on the Banach modules of continuous sections
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
, as we note in the following lemma.

Lemma 1.15
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
(X,\pi_{X},\Omega)
and
(Y,\pi_{Y},\Omega)
be two Banach bundles over
\Omega
with
C(\Omega)
-modules of sections
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
respectively. Let
H:\Omega\to\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}B(X_{\omega},Y_{\vartheta\omega})
be a
\vartheta
-covariant homomorphism. Then, for every section
\sigma\in\Gamma(X)
, the function
H\cdot\sigma:\Omega\to Y
via
(H\cdot\sigma)(\omega):=H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)\in Y_{\vartheta\omega}
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
(\omega_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\subset\Omega
be a net converging to
\omega
. Note that

\pi_{Y}(H_{\omega_{\lambda}}\sigma(\omega_{\lambda}))=\vartheta\circ\pi_{X}(\sigma(\omega_{\lambda}))=\vartheta\omega_{\lambda}

which converges to
\vartheta\omega=\pi(H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega))
. Now, let
f\in\Gamma(Y)
be a continuous section, and note that in this case, the net
f(\vartheta(\omega_{\lambda}))\to f(\vartheta(\omega))
, and similarly
\sigma(\omega_{\lambda})\to\sigma(\omega)
. Whence
\|H_{\omega_{\lambda}}\sigma(\omega_{\lambda})-f(\vartheta\omega)\|\to\|H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)-f(\vartheta\omega)\|
since
H
is continuous in the bundle topology. Thus,
H\cdot\sigma
is a continuous mapping into
Y
.
∎

The following lemma uses the central idea in the proof of
[
35
, Theorem 1.4.5]
to show that every covariant homomorphism of bundles over a compact Hausdorff space has a uniformly bounded point-wise norm.

Lemma 1.16
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
(X,\pi_{X},\Omega)
and
(Y,\pi_{Y},\Omega)
be two Banach bundles over
\Omega
with
C(\Omega)
-modules of sections
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
respectively. Let
H\in\hom^{\vartheta}(X,Y)
. Then,
H
is bounded, i.e.,

\|H\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\|H_{\omega}\|_{B(X_{\omega},Y_{\vartheta\omega})}<\infty.

Proof.

Recall that
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
form Banach spaces when endowed with the uniform norm. By Lemma
1.15
, the assignment
\omega\mapsto\|H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)\|
is continuous for every
\sigma\in\Gamma(X)
. Therefore,
\sup_{\omega}\|H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)\|:=K_{\sigma}<\infty
since
\Omega
is compact.

Setting
T_{\omega}:\Gamma\to\ell^{1}(\bigsqcup Y_{\omega})
, via
T_{\omega}\sigma:=H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)\in Y_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
, the above observation yields

\sup_{\omega}\|T_{\omega}\sigma\|_{Y_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}}=K_{\sigma}<\infty

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
. Therefore, by the uniform boundedness principle (
[
74
, Theorem 2.6]
),
M:=\sup_{\omega}\|T_{\omega}\|<\infty
. We are now ready to estimate
\|H_{\omega}e\|
for
e\in X_{\omega}
. Our aim is to express
\|H_{\omega}e\|
in terms of
T_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)
, for a suitable section
\sigma
. Recall that
\Omega
being compact implies
X
has enough sections: for all
e\in X_{\omega}
, there is some
\sigma_{e}\in\Gamma
with
\sigma(\omega)=e
. We need to modify
\sigma_{e}
a bit in order to control its norm.

Let
\epsilon>0
. By Urysohn’s Lemma (
[
56
, Theorem 33.1]
), there is a continuous function
f_{\epsilon}:\Omega\to[0,1]
so that for
A=\{\pi(e)\}
and
B=\{\omega:\|\sigma_{e}(\omega)\|\geq(1+\epsilon)\|e\|\}
one has
f(A)=\{1\}
and
f(B)=\{0\}
. Then, taking
\sigma_{\epsilon}=f\cdot\sigma_{e}
, one has
\|\sigma_{\epsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq(1+\epsilon)\|e\|
, and
\sigma_{\epsilon}(\pi(e))=e
. Then,

\|H_{\omega}e\|_{Y_{\vartheta\omega}}=\|H_{\omega}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\omega)\|=\|T_{\omega}\sigma_{e}\|\leq M\|\sigma_{e}\|_{\infty}\leq M(1+\epsilon)\|e\|

and we are done.
∎

The following is a specialization of
[
35
, Theorem 1.4.4]

Lemma 1.17
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
(X,\pi_{X},\Omega)
and
(Y,\pi_{Y},\Omega)
be two Banach bundles over
\Omega
with
C(\Omega)
-modules of sections
\Gamma(X)
and
\Gamma(Y)
respectively. Let
H:\Omega\to\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}B(X_{\omega},Y_{\vartheta\omega})
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The function
H
is a
\vartheta
-covariant homomorphism.

(ii)

For every section
\sigma\in\Gamma(X)
, the functions
H\cdot\sigma:\Omega\to Y
via
(H\cdot\sigma)(\omega):=H_{\omega}\sigma(\omega)\in Y_{\vartheta\omega}
are continuous.

(iii)

For every section
\sigma
in a dense
C(\Omega)
-submodule
W\leq\Gamma(X)
, the function
H\cdot\sigma
is a continuous.

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii):
This is Lemma
1.15
.

(ii)\Rightarrow(iii):
This is obvious.

(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
: Let
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\subset X
be a net converging to
e_{0}
in the bundle topology. Let
\omega_{\lambda}:=\pi(e_{\lambda})\to\omega_{0}:=\pi(e_{0})
be the associated net of base points which we know converges. Let
\epsilon>0
. Since
X
has enough continuous sections, we may find
\sigma_{0}\in\Gamma(X)
so that
\sigma_{0}(\omega_{0})=e
. Furthermore, as
W
is a dense sub-module of sections, we may find
w\in W
with
\|w-\sigma_{0}\|_{\infty}<\frac{\epsilon}{\|H\|_{\infty}3}
.

Now, let
f\in\Gamma(Y)
. Note that
\|f\circ\vartheta-H\cdot w\|
is a continuous function in
\omega
, and that moreover by the reverse triangle inequality,

\left|\|f(\vartheta\omega_{0})-H_{\omega_{0}}w(\omega_{0})\|-\|H_{\omega_{0}}e-f(\vartheta\omega_{0})\|\right|\leq\|H_{\omega_{0}}\|\|e-w(\omega_{0})\|=\|H_{\omega_{0}}\|\|\sigma_{0}(\omega_{0})-w(\omega_{0})\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\,.

Therefore, for any
\eta>0
there is
\lambda
large enough so that

\left|\|f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})-H_{\omega_{\lambda}}w(\omega_{\lambda})\|-\|H_{\omega_{0}}e-f(\vartheta\omega)\|\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3}+\eta\,.

(1.2)

The following bound holds by combining the triangle inequality and the reverse triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\left|\|H_{\omega_{\lambda}}e_{\lambda}-f(\vartheta\omega)\|-\|H_{\omega}e-f(\vartheta\omega)\|\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\|H_{\omega_{\lambda}}e_{\lambda}-f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})\|-\|f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})-H_{\omega_{\lambda}}\sigma_{0}(\omega_{\lambda})\|\right|

\displaystyle\text{\hskip 22.76219pt}+\left|\|f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})-H_{\omega_{\lambda}}\sigma_{0}(\omega_{\lambda})\|-\|f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})-H_{\omega_{\lambda}}w(\omega_{\lambda})\|\right|

\displaystyle\text{\hskip 22.76219pt}+\left|\|f(\vartheta\omega_{\lambda})-H_{\omega_{\lambda}}w(\omega_{\lambda})\|-\|H_{\omega}w(\omega)-f(\vartheta\omega)\|\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|H\|_{\infty}\left(\|e_{\lambda}-\sigma_{0}(\omega_{\lambda})\|+\|\sigma_{0}-w\|_{\infty}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{3}+\eta

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\eta\text{ for $\lambda$ large enough.}

Note we have used (
1.2
) to obtain the estimate on the third summand in the second inequality. Since
\eta
and
\epsilon
were chosen independently, this completes the proof.
∎

1.3
C^{*}
-algebras and Quasi-local Algebras

Let us recall here that a
C^{*}
-algebra,
\mathcal{A}
is a complete normed algebra with norm
\|\cdot\|
satisfying the
C^{*}
-condition
\|a^{*}a\|=\|a\|^{2}
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is unital if it possesses a unit element
\mathbbm{1}
, and we say that
\mathcal{A}
is separable if, with the topology generated by
\|\cdot\|
,
\mathcal{A}
contains a countable, dense subset.

In view of the following theorem, we never loose generality by assuming a
C^{*}
-algebra is contained in the bounded operators on a Hilbert space. This is particularly useful for some constructions, but we aim to present our results abstractly wherever possible.

Theorem 1.18

(Theorem 7.11
[
18
]
)
.

Every
C^{*}
-algebra has a faithful representation
\pi
into the bounded operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

In fact, more is true once one specifies a reference state.

Theorem 1.19

(Gelfand-Naimark-Segal Theorem)
.

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and
\psi
be a state on
A
. There exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a
*
-representation
\pi
, and a normalized cyclic vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
so that
\psi(a)=\langle\xi,\pi(a)\xi\rangle
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be two unital
C^{*}
-algebras acting on the Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
respectively. One may form their algebraic tensor product in the standard way:

\mathcal{A}\odot\mathcal{B}=\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\otimes b_{k}\colon n\text{ is finite}\right\}

(where the sums are taken modulo the usual bilinear relations). which is easily checked to be a
*
-subalgebra of
B(H\otimes K)
. We recall the minimal (a.k.a. spatial) tensor product of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}=\overline{\mathcal{A}\odot\mathcal{B}}^{\|\cdot\|}
. The norm of an arbitrary element
x\in\mathcal{A}\odot\mathcal{B}
is therefore given by

\|x\|_{\min}=\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\otimes b_{k}\right\|\,.

A fundamental result is that the value of this norm is independent of the choice of faithful representations of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}

[
12
, Proposition 3.3.11]
. In particular, the norm on the minimal tensor product is a (in fact, the smallest)
cross norm
in the sense that
\|a\otimes b\|_{\min}=\|a\|_{\mathcal{A}}\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}}
which also satisfies the
C^{*}
-property
[
81
, Theorem IV.4.19]
. Moreover,
A\otimes_{\min}B\cong B\otimes_{\min}A
.

Following the convention of Kadison and Ringrose
[
45
]
among others, we shall denote by
(\mathcal{A})_{\alpha}
the ball of radius
\alpha\geq 0
centered at the origin in
\mathcal{A}
. In particular,
(\mathcal{A})_{1}=\{a\in\mathcal{A}\colon\|a\|\leq 1\}
. Moreover, we will denote the positive semidefinite elements of a
C^{*}
-algebra as
(\mathcal{A})_{+}
.

1.3.1
Weakly*-Continuous State-valued Functions

Throughout,
\Omega
will be a compact Hausdorff space, and
\mathcal{A}
will be a unital
C^{*}
algebra. We will primarily be concerned with the set of continuous functions
A:\Omega\mapsto\mathcal{A}
which we shall write as
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
. As the later sections will deal with comparing functions and their evaluations, we shall endeavor to always write an element of
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
with capital Latin letters and elements of
\mathcal{A}
with lowercase Latin letters.

Lemma 1.20
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space, and let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra with a unit. Denote by
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
the set of continuous functions
F:\Omega\to A
. The following hold.

1.

If a function
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{A}
is continuous then the mapping
\omega\mapsto\|F(\omega)\|\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is continuous.

2.

Equipped with pointwise operations,
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
is a
C^{*}
-algebra under the norm

\|F\|:=\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\|F(\omega)\|

Moreover, the norm is always achieved at some
\omega_{0}
.

3.

The algebraic tensor product
C(\Omega)\odot A
can be isometrically identified with a dense
*
-subalgebra of
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
. In particular,
C(\Omega)\otimes_{\min}A\cong C(\Omega,A)
.

Sketch of the proof.

1. This follows from the reverse triangle inequality. 2. Is a standard exercise.

3. We refer the reader to
[
81
, Theorems IV.4.14 and IV.7.3]
for the details.
∎

Let
S(\mathcal{A})
denote the state space of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e., a linear functional
\psi\in S(\mathcal{A})
if and only if
\psi(\mathbbm{1})=1
and
\psi(a^{*}a)\geq 0
for all
a\in A
. We will be considering maps
\psi:\Omega\to S(\mathcal{A})
. Let us recall some basic information here.

Lemma 1.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
S(\mathcal{A})
denote its state space. Then,
S(\mathcal{A})
is a norm-closed convex subset of the unit ball of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. Moreover by an application of the Krein-Milman theorem,
S(\mathcal{A})
is the weak-* closure of the convex hull of its extreme points. In particular, when
\mathcal{A}
is separable,
S(\mathcal{A})
is a closed, compact metrizable space.

This information is standard and can be found in
[
17
,
81
]
among other sources. A nice fact we shall use with some frequency is the following:

Lemma 1.22
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space and suppose that
\omega\mapsto\psi_{\omega}
is a weakly*-continuous mapping into the state space
S(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the subspace topology induced by the norm on
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. Then, for any
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
, the map
\omega\mapsto\psi_{\omega}(A(\omega))\in\mathbb{C}
is continuous.

Sketch of the proof.

This follows from a straightforward
\epsilon/2
argument.
∎

1.3.2
Quasilocal Algebras and Quantum Spin Chains

Now, we recall the construction of the local and quasilocal algebras of observables. For a more detailed discussion of this construction, we refer the reader to Chapter 2 in
[
10
]
. Consider the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}
equipped its usual metric
d(n,m)=|n-m|
. To each site
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we associate a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{n}
which is an isomorphic copy of a fixed unital
C^{*}
-algebra,
\mathcal{A}
. I.e.
\mathcal{A}_{n}\cong\mathcal{A}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Given a finite subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
one forms the
local algebra (of observables)

\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}:=\bigotimes_{n\in\Lambda}\mathcal{A}_{n}\,

where the tensor product is the minimal tensor product. We will write
\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}
to indicate that
\Lambda
is a finite subset.

Now, given any two finite volumes
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda_{1}
, there is a canonical inclusion
\iota_{\Lambda_{0}\to\Lambda}:\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{1}}
given by
a\mapsto a\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\Lambda_{1}\setminus\Lambda_{0}}
where the latter factor indicates a
\mathbbm{1}
at any site in
\Lambda_{1}\setminus\Lambda_{0}
. In this way, one identifies
A_{\Lambda_{0}}
with its inclusion into
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{1}}
, and therefore, we may consider
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{0}}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{1}}
whenever
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda_{1}
. The family of local algebras
\{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}\colon\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}\text{ is finite}\}
is therefore partially ordered by inclusion. Thus, the union forms a
*
-algebra called the algebra of
local observables

\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}:=\bigcup_{\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}

which is equipped with a canonical
C^{*}
-semi norm
\|\cdot\|
given by the norms on the
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
’s. Completing
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
with respect to this seminorm forms the
algebra of quasilocal observables

\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}:=\overline{\bigcup_{\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}}^{\|\cdot\|}
. One may regard the quasilocal algebra as the inductive limit of a sequence of local algebras as well (see
[
57
, Chapter 6]
).

The following theorem was noted by Takeda in
[
80
]
(see also
[
77
]
).

Theorem 1.23

(Takeda’s Theorem)
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}
be the integer lattice and consider the set
\mathcal{P}
of all finite subsets
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
. Associate to each site a replica of the unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and form the local algebras
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}=\bigotimes_{x\in\Lambda}\mathcal{A}_{x}
, and quasilocal algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
as above. Suppose
\psi_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{S(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda})}
is a family of states satisfying the compatibility condition that whenever
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}
one has

\psi_{\Lambda}\circ\iota_{\Lambda_{0}\to\Lambda}=\psi_{\Lambda_{0}}

(1.3)

Then, there is a uniquely defined state
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
so that
\psi|_{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}}=\psi_{\Lambda}
for all
\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}
.

In particular, Takeda’s theorem provides a rigorous justification for the existence of a product state in the thermodynamic limit.

We introduce some further notation for the canonical translation symmetry on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\tau_{1}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote right translation which acts on pure tensors as follows. Given an interval
[n,n+m]\subset\mathbb{Z}
and a collection
a_{n},\ldots,a_{n+m}\in\mathcal{A}
, let
a_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+m}
denote the element of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
with the operator
a_{j}
at position
j
for each
j=n,\ldots,n+m
. Then

\tau_{1}(a_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+m})=b_{n+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{n+m+1}\,\forall n\in\mathbb{Z},\,m\in\mathbb{N},

where
b_{j}=a_{j-1}
for each
j=n+1,\ldots,n+m+1
. This definition is clearly consistent with the inclusion mappings in the sense that
\tau_{1}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{0}})=\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{0}+1}
whenever
\Lambda_{0}\Subset\mathbb{Z}
. Note
\tau_{k}:=(\tau_{1})^{k}
extends to an automorphism of
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

In
[
30
]
, the authors study the structure of states which are
\tau
-invariant by examining a specific quotient of the quasilocal algebra. Our aim is now to give a similar structure theorem in the case that
\psi
is a weakly* continuous function of states which co-varies with respect to
\tau
. This leads us to the following section.

2
Structure of Driven Translation Covariant States

In this section, we state and prove our main structure theorem, Theorem
2.34
, for the following class of states.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with a homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
\omega\mapsto\psi_{\omega}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
be a weakly*-continuous function. We say that
\psi
is a
translation covariant state
(TCVS) if the following holds

\psi_{\omega}\circ\tau_{k}=\psi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z};\,\omega\in\Omega\,.

(2.1)

If additionally
\vartheta
is an ergodic automorphism of
\Omega
with respect to a given Radon probability measure on
\Omega
, then we say
\psi
is an
ergodic TCVS
.

Speaking colloquially, Theorem
2.34
below generalizes Proposition 2.1 of
[
30
]
, showing that (in a sense we shall make rigorous) any translation covariant state factors into iterations of a composition of
random linear maps
as in

\psi_{\omega}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\varrho_{\omega}\circ E_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots E_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}(e_{\vartheta^{n+1}\omega})

where the
E_{a,\omega}
’s are linear maps in an appropriate spaces
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{+}
and
e_{\omega}
is a random element of this space, and
\varrho_{\omega}
is a random linear functional in
(\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{+})^{*}
.

2.1
Setup and Basic Properties

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and consider the associated spin chain algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
equipped with the group action of translation
\mathbb{Z}\ni k\mapsto\tau_{k}\in\Aut(\mathcal{A}_{Z})
as in Section
1.3
. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with a Radon probability measure
\mathbb{P}
and an ergodic measure preserving homeomorphism
\vartheta
. As we will be considering mappings from
\Omega\to\mathcal{A}
, we recall our convention that elements of
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
are written with capital Latin letters and elements of
\mathcal{A}
with lower case Latin letters, the exception being the unit, which we write as
\mathbbm{1}
.

Let us record an interesting consequence of Takeda’s theorem: the existence of a co-variant product state.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an (ergodic) homeomorphism
\vartheta
, and suppose that
\omega\mapsto\eta_{\omega}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})
is a norm-continuous mapping. Form
\xi^{\Lambda}_{\omega}:=\bigotimes_{x\in\Lambda}\eta_{\vartheta^{x}\omega}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda})
for all
\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}
. Then, there is a unique state
\xi_{\omega}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
that is weakly*-continuous in
\omega
, translation co-variant,

\xi_{\omega}(\tau_{1}(a))=\xi_{\vartheta\omega}(a)

and has the property that for any
X,Y\Subset\mathbb{Z}
with
X\cap Y=\varnothing
, one has

\xi_{\omega}(a_{X}b_{Y})=\xi_{\omega}(a_{X})\xi_{\omega}(b_{Y}),

(2.2)

for all
a_{X}\in\mathcal{A}_{X}
and all
B_{Y}\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}
.

Definition 2.3
.

We call the state
\xi_{\omega}
defined as in Proposition
2.2
a
translation covariant product state
.

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

For fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, we observe that
\xi_{\omega}
exists and is a state on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
by Takeda’s theorem. We now show the translation co-variance and factor properties.

Let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
and write
X=\supp(a)
. By linearity, we may assume
a=\bigotimes_{x\in X}a_{x}
is a pure tensor. Now,

\xi_{\omega}\circ\tau_{k}(a)=\xi^{X+k}_{\omega}(a)=\prod_{x\in X}\eta_{\vartheta^{x+k}\omega}(a_{x+k})=\prod_{x\in X}\eta_{\vartheta^{x}\vartheta^{k}\omega}(a_{x})=\xi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}(a),

with the second to last equality following since the individual operators
a_{x}
do not vary with their subscript. Therefore
\xi_{\omega}
has the co-variance property on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
, and this extends to all
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
by density and the fact that
\xi_{\omega}
is a state. The factor property (
2.2
) follows similarly.

We need now show
\omega\mapsto\xi_{\omega}
is weak-* continuous. Let
a
be a nonzero pure tensor as above with (finite) support
X
. Let
X=\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
be an enumeration of the sites in
X
so that
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{n}
. Observe the following estimate:

\displaystyle|\xi_{\omega}(a)-\xi_{\omega^{\prime}}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n}|\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{0}}\omega}(a_{x_{0}})\cdots\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{j-1}}\omega}(a_{x_{j-1}})\{\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{j}}\omega}(a_{x_{j}})-\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{j}}\omega^{\prime}}(a_{x_{j}})\}\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{j+1}}\omega^{\prime}}(a_{x_{j+1}})\cdots\eta_{\vartheta^{x_{n}}\omega^{\prime}}(a_{x_{n}})|

\displaystyle\leq\|a\|\sum_{x\in X}\|a_{x}\|^{-1}|\eta_{\vartheta^{x}\omega}(a_{x})-\eta_{\vartheta^{x}\omega^{\prime}}(a_{x})|

By linearity, a similar estimate holds for arbitrary elements of
\mathcal{A}_{X}
. Therefore,
\xi_{\omega}
is weakly*-continuous on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
, whence we conclude
\xi_{\omega}
is weakly* continuous on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
by density.
∎

In view of Lemma
1.20
, if
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is some
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we may regard the continuous functions into
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as a subalgebra of
C(\Omega,\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
, namely by identifying
C(\Omega)\otimes_{\min}\mathcal{A}_{0}
with the appropriate subalgebra in
C(\Omega,\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
. With this in mind, it will be convenient for us to introduce the notion of a
cut site
as follows: let
x\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
be a half-integer and let
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}=\mathcal{A}_{[x,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Z}}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
associated to making a cut at
x
and including all sites greater than
x
. Similarly, let
\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}=\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,x)\cap\mathbb{Z}}
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{Z}
associated to sites less than
x
. For example,
\mathcal{A}_{1/2}^{+}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{-}_{1/2}=\mathcal{A}_{-\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}
. Below we will introduce a similar equivalence relation to the one defined in
[
30
]
. We will focus on the relation with respect to
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
, but one can define analogous relations for
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
.

Now, let
\psi
be a translation co-variant state as above, and consider the continuous functions
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})
for a fixed
x
. Declare two functions
A,B\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})
equivalent, written
A\sim_{x}B
, if and only if for all
\omega\in\Omega
one has

\psi_{\omega}(C^{-}(\omega)\otimes(A(\omega)-B(\omega)))=0,\,\,\forall C^{-}\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})\,.

(2.3)

A priori
, these equivalence classes of functions depend on
x
. We shall write
[A]^{+}_{x}
for the equivalence class of
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})
. Furthermore, for any function
C^{-}\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})
, we note that the set
\mathfrak{G}_{C^{-}}:=\{F\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})\colon\psi(C\otimes F)=0\}
is a closed subspace of
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})
. This follows from the fact that
C^{-}\otimes f
is a continuous mapping into
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and the images of
\psi
are bounded linear operators. Hence, the intersection

I_{x}:=\bigcap_{C\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})}\mathfrak{G}_{C}

(2.4)

is also a closed subspace. Let us write
\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x}:=C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})/I_{x}
which is a Banach space.

Note that we can take a point-wise approach as well. Given
a,b\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}
, we define the equivalence relation
a\sim_{x,\omega}b
if and only if
\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes(a-b))=0
for all
c\in\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}
. By essentially the same argument as above, the set
\mathfrak{g}_{c,\omega}=\{a\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}\colon\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)=0\}
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}
and so is the common intersection

I_{x,\omega}:=\bigcap_{c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{c,\omega}\,.

(2.5)

Form the vector space
\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x,\omega}=\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}/I_{x,\omega}
which we note is a Banach space as well.

Notation 2.4
.

For an equivalence class in
\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x,\omega}
, as constructed above, we shall write
[a,\omega]_{x}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For every
\omega\in\Omega
, the dimension of
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is at least one.

Proof.

Suppose there is an
\omega
for which
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is zero. Then, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
, one has
\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)=0
. In particular this means
\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}})=0
, which means
\psi_{\omega}
is the zero linear functional, a contradiction.
∎

The following lemma justifies this choice of notation.

Lemma 2.6
.

For every cut site
x
, and for every
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a contractive linear map
p_{x,\omega}
making the following diagram commute:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x,\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim_{x,\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{x,\omega}}

where the vertical arrows denote the quotient maps.

In this way, we can view
p_{x,\omega}
as “evaluation at
\omega
” for the equivalence class
[A]_{x}^{+}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Let us make a preliminary observation. With notation as above, we have
\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})\subset I_{x,\omega}
.
Indeed, let
a\in\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})
. Then there is some
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})
so that
A(\omega)=a
and for all
C\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})
, we have the functional identity
\psi(C\otimes A)\equiv 0
. Since
\pi_{\omega}:C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})\to\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}
is a surjection, we note that for any choice of
c\in\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}
, there is a continuous
\tilde{C}:\Omega\to\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}
so that
C(\omega)=c
. Therefore
a fortiori

\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)=\psi_{\omega}(\tilde{C}(\omega)\otimes A(\omega))=0\,\forall c\in\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x}\,.

With this in hand, define
p_{x,\omega}([A]_{x}^{+})=[A(\omega)]_{x,\omega}^{+}
, which is well defined by the inclusion
\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})\subset I_{x,\omega}
.

Now, to see that
p_{x,\omega}
is contractive, note the following

\displaystyle\|p_{x,\omega}[A]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x,\omega}}=\inf_{b\in I_{x,\omega}}\|\pi_{\omega}(A)+b\|_{\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{b\in\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})}\|\pi_{\omega}(A)+b\|_{\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}}=\inf_{B\in I_{x}}\|\pi_{\omega}(A+B)\|_{\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{B\in I_{x}}\|A+B\|_{C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})}=\|[A]^{+}_{x}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x}}

where the first inequality follows from our observation that
\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})\subset I_{x,\omega}.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

Note that one should expect
I_{x,\omega}\supsetneq\pi_{\omega}(I_{x})
, in general. Indeed, given
a\in I_{x,\omega}
, it is not clear how to construct a continuous function
A(\omega)
so that
A(\omega)=a
, and
\psi_{\omega}(C(\omega)\otimes A(\omega))=0
for all
\omega
and
C\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})
. One compelling example where this
is
possible is if the singleton
\{\omega\}
were a connected component of
\Omega
in which case the simple function
A(\omega^{\prime}):=a\chi_{\{\omega\}}(\omega^{\prime})
satisfies the required property.

We will now turn to a more detailed discussion of the subspaces
I_{x,\omega}
. For any
\omega\in\Omega
, one can view the
I_{x,\omega}
as the intersection over the kernels of a family of seminorms given by

\begin{split}\rho_{\omega,c}&:\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\\
&\,a\mapsto|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|,\end{split}

(2.6)

so that
\mathfrak{g}_{c}=\rho_{\omega,c}^{-1}\{0\}.
It is not difficult to see that the
\rho_{\omega,c}
are linearly homogeneous in the sense that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, one has

|\lambda|\rho_{\omega,c}=\rho_{\omega,\lambda c}

for any
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
. Thus, we obtain the equalities

I_{x,\omega}=\bigcap_{c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{c}=\bigcap_{c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}}\mathfrak{g}_{c}

(2.7)

One can similarly form a class of seminorms on
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
via
\sigma_{\omega,a}=|\psi_{\omega}(\,\cdot\,\otimes a)|
. By similar arguments one can form
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}
as the quotient of
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
by the intersection
J_{x,\omega}:=\bigcap_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}\sigma_{\omega,a}^{-1}(\{0\})\,.
An analogue of equation (
2.6
) holds in this context as well.
Before stating the next result, let us record an elementary fact below for convenience.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space and let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space. If
\mathcal{F}\subset C(\Omega,\mathfrak{X})
is a uniformly bounded, equicontinuous family of functions, then the assignment
\omega\mapsto\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f(\omega)\|
is continuous.

We leave the proof as an exercise to the reader.

With Lemma
2.8
in hand, we aim to show the following.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
be a cut site and fix
\omega\in\Omega
. Let
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
via

\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}(a)=\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})_{1}}\rho_{\omega,c}(a)=\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})_{1}}|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|

Then,
\mathfrak{n}
is a subadditive, linearly homogeneous, positive semi-definite function which is constant on equivalence classes modulo
I_{x,\omega}
. Thus
\mathfrak{n}
descends to a norm on
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
. This norm,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}
, is majorized by the quotient norm. Moreover, for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
, the mapping
\omega\mapsto\|[a,\omega]_{x}\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}
is continuous in
\omega
.

Proof.

Note that the seminorms defined in (
2.6
) are constant on the equivalence classes in
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
. Indeed, fix
\omega\in\Omega
and suppose
a\sim_{x,\omega}a^{\prime}
and observe that for any
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}

|\rho_{\omega,c}(a)-\rho_{\omega,c}(a^{\prime})|\leq|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a^{\prime})|=|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes(a-a^{\prime}))|=0

(2.8)

since
a-a^{\prime}\in I_{x,\omega}
. It follows easily that
\mathfrak{n}(a)=\mathfrak{n}(a+b)
for any
b\in I_{x,\omega}
. Suppose now that
\mathfrak{n}(a)=0
. This means that
|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|=0
for all
c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}
. Thus, by linear homogeniety of the seminorms
\rho_{\omega,c}
, we obtain
a\sim_{x,\omega}0
. Lastly, note that for any
b\in I_{x,\omega}
, and any
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
,

\rho_{c}(a)=\rho_{c}(a+b)=|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes(a+b))|\leq\|c\|\|a+b\|\,.

Therefore,
\|[a]_{x,\omega}\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}\leq\|[a]_{x,\omega}^{+}\|_{x,\omega}
by definition of the quotient norm as an infimum.

Notice that for all
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
and all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
the following inequality holds

|\rho_{\omega,c}(a)-\rho_{\omega^{\prime},c}(a)|\leq|(\psi_{\omega}-\psi_{\omega^{\prime}})(c\otimes a)|\leq 2\|c\|\|a\|\,.

(2.9)

We aim to show that for fixed
a
, the family

\mathscr{F}_{a}:=\{g_{c}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\text{ via }g_{c}(\omega)=|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|\colon c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}\}

is equicontinuous.

To this end, let
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
and
\epsilon>0
. Recall that for any Hermitian
h\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
, one has
-\|h\|\mathbbm{1}\leq h\leq\|h\|\mathbbm{1}
, since
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Now, note that for any
a\in(\mathcal{A}_{x})^{+}
, the linear functional
\psi_{\omega}((\,\cdot\,)\otimes a^{*}a)
is positive on
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
. Therefore, for any Hermitian
h\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{s.a.}
, one has

-\|h\|\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a^{*}a)\leq\psi_{\omega}(h\otimes a^{*}a)\leq\|h\|\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a^{*}a)

Therefore, whenever
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
and
h\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{s.a.}
, one has

\displaystyle|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(h\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(h\otimes a)|\leq\|h\||(\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}-\psi_{\omega})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)|

In general, if
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
is nonzero, we have that

\displaystyle|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(c\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|

\displaystyle\leq|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(\Re(c)\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(\Re(c)\otimes a)|+|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(\Im(c)\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(\Im(c)\otimes a)|

\displaystyle\leq(\|\Re(c)\|+\|\Im(c)\|)|(\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}-\psi_{\omega})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\|c\|\,\cdot|(\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}-\psi_{\omega})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)|\,.

Lastly, recall that any
a\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})
can be decomposed into a sum of positive operators:
a=a_{1}-a_{2}+ia_{3}-ia_{4}
which once again is a general fact about functional calculus in
C^{*}
-algebras (See
[
57
]
or
[
10
, Section 2.2]
).

Finally, one has the following estimate for any
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
and any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}

|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(c\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(c\otimes a_{j})-\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a_{j})|\leq 2\|c\|\sum_{j=1}^{4}|(\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}-\psi_{\omega})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a_{j})|\,.

(2.10)

Now, since
\psi_{\omega}
is weakly*-continuous, each
|(\psi_{\omega})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a_{j})|
is a continuous function
\Omega\mapsto[0,\infty)
. Therefore, we can choose neighborhoods
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},U_{4}\subset\Omega
(all of which contain
\omega_{0}
), so that

|(\psi_{\omega}-\psi_{\omega_{0}})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a_{j})|<\frac{\epsilon}{8},\quad\text{for all $\omega\in V:=U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3}\cap U_{4}$.}

Hence, for all
\omega\in V
, we have

|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)-\psi_{\omega_{0}}(c\otimes a)|<\epsilon\|c\|\,.

Therefore, since the choices of
U_{j}
did not depend on
c
at all, it follows that
\mathscr{F}_{a}
forms a bounded family of functions that is equicontinuous at
\omega_{0}
, thus equicontinuous everywhere since
\omega_{0}
was arbitrary. Hence,

\|[a]_{x,\omega}\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}=\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}}\rho_{\omega,c}(a)=\sup_{g\in\mathscr{F}_{a}}g(\omega),

is continuous as a function of
\omega
by Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

An analogous argument to the inequality in (
2.8
) shows that the
\sigma_{\omega,a}
are constant on equivalence classes in
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
.

Remark 2.11
.

Let
\eta_{\omega}:\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}\times\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
via
\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}):=\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)
. This is a well-defined, nondegenerate, bilinear form by construction. Indeed, if
\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a,\omega]^{+}_{x})=0
for all
a\in\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
, then,
\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)=0
for all
a
, thus,
c\sim_{x,\omega}0\in\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}
. One also notes that since
\psi_{\omega}
is constant on equivalence classes in
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}
and
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
in the first and second coordinates respectively, the following holds:

|\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a,\omega]_{x}^{+})|=|\eta_{\omega}([c+d,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a+b,\omega]_{x}^{+})|\leq\|c+d\|\|a+b\|

for all
d\in J_{x,\omega}
and
b\in I_{x,\omega}
. Hence

|\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a,\omega]_{x}^{+})|\leq\|[c,\omega]_{x}^{-}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}}\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}\,.

We note that
\eta_{\omega}([\mathbbm{1},\omega]_{x}^{-},[\mathbbm{1},\omega]_{x}^{+})\equiv 1
, so we conclude that
\|\eta_{\omega}\|=1
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
x
be a cut site and let
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be as in Lemma
2.9
. Whenever
\omega
is such that
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional, we have equality of the
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}
and quotient norms:
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}
. In particular, suppose that there is an open set
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
so that for all
\omega_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
, the quotient space
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega_{0}}^{+}
is finite dimensional. Then, for all
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, the mapping
\Omega_{0}\ni\omega\mapsto\|[a,\omega_{0}]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega_{0},x}^{+}}
is continuous for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
.

Proof.

Consider the bilinear form
\eta_{\omega}:\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}\times\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
defined via
\eta_{\omega}([a,\omega]_{x}^{-},[c,\omega]_{x}^{+})=\psi_{\omega}(a\otimes c)
. Notice
\eta_{\omega}
is nondegenerate for all
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
by Remark
2.11
. Hence,
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}
is finite dimensional and canonically isomorphic to the dual
(\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+})^{*}
for all
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
.

Now, let
[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
. By basic duality theory, we know there is some linear functional
\ell\in((\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+})^{*})_{1}
, the unit ball, so that
\ell([a,\omega]_{x}^{+})=\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}
. Therefore, since
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}\cong(\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+})^{*}
, there is some
[c,\omega]_{x}^{-}\in\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}
with
\|[c,\omega]_{x}^{-}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{-}}\leq 1
so that
\ell=\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},\,\cdot\,)
. Thus,

\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}=|\ell([a,\omega]_{x}^{+})|=|\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega]_{x}^{-},[a,\omega]_{x}^{+})|=|\rho_{\omega,c}(a)|\leq\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}\,,

as claimed. Thus, we conclude that
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega_{0}}^{+}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{n}(\omega)}
in
\Omega_{0}
. If the second hypothesis holds, continuity of the mapping
\omega\mapsto\|[a]_{x,\omega}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}
in
\Omega_{0}
is a consequence of the equality established above and the fact that
\omega\mapsto\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}
is continuous in
\Omega_{0}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
.
∎

Some remarks are in order.

Remark 2.13
.

The key step in order to conclude
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}}
was that
\ell
is of a specific form, namely, that there exists some
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
implementing
\ell
via
\psi_{\omega}
. We used the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional to conclude this via the canonical isomorphism. It may not be true in general that such an
\ell
obtained by, e.g., the Hahn-Banach theorem can be implemented by such an element of
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
.

Remark 2.14
.

In particular, when
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional for all
\omega
, this means that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
the assignment
\omega\mapsto\|[a,\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}
is continuous in
\omega
by Lemma
2.9
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Suppose there is an open set
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
so that for every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, the quotient
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional. In this case, given any
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})
, the numerical mapping given by

\Omega_{0}\ni\omega\mapsto\|[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{B_{x,\omega}}

is continuous.

Proof.
