Again our strategy is to prove that a certain family of functions forms a bounded, equicontinuous family. Let
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
and note for all
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
and all
\omega\in\Omega
one has

\displaystyle|\rho_{c,\omega}(A(\omega))-\rho_{c,\omega_{0}}(A(\omega_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes A(\omega))-\psi_{\omega_{0}}(c\otimes A(\omega_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq\|c\|\|A(\omega)-A(\omega_{0})\|+|(\psi_{\omega}-\psi_{\omega_{0}})(c\otimes A(\omega_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq\|c\|\|A(\omega)-A(\omega_{0})\|+\|c\|\sum_{1}^{4}|(\psi_{\omega}-\psi_{\omega_{0}})\left[\mathbbm{1}\otimes A_{j}(\omega_{0})\right]|

by the same argument preceding equation (
2.10
), where we have written
A_{(}\omega_{0})=A_{1}(\omega_{0})-A_{2}(\omega_{0})+iA_{3}(\omega_{0})-iA_{4}(\omega_{0})
. Therefore, by a similar argument to the one proceeding equation (
2.10
), the family
\mathcal{F}_{A}:=\{f_{c}(\omega)=|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes A(\omega))|\colon c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}\}
is equicontinuous, and therefore so is their supremum by Lemma
2.8
. By Proposition
2.12
, we have

\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-})_{1}}\rho_{\omega,c}(A(\omega))=\|[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathfrak{n}(\omega)}=\|[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}}

for all
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, and this assignment is continuous in
\omega
there, by equicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}_{A}
.

∎

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
x
be a cut site and suppose that there is an open subset
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
for which every
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite-dimensional whenever
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
. Let
\mathbb{A}=\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega_{0}}\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
equipped with the surjection
\varrho(\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+})=\omega
. Then, taking
\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}
as the space of cross-sections,
(\mathbb{A},\varrho)
may be furnished with a unique topology carrying the structure of a Banach bundle over
\Omega_{0}
.

Proof.

Lemma
2.15
demonstrates that
\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}
fulfills the first requirement of Theorem
1.11
. For the next requirement, observe that
\{[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}^{+}\colon A\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})\}
is indeed dense in
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}
since the constant functions are continuous maps into
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
.
∎

Notation 2.17
.

In the case that
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional for all
\omega\in\Omega
, the bundle resulting from Theorem
2.16
will be written
\mathscr{B}_{x}=(\{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}\}_{\omega},\pi^{x},\Omega)
.

We now turn to a discussion of the action of the translation automorphism
\tau_{1}
and of the Koopman operator
K_{\vartheta}
on
\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}
and
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
. As we shall see later, the canonical map associated to
\tau_{1}
is linked with the canonical map associated to the Koopman operator
K_{\vartheta}
given by pre-composition with the measure-preserving ergodic map
\vartheta
.

First, note that for any cut site
x
, the automorphism
\tau_{1}
restricts to an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
onto
\mathcal{A}_{x+1}^{+}
. One can canonically extend the action of
\tau_{1}
to the continuous functions via post-composition of the image. The resulting map is easily shown to be an isometry and in particular, repeating the procedure for
\tau_{-1}
shows these maps to be bounded-ly invertible. Similarly, by defining
K_{\vartheta}:C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})\to C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})
via
K_{\vartheta}:=A\circ\vartheta
, one obtains a bounded-ly invertible isometry
[
26
, Section 4.5]
. Let us record this as a lemma.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\tau_{1}
denote the automorphism of translation on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and let
\vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega
denote a continuous measure preserving ergodic map. Let
x
be a cut site. The following hold:

1.

Define
T_{x}:C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x})\to C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{+}_{x+1})
via
T_{x}(A)(\omega):=\tau_{1}(A(\omega))
. Then,
T_{x}
defines a linear isometry with bounded inverse.

2.

Define
K_{x}:C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})\to C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})
via
K_{x}A:=A\circ\vartheta
. The
K_{x}
is a boundedly invertible linear isometry of
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})

3.

The following commutation relation holds

K_{x+1}T_{x}=T_{x}K_{x}

Proof.

To see the commutation relation, let
\omega\in\Omega
and observe that

(T_{x}K_{x}A)(\omega)=\tau_{1}(A(\vartheta\omega))=K_{x+1}(\tau_{1}(A(\omega))=(K_{x+1}T_{x}A)(\omega)\,.\qed

Remark 2.19
.

Since
T_{x}
and
K_{x}
are both instances of the restriction of a linear map on
C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{Z})
to a specific subalgebra, we will slightly abuse notation and drop the subscripts when no confusion can arise.

Now, we shall study the action of
T
and
K
under the quotient.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
x
be a cut site. The following hold:

1.

For each
\omega
, one has a canonical isometric isomorphism between
\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x,\vartheta(\omega)}
and
\mathcal{B}^{+}_{x+1,\omega}
given by the map
\tilde{T}_{x,\theta\omega}:[a,\vartheta\omega]_{x}^{+}\mapsto[\tau_{1}(a),\omega]_{x+1}^{+}
.

2.

Let
T
and
K
be as above. There exists an isometric isomorphism
V_{x}:\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}\to\mathcal{B}_{x+1}^{+}
so that the following diagrams commute
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{x,\vartheta\omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x+1}^{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{x+1}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{x+1,\omega}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{x,\vartheta\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{T}_{x,\theta\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{x+1,\omega}}

Proof.

1. Define the linear map
\tilde{T}_{x,\vartheta\omega}[a,\vartheta\omega]_{x}^{+}=[\tau_{1}(a),\omega]_{x+1}^{+}
. To check that this is well defined, suppose
[a,\vartheta\omega]_{x}^{+}=0
. Then, for all
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
, we have that

0=\psi_{\vartheta\omega}(c\otimes a)=\psi_{\omega}(\tau_{1}(c\otimes a))=\psi_{\omega}(\tau_{1}(c)\otimes\tau_{1}(A))\,.

(2.11)

Since
\tau
is an automorphism, in particular we have the above holding for all
d\in\mathcal{A}_{x+1}^{-}
. Hence
[\tau_{1}(a)]_{x+1,\omega}=0
as required.

It is not hard to argue that equation (
2.11
) implies
I_{x+1,\omega}=\tau_{1}(I_{x,\vartheta\omega})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{T}_{x,\vartheta\omega}[a,\vartheta\omega]_{x}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x+1,\omega}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{b\in I_{x+1,\omega}}\|\tau_{1}(a)+b\|_{\mathcal{A}_{x+1}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau_{-1}(b)\in I_{x,\vartheta\omega}}\|a+\tau_{-1}(b)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{x}}=\|[a,\vartheta\omega]_{x}^{+}\|

Thus,
\tilde{T}_{x,\vartheta\omega}
is an isometry. One can run this same argument with the canonical map associated to
\tau_{-1}
to see that
\tilde{T}_{x,\vartheta\omega}
has a bounded inverse.

2. Let
V_{x}[A]_{x}=[KTA]_{x+1}
. To see this is well defined, let
[A]_{x}^{+}=0
. For any
C^{-}_{x+1}\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x+1}^{-})
and for all
\omega\in\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(C^{-}_{x+1}(\omega)\otimes KTA(\omega))

\displaystyle=\psi_{\omega}(C^{-}_{x+1}(\omega)\otimes\tau_{1}(A(\vartheta\omega)))=\psi_{\omega}\left(\tau_{1}\left(\tau_{-1}(C_{x+1}^{-}(\omega))\otimes A(\vartheta\omega)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\vartheta\omega}\left(\left(\tau_{-1}(C_{x+1}^{-}(\omega))\otimes A(\vartheta\omega)\right)\right)=\psi_{\omega^{\prime}}(D(\omega^{\prime})\otimes A(\omega^{\prime}))=0,

where we have set
\omega^{\prime}=\vartheta\omega
, and
D(\omega)=T^{-1}K^{-1}C^{-}_{x+1}(\omega)\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}^{-}_{x})
. Notice that the final equality in the above holds for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
, and since
\vartheta
is a homeomorphism, we conclude that
[KTA]_{x+1}^{+}=0
as required. Equivalently,
I_{x}\supset KTI_{x+1}.
Using essentially the same argument as in Lemma
2.6
, we conclude that
V_{x}
is bounded. Running this same argument with
K^{-1}T^{-1}
, we see that in fact,
V_{x}
has a bounded inverse as well.

Now, let
[A]_{x}^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}
. We have

\tilde{T}_{x,\vartheta\omega}p_{x,\vartheta\omega}[A]_{x}^{+}=[\tau_{1}(A(\vartheta\omega)]_{x+1,\omega}^{+}=p_{x+1,\omega}[KTA]_{x+1}^{+}=p_{x+1,\omega}V_{x}[A]_{x}^{+},

as required.
∎

Corollary 2.21
.

If
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional for all
\omega\in\Omega
, then every
\mathcal{B}_{x+1,\omega}^{+}
is also finite dimensional.

Remark 2.22
.

A natural question is whether or not our construction of a Banach bundle out of the quotient spaces
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
will hold if we relax the condition that
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
be finite dimensional. This is not clear since Lemma
2.9
requires us to produce a linear functional
\ell
of a specific form, namely,
\ell=\eta_{\omega}([c,\omega],\cdot)
in order to show that the
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}
norm equals the quotient norm. In infinite dimensions, the Hahn-Banach theorem could be used here, but it will not guarantee that
\ell
is of the correct form, and it is not clear to us at this time how to verify continuity in
\omega
of the norms
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
which is required for the Fell-Doran theorem.

However, there is a remarkably similar construction in the functional analysis literature in
[
73
]
. A careful review of this paper shows that if we relax the finite-dimensionality requirement, then
\{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\colon\omega\in\Omega\}
can be topologized to form a continuous normed quotient vector bundle. This is more generality than we need, so we will tend to other results.

2.2
The Fundamental Theorem

Let us recall the notation and set-up of the previous section. Fix a cut site
x\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
, and consider the family of Banach spaces
\{\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}\colon\omega\in\Omega\}
defined fiber-wise by the equivalence relation
a\sim_{\omega}a
if and only if
\psi(X\otimes(a-b))=0
for all
X\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
. Consider also the family of selections
\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}:=\{\omega\mapsto[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\}
where
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})
, which forms a linear space. Theorem
1.11
and Corollary
2.21
motivate the following definition:

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff topological space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
\psi_{\omega}
a weakly* continuous mapping into the state space of the quasi-local algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
that is translation co-variant with respect to
\vartheta
. If for every
\omega\in\Omega
, each quotient space
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}
at the cut site
x\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
, is finite dimensional, we say that
\psi_{\omega}

has small correlations
.

An example of such a TVCS is as follows.

Example 2.24
.

Let
\xi_{\omega}
be the weakly*-continuous covariant product state from Proposition
2.2
. Then,
\xi_{\omega}
has small correlations with fiber dimension 1.

Proof.

To see this,
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
. We note that if
a\sim_{x,\omega}b
, then,
\psi_{\omega}(a-b)=0
and so
I_{x,\omega}\subset\ker\psi_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}
. Conversely, if
a\in\ker\psi_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}
, then since
\psi_{\omega}
factors over the bond at
x
, we get
\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a)=\psi_{\omega}(c)\psi_{\vartheta^{x}\omega}(a)=0
for all
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
for all
\omega
. So
I_{x,\omega}
is the kernel of a linear functional and is therefore of codimension one by general functional analysis, hence
\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}\cong\mathbb{C}
for all
\omega
.
∎

Several remarks are in order.

Remark 2.25
.

The analysis in Example
2.24
works for any weakly* continuous function for which
\psi_{\omega}(a\otimes b)=\psi_{\omega}(a)\psi_{\omega}(b)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
, all
b\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}
and all
\omega\in\Omega
(i.e. a state with no correlations across the bipartition of the chain at
x
)

Remark 2.26
.

In general, we do not expect that the Bundle fibers are all equi-dimensional as in Example
2.24
. However, we show in Lemma
2.29
below, that the Banach bundle describing the correlations is independent of the cut-site up to isomorphism.

Remark 2.27
.

Nota bene
if
\psi_{\omega}
has small correlations across
x
, then the Banach spaces
\{B_{x,\omega}^{+}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
equipped with the linear space of sections given by
\{\omega\mapsto[A(\omega),\omega)]_{x}\colon A\in C_{\Omega}(A_{x}^{+})\}
defined in Lemma
2.6
form a Banach bundle over
\Omega
. We denote this bundle by
(\mathcal{C},\pi,\Omega)
(reference to a specific cut site is not necessary due to Remark
2.26
), and we refer to it as the
small correlation bundle
. We shall see in Theorem
2.32
below that the use of the definite article is justified since
\mathcal{C}
is minimal with respect to certain natural conditions.

We now show a TCVS analog of
[
30
, Proposition 2.1]
in Theorem
2.34
below. We split the proof into a few easier but technical lemmas first.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated quasilocal algebra. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Let
\psi_{\omega}
be a weakly* continuous, translation covariant state on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with small correlations. Let also
\mathscr{B}_{x}
be the Banach bundle at the cut site
x
. Fix
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\omega\in\Omega
. Define the mapping

\begin{split}E^{x}_{a,\omega}&:\mathcal{B}_{x,\omega}^{+}\to\mathcal{B}_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}^{+}\text{ via }\\
&[b,\omega]_{x}\mapsto[a\otimes\tau_{1}(b),\vartheta^{-1}\omega]_{x}\,.\end{split}

(2.12)

The following properties hold.

(a)

For each
a\in A
and
\omega\in\Omega
, the
E^{x}_{a,\omega}
mapping is well-defined bounded linear map on the fiber with

\|E^{x}_{a,\omega}[b,\omega]\|\leq\|a\|_{A}\|[b,\omega]_{x}\|

(2.13)

(b)

For all
a,b\in A
, the mapping

\omega\mapsto E^{x}_{a,\omega}[b,\omega]_{x}\in B_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}

(2.14)

is continuous in the (unique) Banach bundle topology. Thus,
E_{a}\in\hom^{\vartheta}(\mathscr{B}_{x},\mathscr{B}_{x})
.

(c)

There exists a continuous section
e^{x}\in\Gamma(\mathscr{B}_{x})
and a homomorphism
\varrho^{x}\in\hom(\mathscr{B}_{x},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
so that for any pure tensor
a\otimes b\otimes\cdots\otimes c\in\mathcal{A}_{[x,x+n]}
, one has

\psi_{\omega}(a\otimes b\otimes\cdots\otimes c)=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}\circ E^{x}_{a,\omega}\circ E^{x}_{b,\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots\circ E^{x}_{c,\vartheta^{n}\omega}(e^{x}(\vartheta^{n}\omega))\,.

(2.15)

Proof.

(a)
: Linearity in the argument is obvious, so let us check this map is well-defined. Let
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}
and note that

\psi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}(c\otimes a\otimes\tau_{1}(b))=\psi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}(\tau_{1}(\,\tau_{-1}(c\otimes a)\otimes b\,))=\psi_{\omega}(\tau_{-1}(c\otimes a)\otimes b)\,.

Thus, if
[b,\omega]_{x}=0
, its image
E^{x}_{a,\omega}[b,\omega]_{x}\in\mathcal{B}_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}^{+}
is also zero. To show the norm estimate, let
d\in I_{x,\omega}
, and note that

\|E^{x}_{a,\omega}[b,\omega]_{x}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}}=\|E^{x}_{a,\omega}[b+d,\omega]_{x}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}}=\inf_{f\in I_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}}\|a\otimes\tau_{1}(b+d)+f\|_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}\leq\|a\|\|b+d\|_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}\,

since
\tau_{1}
is an automorphism. Taking the infimum in
d
, over both sides yields (
2.13
).

(b)
: Let
(\omega_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a net in
\Omega
which converges to
\omega^{\prime}
. We need to show that
E^{x}_{a,\omega_{\lambda}}[b,\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}\to E^{x}_{a,\omega^{\prime}}[b,\omega^{\prime}]_{x}
in the bundle
\mathscr{B}_{x}
. To begin, notice that by the Stone-Weierstraß theorem for bundles combined with Theorem
1.11
, the linear space of sections given by

\mathcal{B}_{x}^{+}:=\{\omega\mapsto[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\colon A\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{Z})\}

is dense in
\Gamma(\mathscr{B}_{x})
. Therefore, by Lemma
1.8

(iii)
, it is sufficient to show that the basepoints converge and that for every
A\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
, the net of norms
\|E^{x}_{a,\omega_{\lambda}}[b,\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}-[A(\omega_{\lambda}),\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}\|\to\|E^{x}_{a,\omega^{\prime}}[b,\omega^{\prime}]_{x}-[A(\omega^{\prime}),\omega^{\prime}]_{x}\|
. We note
\pi(E^{x}_{a,\omega_{\lambda}}[b,\omega_{\lambda}]_{x})=\vartheta^{-1}(\omega_{\lambda})\to\vartheta^{-1}(\omega^{\prime})=\pi(E^{x}_{a,\omega^{\prime}}[b,\omega^{\prime}]_{x})
since
\vartheta
is an homeomorphism.

Now, set
\tilde{A}(\omega)=a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)-A\circ\vartheta^{-1}(\omega_{\lambda})\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}).
By Lemma
2.9
, and the assumption that
\psi_{\omega}
has small correlations, we have

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\|[\tilde{A}(\omega),\omega]_{x}\|_{B_{x,\omega}^{+}}

is continuous. One computes

\displaystyle\|E^{x}_{a,\omega_{\lambda}}[b,\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}-[A(\omega_{\lambda}),\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}\|

\displaystyle=\|[a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)-A\circ\vartheta^{-1}(\omega_{\lambda}),\vartheta^{-1}(\omega_{\lambda})]_{x}\|

\displaystyle=\|[\tilde{A}(\omega_{\lambda},\vartheta^{-1}\omega_{\lambda}]_{x}\|_{B_{x,\vartheta^{-1}\omega_{\lambda}}^{+}}

\displaystyle\to\|[\tilde{A}(\omega^{\prime}),\vartheta^{-1}\omega^{\prime}]_{x}=\|E^{x}_{a,\omega^{\prime}}[b,\omega^{\prime}]_{x}-[A(\vartheta^{-1}\omega^{\prime}),\vartheta^{-1}\omega^{\prime}]_{x}\|\,,

concluding the proof.

(c)
: Take
e^{x}(\omega)=[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}},\omega]_{x}
and
\varrho_{\omega}[b,\omega]_{x}=\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}}\otimes b)
. The section
e^{x}
is continuous by definition of the topology on
\mathscr{B}_{x}
. Notice also that
\varrho^{x}_{\omega}[A(\omega),\omega]=\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes A(\omega))
is continuous by Lemma
1.22
. To show (
2.15
), let us first start with two uncorrelated observables
a\otimes b\in\mathcal{A}_{[x,x+1]}
. One has

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}}\otimes a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}[a\otimes b,\omega]_{x}=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}[a\otimes\tau_{1}(\tau_{-1}(b)),\vartheta^{-1}\vartheta\omega]_{x}

\displaystyle=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}\circ E_{a,\omega}[\tau_{-1}(b),\vartheta\omega]_{x}=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}\circ E_{a,\omega}[\tau_{1}(\tau_{-1}(\tau_{-1}(b))),\vartheta^{-1}\vartheta(\vartheta\omega)]_{x}

\displaystyle=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}E_{a,\omega}^{x}\circ E_{b,\vartheta\omega}^{x}[\mathbbm{1}_{A_{x}^{+}},\vartheta^{2}\omega]_{x}=\varrho^{x}_{\omega}\circ E^{x}_{a,\omega}\circ E^{x}_{b,\vartheta\omega}(e^{x}(\vartheta^{2}\omega))\,.

Note that since
b
is supported on a single site
\tau_{-2}(b\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{x+2}^{+}})=b\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}}
, from which the last equality follows. The general formula now follows by induction.
∎

Lemma 2.29

(Freedom from a specific cut site.)
.

Suppose that
\psi_{\omega}
is a TCVS with small correlations. Then, for any cute site
x\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
there is an isometric Banach bundle isomorphism
\tilde{T}
between
\mathscr{B}_{x}
and
\mathscr{B}_{x+1}
which is covariant in
\vartheta
. Moreover the transfer operators defined in (
2.12
) obey

E_{a,\omega}^{x}=\tilde{T}^{-1}\circ E_{a,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}^{x+1}\circ\tilde{T}

(2.16)

Proof.

Recall that possessing small correlations is enough to guarantee that the family
\{B_{x,\omega}^{+}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
forms a Banach bundle
(\mathscr{B}_{x},\pi^{x},\Omega)
with a dense
C(\Omega)
-sub-module of sections given by
\{\omega\mapsto[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\colon A\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})\}\subset\Gamma(\mathscr{B}_{x})
. By Corollary
2.21
, we in fact have a family of Banach bundles indexed by the half-integers.

Let
\tilde{T}[b,\omega]_{x}=[\tau_{1}(b),\vartheta^{-1}\omega]_{x+1}
as in Lemma
2.20
. Notice that
\pi^{x+1}\circ\tilde{T}=\vartheta^{-1}\circ\pi^{x}
. Thus,
\tilde{T}
preserves convergence of the base-points since
\vartheta
is an homeomorphism. We have already seen that
\tilde{T}
is fiber-wise a linear isometry, so all that needs to be done is to show that it is an homeomorphism in the bundle topology. This is immediate by observing that

\|[b,\omega]_{x}-[A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\|=\|\tilde{T}[b-A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\|=\|[b,\vartheta^{-1}\omega]_{x+1}-[(K_{\vartheta}A)(\vartheta^{-1}\omega),\vartheta^{-1}\omega]_{x+1}\|\,.

Since the Koopman operator
K_{\vartheta}
is a
*
-isomorphism, it follows that
\{\omega\mapsto[K_{\vartheta}A(\omega),\omega]_{x}\colon A\in C_{\Omega}(\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+})\}\subset\Gamma(\mathscr{B}_{x+1})\}
is a dense
C(\Omega)
sub-module of
\Gamma(\mathscr{B}_{x+1})
. Taking these observations together is enough to guarantee that whenever
(b_{\lambda})\subset\mathscr{B}_{x}
is a convergent net, then so is
(\tilde{T}b_{\lambda})\subset\mathscr{B}_{x+1}
by Lemma
1.8
. Equation (
2.16
) follows from straightforward computation.
∎

Notation 2.30
.

In view of this freedom of choice, we will henceforth drop the dependence on a choice of cut site and instead write all formulae which follow with respect to the cut
x=-1/2
, so that
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{+}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}=:\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{-}=\mathcal{A}_{-\mathbb{N}}:=\mathcal{A}^{-}
. We will also reserve the letters
\varrho=\varrho^{-1/2}
,
E:=E^{-1/2}
, and
e^{1/2}:=e
for the family of objects defined as in Lemma
2.20
on the canonical bundle
(\mathcal{C},\pi,\Omega)
.

In
[
30
]
, the transfer operators are defined in a slightly different way by considering a map
E
defined on a tensor product of the on-site
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
and the space of correlations
B
. This allows them to define iterates on successive tensor powers of
\mathcal{A}
. Since we prefer to point-wise notation for our transfer operators, it is not as obvious that such an extension exists but the repairs are only minimal as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 2.31

(Iterates of Transfer Operators)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
the associated quasilocal algebra. Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the canonical bundle as in Remark
2.27
. Given a family of transfer operators
T_{1}:=\{E_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\}\subset\hom^{\vartheta^{-1}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
, and a positive number
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and any finite sum of pure tensors
\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}
,

\|\sum_{j}E_{a_{1}^{(j)}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{a_{p}^{(j)}}\|\leq\|\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}^{(j)}\|_{\min}

(2.17)

Thus, there exists a family of transfer operators
\{E^{(p)}_{b}:b\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}\}\subset\hom^{\vartheta^{-p}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
extending length
p
compositions
E_{a_{1}}\circ\cdots E_{a_{p}}
of elements of
T_{1}
.

Proof.

We have already observed that the transfer operators are linear in
a
. It is clear that if the estimate holds, then for any
d\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}
, one can find a sequence of iterates of the form
s
as above converging to
d
in
\|\cdot\|_{\min}
, and the resulting operator agrees with
[b,\omega]\mapsto[d\otimes b,\vartheta^{-p}\omega]
. So the proof reduces to showing that the estimate holds. We will show this in the case that
p=2
, and from there the proof follows easily by induction.

Let
v:=\sum_{j}a_{j}\otimes b_{j}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\min}\mathcal{A}
, and consider the operator
E^{(2)}_{v,\omega}:=\sum_{j}E_{a_{j},\vartheta\omega}\circ E_{b_{j},\omega}
. We see that for any
[x,\omega]\in\mathcal{C}
, one has

\displaystyle\|E^{(2)}_{v,\omega}[x,\omega]\|

\displaystyle=\|\sum_{j}E_{a_{j},\vartheta\omega}\circ E_{b_{j},\omega}[x,\omega]\|=\|\left[\sum_{j}a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)\otimes x,\vartheta^{-2}\omega\right]\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\sum_{j}a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)\|\|[x,\vartheta^{-2}\omega]\|=\|v\|_{\min}\|[x,\vartheta^{-2}\omega]\|.

To obtain the last equality, recall that in the expression
a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)
we have identified
a
with its image under the faithful representation
\iota_{0}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
via
a\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{(-\infty,-1]}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{[1,\infty)},
and similarly with
b
and
\iota_{1}
. Recalling that the value of the minimal tensor norm does not depend on the choice of faithful representation (
[
12
, Proposition 3.3.11]
), we obtain

\|\sum_{j}a\otimes\tau_{1}(b)\|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}}=\|\sum_{j}a\otimes b\|_{\min}=\|v\|_{\min},

completing the proof.
∎

Theorem 2.32

(Minimality)
.

Suppose that
\psi_{\omega}
is a TCVS with small correlations. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the canonical correlation bundle defined in Remark
2.27
with its native transfer apparatus
(\varrho,E,e)
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\pi,\Omega)
be any Banach bundle satisfying the following:

(i)

a Banach bundle
(\mathcal{E},\pi,\Omega)
so that each fiber is nontrivial and has finite dimension,

(ii)

a continuous section
f\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
,

(iii)

a homomorphism
\varphi\in\hom(\mathcal{E},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
, with
\varphi_{\omega}(f(\omega))\equiv 1
and
\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\|f_{\omega}\|\leq 1

(iv)

and, a family of
transfer operators

\{F_{a,\omega}:\mathcal{E}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\colon a\in\mathcal{A},\omega\in\Omega\}\subset\hom^{\vartheta}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})

which are bounded and linear in
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}
, and satisfy
F_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}(f(\omega))=f(\vartheta^{-1}\omega)
and
\varphi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\circ F_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}=\varphi_{\omega}
.

so that whenever
n<m
we have

\psi_{\omega}(a_{n}\otimes a_{n+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m})=\varphi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{a_{m},\vartheta^{m}\omega}(f(\vartheta^{m}\omega))

(2.18)

Then the following two conditions are equivalent

(a)

There exists a
\vartheta^{-1}
-co-variant isometric isomorphism
\Phi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{E}
.

(b)

For all
\omega
, the span of the transfer operators
\spn\{F_{a,\omega}\circ F_{b,\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{c,\vartheta^{n}}\circ f(\vartheta^{n}\omega)\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
and,
\spn\{\varphi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{a,\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ F_{b,\vartheta^{-(n-2)}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{c,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\}\subset\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
are dense.

Moreover, in the case that
(a)
or
(b)
holds, the transfer operators satisfy:

F_{a,\omega}=\Phi\circ E_{a,\omega}\circ\Phi^{-1}

(2.19)

Proof.

(a)\Rightarrow(b)
follows from the definition of a co-variant isometric isomorphism.

To see
(b)\Rightarrow(a)
, define
\Phi([a,\omega])=F_{a,\omega}(f(\omega))
. In particular, we have that and
\Phi(e(\omega))=\Phi([\mathbbm{1},\omega])=f(\omega)
.

To check that
\Phi
is well-defined, suppose
[a,\omega]=0
. In which case, for all
X^{-}\in\mathcal{A}^{-}
,

\displaystyle 0=\psi_{\omega}(X^{-}\otimes a)\,.

In particular, this holds for all
x_{-n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{-1}
. But by the transfer conditions, this means

0=\phi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{x_{-n},\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ F_{x_{-1},\vartheta^{-1}}\circ F_{a,\omega}(f(\omega))=\phi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{x_{-n},\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ F_{x_{-1},\vartheta^{-1}}\circ\Phi([a,\omega])\,.

By linearity, the above then holds for a dense subspace of
\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
, so it must be that the element
\Phi([a,\omega])=0_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
.

Now, let
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
, and observe that for any
d\in[0,\omega]\cap\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}}

\|\Phi([a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},\omega])\|=\|\Phi([a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}+d,\omega])\|=\|F_{a+d,\omega}(f(\omega))\|\leq\|F_{a+d,\omega}\|\leq\|a+d\|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}}

Whence,

\|\Phi([a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},\omega])\|\leq\|[a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}

since
\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is dense and
[0,\omega]
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore,
\Phi
extends to a linear map on all fibers in
\mathcal{C}
. By construction, one sees that
\Phi
has a fiber-wise dense range in
\mathcal{E}
.

Moreover,
\Phi
is an injection. To see this, suppose that
\Phi([a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},\omega])=0_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
. By the transfer property, this means that

0=\varphi_{\omega}\circ F_{a_{0},\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}\circ f(\vartheta^{n}\omega)=\psi_{\omega}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\,.

By Lemma
2.9
, this means,

\|[a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},\omega]\|=\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}^{-})_{1}}|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})|\leq 8|\psi_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})|=0

where the inequality is gotten by appealing to the Cartesian decomposition of operators in a
C^{*}
-algebra as in the argument leading up to inequality (
2.10
) in Lemma
2.9
. In fact, by working a little harder we can see that
\Phi
is an isometry: appealing again to Lemma
2.9
, we have

\displaystyle\|[b,\omega]\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{c\in(\mathcal{A}^{-})_{1}}|\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes b)|=\sup_{d\in D\subset(\mathcal{A}^{-})_{1}}|\varphi_{\vartheta^{\min\{\supp(d)\}-1}\omega}\circ F^{(|\supp(d)|)}_{d,\omega}\circ F_{b,\omega}(f(\vartheta\omega))|

\displaystyle=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}^{*}}|g(F_{b,\omega}(f(\omega)))|=\|F_{b,\omega}(f(\omega))\|\,

where by
D
we denote intersection of
\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
(A^{-})_{1}
, which is dense in
(\mathcal{A}^{-})_{1}
; and by
E^{(|\supp(d)|)}_{d,\omega}
we mean the iterated transfer operator as in Lemma
2.31
.

We need to check that
\Phi
is an homeomorphism. This follows since
\Phi
takes a dense submodule of
\Gamma(\mathcal{C})
to the dense submodule induced by the transfer operators in
\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
. Indeed, let
\Gamma_{0}
be the family of sections gotten by
\spn_{C(\Omega)}\{E_{a,\omega}\circ\cdots\circ E_{b,\vartheta^{n}\omega}\circ e\}
. Then,
\Phi(\Gamma_{0})=\spn_{C(\Omega)}\{\omega\mapsto F_{a,\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{b,\vartheta^{n}\omega}\circ f(\omega)\}
which is fiber-wise dense in
\mathcal{E}
by assumption. or equivalently, it is a dense
C(\Omega)
-submodule of
\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
. Thus, the topologies on
\mathcal{E}
induced by the dense submodule
\Phi(\Gamma_{0})
sections are the same as that induced by
\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
, so we conclude
\Phi
is continuous. The same argument works to show that
\Phi^{-1}
is continuous.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

In words, the minimality theorem says that with respect to properties (i)-(iv), having a dense subspaces generated by the transfer operators acting on distinguished sections
\varphi_{\omega}
and
e(\omega)
, is enough to ensure that the whole apparatus is in fact isometrically isomorphic to the small correlations bundle.

In Lemmas
2.28
–
2.31
and Theorem
2.32
, we have extensively explored the properties of the small correlations bundle
\mathcal{C}
. We have seen enough properties that we can now state our first main result.

Theorem 2.34

(Theorem
A
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated quasi-local algebra with canonical group action by translation
\mathbb{Z}\overset{\tau}{\curvearrowright}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Suppose
\omega\mapsto\psi_{\omega}
is a weakly*-continuous mapping into the states
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
which is translation covariant.
The following are equivalent.

(a)

For all
\omega\in\Omega
, the subspace
\spn\{\psi_{\omega}(X\otimes(\,\cdot\,))\colon X\in\mathcal{A}^{-}\}\subset(\mathcal{A}^{+})^{*}
is finite dimensional.

(b)

There exists a
transfer apparatus
consisting of a Banach bundle
(\mathcal{E},\pi,\Omega)
so that each fiber is nontrivial and finite dimensional, and a triple
(\phi,f,\{F_{a,\omega}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{+}})
of covariant homomorphisms implementing
\psi_{\omega}
as in (
2.18
).

Proof.

To see the
(a)\Rightarrow(b)
direction, note that the spanning condition implies that the Banach spaces
B^{-}_{-1/2,\omega}:=\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\omega}
are all finite dimensional. By our observations in Remark
2.11
, there is a perfect bilinear pairing
\eta_{\omega}:B_{\omega}^{-}\times B^{+}_{\omega}\to\mathbb{C}
, and thus
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{+}
is finite dimensional for all
\omega
as well. Thus,
\psi_{\omega}
has small correlations across every cut by Lemma
2.29
. Further, all the Banach bundles
\mathscr{B}_{x}^{+}
are isomorphic to the canonical bundle
\mathcal{C}=\mathscr{B}_{-1/2}^{+}
, which we take to be
\mathcal{E}
. The rest of the theorem statement follows from Lemma
2.28
.

The direction
(b)\Rightarrow(a)
follows essentially the same argument as Proposition 2.1 in
[
30
]
with some minor modifications. Define a pair of maps
Q^{+}_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}^{+}\to\mathcal{E}_{\omega}
and
Q^{-}_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}^{-}\to\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{*}
using the homomorphisms
\varphi
,
F_{a}
(with
a\in\mathcal{A}
), and the section
f
as follows:

\begin{split}Q^{+}_{\omega}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})&=F_{a_{0},\omega}\circ F_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}(f(\vartheta^{n}\omega))\in\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\\
Q^{-}_{\omega}(a_{-n}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{-1})&=\varphi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{a_{-n},\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{a_{-1},\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\in\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}.\end{split}

One checks that for all
\omega\in\Omega
, both
Q^{\pm}
are linear mappings from
\mathcal{A}^{+}\to\mathcal{E}_{\omega}
and
\mathcal{A}^{-}\to\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\omega}
respectively. We need to check that
Q_{\omega}^{\pm}
extend to bounded operators. Since the span of pure tensors is dense in the minimal
C^{*}
-norm, it is sufficient to show that

Q_{\omega}^{+}\left(\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots a_{n}^{(j)}\right)\leq\left\|\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots a_{n}^{(j)}\right\|_{\min}

To do so, let
V_{\omega}=\spn\{\varphi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{a,\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{b,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\}
, and
W_{\omega}=\spn\{F_{a_{0},\omega}\circ F_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{a_{n},\vartheta^{n}\omega}(f(\vartheta^{n}\omega))\}
. Since every
\mathcal{E}_{\omega}
is finite dimensional, so is each dual space
\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{*}
, and hence so are
V_{\omega}
and
W_{\omega}
. Now by the Minimality Theorem
2.32
, we know that
V_{\omega}
and
W_{\omega}
can be mapped isometrically into the small correlations bundle. By Lemma
2.9
, we conclude that
V_{\omega}
and
W_{\omega}
are norming for one another.

Furthermore, one checks that

\displaystyle Q^{-}_{\omega}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}}b_{-m}^{(j^{\prime})}\otimes\cdots b_{0}^{(j^{\prime})}\right)\circ Q_{\omega}^{+}\left(\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots a_{n}^{(j)}\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\omega}\left\{\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}}b_{-m}^{(j^{\prime})}\otimes\cdots b_{0}^{(j^{\prime})}\right)\otimes\left(a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots a_{n}^{(j)}\right)\right\}

Therefore, by decomposing a
v\in(V_{\omega})_{1}
into a sum of pure tensors, we may rewrite

|v(Q^{+}(\sum a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}^{(j)})|=\psi_{\omega}(v^{\prime}\otimes\sum a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}^{(j)}),

for some sum of pure tensors
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{-}
. Thus, we can bound
\|Q^{+}(a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}^{(j)})\|\leq 8|\psi_{\omega}(v^{\prime}\otimes\mathbbm{1})|\|\sum_{j}a_{1}^{(j)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}^{(j)}\|
by appealing to Cartesian decompositions if necessary. Therefore,
Q^{+}
extends to a bounded linear operator since
|\psi_{\omega}(v^{\prime}\otimes\mathbbm{1})|\leq\|[v^{\prime},\omega]^{-}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}^{-}}=\|v\|
, by Theorem
2.32
once again. A similar argument works for
Q^{-}
. Therefore,

\psi_{\omega}(X^{-}\otimes X^{+})=Q^{-}_{\omega}(X^{-})\circ Q^{+}_{\omega}(X^{+}),\quad\forall X^{-}\in\mathcal{A}^{-},X^{+}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\,.

Now, since the range of
Q_{\omega}^{-}
is
\spn\{\varphi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}\circ F_{a,\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ F_{b,\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}^{*}
, the latter being finite dimensional, we have that

\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\omega}\supset\spn\{F^{-}_{\omega}(X^{-})\colon X^{-}\in\mathcal{A}^{-}\}\cong\spn\{\psi_{\omega}(X^{-}\otimes(\,\cdot\,))\colon X^{-}\in\mathcal{A}^{-}\}\subset(\mathcal{A}^{+})^{*}

is finite dimensional for all
\omega
.
∎

Definition 2.35
.

In view of Theorems
2.34
, the existence of a triple
(\varphi,F,f)
where
\varphi\in\hom(\mathcal{E},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
, and
F=\{E_{a}\in\hom^{\vartheta^{-1}}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})\colon a\in\mathcal{A}\}
, and a section
f\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
satisfying Theorem
2.34
.(b)(i)-(iv) is inherent to the expansion of a TCVS
\psi_{\omega}
with small correlations. To make the following more readable, we shall call such a triple a
transfer apparatus
on the bundle
(\mathcal{E},\pi,\Omega)
. We shall reserve the letters
(\varrho,E,e)
for the transfer apparatus defined on the canonical bundle
(\mathcal{C},\pi,\Omega)
as in Lemma
2.20
.

An immediate corollary is the following characterization of the small correlations property.

Corollary 2.36
.

Let
\psi_{\omega}
be a TCVS on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then
\psi_{\omega}
has small correlations if and only if either of the equivalent conditions in Theorem
2.34
are satisfied.

2.3
TCVS with Small Correlations are Weakly* Dense

In
[
30
, Proposition 2.6]
, it is shown that the class of
C^{*}
-finitely correlated states is weakly* dense inside the translation invariant states. We aim to show an analogue of this for translation covariant states with small correlations. To do so, we need to recall some light details about direct sums and tensor products of Banach bundles.

Let
X
and
Y
be two Banach bundles over
\Omega
. Form the family
\{Z_{\omega}:=X_{\omega}\oplus Y_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
by taking the topological direct sum of the fibers, equipped with the canonical surjection
\pi
onto the base-points. Taking
\Gamma=\Gamma(X)\oplus\Gamma(Y)
defines a Banach module of sections into
Z:=\bigsqcup_{\omega}Z_{\omega},
and therefore there exists a unique topology turning
(Z,\pi,\Omega)
into a Banach bundle with
\Gamma(X)\oplus\Gamma(Y)
a dense sub-module of continuous sections (Theorem
1.11
). We say that
Z
is the
direct sum
of
X
and
Y
and write
Z=X\oplus Y
. It is not difficult to check that the analogous definition for homomorphisms is valid.

Notation 2.37
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated quasilocal algebra. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. By
\mathfrak{C}
, we mean the set of translation co-variant states on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The subset of those translation co-variant states with small correlations is denoted by
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{C}
.

Lemma 2.38

(
\mathfrak{C}
is Convex)
.

With notation as in
2.37
, both
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{K}
are convex within the vector space of weakly*-continuous mappings into
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as in
[
30
, Proposition 2.6(2)]
. The nontrivial part is to show that
\mathfrak{K}
is convex. To this end, let
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{K}
and
\mu\in(0,1)
. Clearly,
g:=\mu\psi+(1-\mu)\phi\in\mathfrak{C}
. Theorem
2.34
tells us there exists Banach bundles
(\mathcal{E},\pi_{\mathcal{E}},\Omega)
and
(\mathcal{F},\pi_{\mathcal{F}},\Omega)
, sections
e\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E})
and
f\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F})
, homomorphisms
\varrho\in\hom(\mathcal{E},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
and
\varphi\in\hom(\mathcal{F},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
, and transfer operators
\{E_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\in\hom^{\vartheta}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})
, and
\{F_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\subset\hom^{\vartheta}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
, for
\psi
and
\phi
respectively. Form
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{F}
as the direct sum of the Banach bundles. One readily checks that with
\gamma:=\mu\varrho+(1-\mu)\varphi\in\hom(\mathcal{G},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
,
h:=e\oplus f
, and transfer operators
\{G_{a}:=E_{a}\oplus F_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}},
that the bundle
(\mathcal{G},\pi_{\mathcal{G}},\Omega)
satisfies (
2.18
) for the state
g
.
∎

Lemma 2.39

(
\mathfrak{K}
is robust under grouping)
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\psi\in\mathfrak{K}
. There exists a weakly* continuous mapping
\psi^{(p)}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}((\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}})
that is
\vartheta^{p}
-covariant with respect to translation on
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\psi^{(p)}
has small correlations.

Proof.

Just as in
[
30
, Proposition 2.6(3)]
,
\psi^{(p)}
is just
\psi
viewed as taking arguments from
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
, in which case, the iterated transfer maps from Lemma
2.31
determine the expectation values.
∎

Lemma 2.40

(Aromatic Average)
.

Suppose that
\phi
is a TCVS on
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
with small correlations that co-varies with respect to
\vartheta^{p}
. I.e.
\phi\circ\tau_{kp}=\phi_{\vartheta^{kp}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
\phi
has small correlations, then the state

\bar{\phi}_{\omega}:=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p}\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ\tau_{n}

(2.20)

is translation covariant with respect to
\vartheta
on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and has small correlations.

Proof.

Checking translation covariance is not difficult:

\bar{\phi}_{\omega}\circ\tau_{1}=\frac{1}{p}(\phi_{\omega}\circ\tau_{1}+\phi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\circ\tau_{2}+\cdots+\phi_{\vartheta^{-(p-1)}\omega}\circ\tau_{p})=\frac{1}{p}(\phi_{\vartheta\omega}+\phi_{\omega}\circ\tau_{1}+\cdots+\phi_{\vartheta^{-(p-1)}}\circ\tau_{p})=\bar{\phi}_{\vartheta\omega}\,.

Now we need to construct a bundle satisfying part (b) of Theorem
2.34
. To begin, let
(\mathcal{C}^{\phi},\pi,\Omega)
be the canonical bundle associated to
\phi
on
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
equipped with transfer apparatus
(\varrho^{\phi},\{E^{\phi}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{A}^{p}},e^{\phi})
.

Let
0<n\leq p-1
, consider the vector subspaces of
\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}
given by

\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{\omega}:=\spn\{[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\omega]\colon\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}\}.

We take
\mathcal{C}^{(0)}_{\omega}=\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}
. For each
\omega\in\Omega
we now define the finite-dimensional vector space

\mathcal{R}_{\omega}:=\spn\left\{\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]\colon\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}\right\}\subset\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}\mathcal{C}^{(n)}_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}

(2.21)

equipped with the graph norm

\left\|\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]\right\|_{\mathcal{R}_{\omega}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\|[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}}

(2.22)

Note that each element of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
is a
p
-tuple of equivalence classes of the translates of a single operator
\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}
up to the cut
p
.

Now, let
\mathcal{R}:=\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
be equipped with the surjection
\pi(\oplus[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\vartheta^{-n}\omega])=\omega
. Let also
\Gamma
be the linear space of functions of the form

\alpha:\omega\mapsto\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(A(\omega)),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]

(2.23)

where
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)})
. One readily checks that
\Gamma
is fiber-wise dense in
\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, one checks that for all
\alpha\in\Gamma
, the assignment
\omega\mapsto\|\alpha(\omega)\|_{\mathcal{R}_{\omega}}
is continuous since each summand in the graph norm is the norm of a translate of the continuous section
(\omega\mapsto[A(\omega),\omega])\in\Gamma(\mathcal{C}^{\phi})
. Once again, we note that the tuple above is defined using the same function
A
in each coordinate.

Therefore, by the Fell-Doran Theorem (Theorem
1.11
above), there is a unique topology making
\mathcal{R}
into a Banach bundle with
\Gamma
a dense
C(\Omega)
-submodule of continuous sections. For brevity, we will refer to
\mathcal{R}
as the
aromatic bundle
.

We need to equip
\mathcal{R}
with a transfer apparatus that implements the average
\bar{\phi}
. To do so, let
a\in\mathcal{A}
and define the maps

\begin{split}F_{a,\omega}&\left(\oplus_{n=0}^{p-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n}(\xi),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]\right):=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi),\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega]\\
&=[a\otimes\tau_{1}(\xi),\vartheta^{-1}\omega]\oplus[\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{2}(\xi),\vartheta^{-2}\omega]\oplus\cdots\oplus[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes(p-1)}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{p}(\xi),\vartheta^{-p}\omega]\,.\end{split}

(2.24)

Claim 2.40.1
.

The maps
F_{a,\omega}
are well-defined
\vartheta^{-1}
-covariant homomorphisms of
\mathcal{R}
.

Postponing the proof of the claim for now, we aim to show the
F_{a}
’s form a set of transfer operators in a transfer apparatus (see Definition
2.35
) for
\bar{\phi}
. To complete the transfer apparatus, let
f(\omega)=\bigoplus[\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{-n}\omega]
, and let
\mu_{\omega}=\frac{1}{p}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}\varrho^{\phi}_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}
. One checks that
f
is a continuous section and that
\mu_{\omega}\in\hom(\mathcal{R},\Omega\times\mathbb{C})
. Furthermore,
\mu_{\omega}(f(\omega))\equiv 1
. Now, let
a\in\mathcal{A}
. We will show that
F_{a,\omega}
implements
\bar{\phi}
with
f
and
\mu
, and the general case will follow by iteration. One has

\displaystyle\mu_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}(F_{a,\omega}(f(\omega)))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\varrho^{\phi}_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega]=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\phi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega}(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-2}\phi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}}(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes a)+\frac{1}{p}\phi_{\omega}(a)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-2}\phi_{\vartheta^{-(n+1)}}(\tau_{n}(a))+\frac{1}{p}\phi_{\omega}(a)

\displaystyle=\bar{\phi}_{\omega}(a)

where the second-to last inequality follows since
\phi
is
\vartheta^{p}
-covariant, and the result of translating by
-p
puts
a
at the zero site. Therefore,
(\mathcal{R},\pi,\Omega)
is a Banach bundle whose fibers are finite dimensional with
(\mu,F,f)
serving as a transfer apparatus for
\bar{\phi}
. Thus by Theorem
2.34
, we conclude that
\bar{\phi}
has small correlations.
∎

Below, we record a proof of Claim
2.40.1
.

Proof of Claim
2.40.1
.

Recall that in any
C^{*}
-algebra, an element is generated by a Cartesian sum of self-adjoint elements. In fact, due to the triangle inequality, to show that the
F_{a}
are well-defined, it is sufficient to show that if
\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}
is self-adjoint and
r=\oplus[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\xi,\vartheta^{-n}\omega]
is zero, then
F_{a}(r)=0
.

Now, let
a\in\mathcal{A}
. We may write
a=a_{0}-a_{1}+ia_{3}-ia_{4}
where each
a_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum\|a_{j}\|\leq 2\|a\|
. Observe that for any
c\in(\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)})_{s.a.}
, one has the estimate

-\|a_{j}\|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(c\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))\leq\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(c\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n-1)}a_{j}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))\leq\|a_{j}\|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(c\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))

(2.25)

Now, we may write any
d\in\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)}
as
d=\Re(d)+i\Im(d)=:d_{1}+id_{2}
, and
d_{1},d_{2}\in(\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)})_{s.a.}
. Therefore, by applying the inequality in (
2.25
) for both
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, we obtain for any
0\leq n\leq p-1
the inequality

\displaystyle|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n-1)}

\displaystyle\otimes a\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|\leq|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d_{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|+|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d_{2}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|a\|\left[|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(\frac{d+d^{*}}{2}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|+|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(\frac{d-d^{*}}{2}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\|a\|\left[|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|+|\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d^{*}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n+1)}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|\right]

\displaystyle=4\|a\||\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}(d\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi))|

where the last equality follows since
\xi
is a self-adjoint. Now, since the fibers
\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}
are finite dimensional, Lemma
2.9
tells us that

\|[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes a\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi),\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega]\|\leq 4\|a\|\|[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\tau_{n+1}(\xi),\vartheta^{-(n+1)}\omega]\|\,.

(2.26)

Lastly, we note that for
n=p-1

\|[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes p}\otimes\tau_{p}(\xi),\vartheta^{-p}\omega]\|\leq\|[\xi,\omega]\|

(2.27)

since
\phi
is
\vartheta^{p}
-covariant: indeed, we note that

\|[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes p}\otimes\tau_{p}(\xi),\vartheta^{-p}\omega]\|=\inf_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{\vartheta^{-p}\omega}}\|\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes p}\otimes\tau_{p}(\xi)+x\|=\inf_{\tau_{-p}(x)}\|\xi+\tau_{-p}(x)\|\leq\inf_{y\in\mathcal{I}_{\omega}}\|\xi+y\|=\|[\xi,\omega]\|.

Finally, it follows by the triangle inequality, inequality (
2.26
), and inequality (
2.27
) that for any element
r\in\mathcal{R}
as defined in the beginning of the proof

\|F_{a,\pi(r)}(r)\|\leq 4\|a\|\|r\|.

(2.28)

Whence
F_{a}
is well-defined for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

We now work a little harder to extend inequality (
2.28
) to all of
\mathcal{R}
. Notice that if
\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}
, then

\|[\xi,\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}}=\|[\xi^{*},\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}}

In fact, if
d\in\mathcal{I}_{\omega}
, then for any
x\in\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)}
one has
0=\phi_{\omega}(x\otimes d)=\overline{\phi_{\omega}(x^{*}\otimes d^{*})}
, whence
\mathcal{I}_{\omega}=\mathcal{I}_{\omega}^{*}
by letting
x
range. Thus,

\|[\xi,\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}}=\inf_{d\in\mathcal{I}_{\omega}}\|\xi+d\|=\inf_{d\in\mathcal{I}_{\omega}}\|\xi^{*}+d^{*}\|=\|[\xi^{*},\omega]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\phi}_{\omega}}.

(2.29)

In fact,
\|[\xi,\omega]\|=0
implies both
\|[\Re(\xi),\omega]\|=0
and
\|[\Im(\xi),\omega]\|=0
by the triangle inequality.

Now, let
s=\oplus[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\eta,\vartheta^{-n}\omega]
for
\eta\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}
, and let
s_{1}=\oplus[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\Re(\eta),\vartheta^{-n}\omega]
and
s_{2}=\oplus[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\Im\eta,\vartheta^{-n}\omega]
so that
s=s_{1}+is_{2}
in
\mathcal{R}
. We note
\|s_{j}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}(\|s\|+\|s^{*}\|)=\|s\|
by (
2.29
). Therefore,

\|F_{a,\omega}(s)\|\leq\|F_{a,\omega}(s_{1})\|+\|F_{a}(s_{2})\|\leq 4\|a\|\left(\|s_{1}\|+\|s_{2}\|\right)\leq 8\|a\|\|s\|_{\infty}

(2.30)

Thus, we have also shown that
\|F_{a}\|_{\infty}\leq 8\|a\|
.

That
F_{a}
is
\vartheta^{-1}
-covariant is now obvious, so the last thing to check is that for any
\alpha\in\Gamma(\mathcal{R})
, the image
F_{a}(\alpha)
is a continuous function into
\mathcal{R}
. This follows immediately from the observation that
a\otimes\tau_{1}(A(\omega))\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)})
for all
A\in C(\Omega,\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)})
, so in fact on the dense
C(\Omega)
submodule
\Gamma
given by sections as in (
2.23
),
F_{a}\cdot\Gamma\subset\Gamma
, and we are done.
∎

We now conclude this section with the following theorem.

Theorem 2.41

(Theorem
B
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated quasilocal algebra. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. By
\mathfrak{C}
, we mean the set of translation co-variant states on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and the subset of those translation co-variant states with small correlations is denoted by
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{C}
. One has that
\mathfrak{K}
is weakly-* dense in
\mathfrak{C}
uniformly in
\omega
.

Proof.

We roughly follow the strategy laid out in
[
30
, Proposition 2.6(d)]
. Let
\psi_{\omega}\in\mathfrak{C}
be a translation co-variant state. For
p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\phi_{\omega}=\psi_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{A}_{[0,p-1]}}
denote the restriction to the local algebra on the interval
[0,p-1]
. Note that
\phi_{\omega}
is weakly-* continuous as a function of
\omega
. Let
\phi^{\prime}_{\omega}=\bigotimes_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\vartheta^{np}\omega}
denote the
\vartheta^{p}
-covariant product state on
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
as constructed in Proposition
2.2
.

As we saw in Example
2.24
, it follows that
\phi^{\prime}_{\omega}
has small correlations on
(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p})_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let now
\bar{\phi}_{\omega}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}^{\prime}\circ\tau_{n}
be the Aromatic average. By Lemma
2.40
, we know that
\bar{\phi}
is a
\vartheta
-TCVS on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with small correlations.

Now, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}^{\loc}
. Find
p
large enough so that
\supp(a)\subset[j,j+m]\subset[0,p-1]
, for some integers
j,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Notice that
\psi_{\omega}(a)
and
\phi_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ\tau_{n}(a)
agree unless
j+m+n\geq p
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\omega}(a)-\bar{\phi}_{\omega}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{p-1-(j+m)}^{p-1}|\psi_{\omega}(a)-\phi_{\vartheta^{n}\omega}^{\prime}\circ\tau_{n}(a)|

(2.31)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|a\|\frac{j+m}{p}\to 0

as
p\to\infty
.

To see that this inequality holds on all
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}
, note that if
0\in\supp(a)
, then the translate
\tilde{a}=\tau_{m}(a)\subset[0,p-1]
for
p
large enough where we set
m=|\min\{\supp(a)\}|+1
. By translation co-variance, we have
|\psi_{\omega}(a)-\bar{\phi}_{\omega}(a)|=|\psi_{\vartheta^{m}\omega}(\tilde{a})-\phi_{\vartheta^{m}\omega}(\tilde{a})|
, so the inequality (
2.31
) applies to the
\sim
-ed quantity. But
\|\tilde{a}\|=\|a\|,
and we are done since the estimate is uniform in
\omega
.
∎

2.4
Fiber Structure

In this section, we present a refinement of Theorem
2.34
that allows one to construct a TCVS from general parts. This result is along the same lines as Proposition 2.4 in
[
30
]
, but is slightly more general in that we do not need to assume the single-site algebra is finite dimensional.

Let us recall some terminology before stating our main theorem. We direct the interested reader to
[
67
, Chapter 13]
(see also
[
25
, Chapter 5]
) for further information. A complex vector space
V
is a
*
-vector space if it comes equipped with an anti-linear involution
(\,\cdot\,)^{*}:V\to V
. Those elements with
v^{*}=v
are called
self-adjoint
, and we write
(V)_{s.a.}
for the vector subspace of all self-adjoint elements. We write
\mathbb{M}_{n}(V)
for the linear space of
n\times n
arrays with entries in
V
equipped with the involution
(v_{i,j})^{*}=(v_{j,i}^{*})
. A
*
-vector space is said to be
matrix ordered
if both of the following are satisfied:

1.

For every
n\geq 0
, there exists a collection of convex cones
C_{n}\subset(\mathbb{M}_{n}(V))_{s.a}
with
C_{n}\cap-C_{n}=\{0\}
which induce an ordering on
\mathbb{M}_{n}(V)
.

2.

For every rectangular
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n\times m}(\mathbb{C})
, one has
A^{*}C_{n}A\subset C_{m}
where

A^{*}(v_{j,k})_{j,k=1}^{n}A=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{s,k}a_{r,\ell}^{*}v_{k,\ell}\right)_{s,\ell=1}^{m}

Furthermore, if
V
is a matrix-ordered
*
-vector space, an element
e\in C_{1}
is said to be an
order unit
if for all
x\in(V)_{s.a.}
, there is real
r>0
with
re+x\in C_{1}
. The element
e
is
Archimedean
if
re+x\in C_{1}
for all
r>0
implies
x\in C_{1}
. The last piece of terminology we recall is that
e
is said to be an
Archimedean matrix order unit
if
e\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{n\times n}
is an Archimedean order unit in
\mathbb{M}_{n}(V)
for all
n\geq 0
.

Theorem 2.42

(Construction Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated spin chain. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. The following are equivalent.

(a)

A state
\psi_{\omega}
on
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a translation co-variant state with small correlations.

(b)

There is a Banach bundle
(\mathcal{E},\pi,\Omega)
with a transfer apparatus
(\varrho,E,e)
so that each of the following holds:

(i)

Each fiber
\mathcal{E}_{\omega}
is a finite-dimensional matrix-ordered
*
-vector space with
e(\omega)
forming an Archimedean matrix order unit.

(ii)

Each transfer operator
E_{a^{*}a,\omega}
are completely positive with respect to the matrix ordering, and the norm estimate
\|E_{a^{*}a,\omega}\|\leq\|a^{*}a\|_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(iii)

The linear functional
\varrho_{\omega}
is completely positive.

(iv)

Both
E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}(e(\omega))=e(\vartheta^{-1}\omega)
and
\varrho_{\omega}(e(\omega))\equiv 1
.

(v)

One has

\varrho_{\omega}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta\omega}\circ\cdots\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{n}\omega}=\varrho_{\vartheta^{n}\omega}.

(2.32)

(vi)

Lastly, defining
\psi^{[-n,n]}_{\omega}=\varrho_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ E^{(2n+1)}\circ e(\vartheta^{n}\omega)
on
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(2n+1)}
, via the iterates, the weak-* limit as
n\to\infty
exists and is a TCVS with small correlations.

Proof.

The forward direction
(a)\Rightarrow(b)
follows by verifying each of
(b)(i)-(vi)
for the canonical bundle. By the work of
[
47
, Proposition 3.4]
, if we can show that there exists a family of states
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}})
so that
\mathcal{I}_{\omega}=\bigcap_{\alpha}\ker\alpha
, then
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
is an operator system with cones given by

V_{\omega}^{(n)}:=\{([x_{i,j},\omega])\colon\forall\epsilon>0,\,\exists d_{i,j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\omega}\colon\epsilon\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{n\times n}+(x_{i,j}+d_{i,j})\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}})_{+}\}

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\omega}
denote the set of
c\in(\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)})_{+}
so that
\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)})=1
. Each
c\in\mathcal{T}_{\omega}
defines a state by setting
f_{c}(\,\cdot\,)=\psi_{\omega}(c\otimes(\,\cdot\,))
. The inclusion
\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{T}_{\omega}}\ker f\supset\mathcal{I}_{\omega}
is trivial. To see the reverse inclusion, first, we note that if
h\in(\mathcal{A}_{(-\infty,0)})_{+}
with
\psi_{\omega}(h)=0
, and if
x\in\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{T}_{\omega}}\ker f
, then the inequality

0\leq|\psi_{\omega}(h\otimes x)|\leq|\psi_{\omega}(h)\|\|x\|=0

holds since
f_{h}
is a positive linear functional on a
C^{*}
-algebra with unit, and therefore achieves its operator norm at the unit. In general, for any
g\in\mathcal{A}_{-(\infty,0)}
, we may write
g=g_{1}-g_{2}+ig_{3}-ig_{4}
where
g_{j}\geq 0
. Whence

|\psi_{\omega}(g\otimes x)|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\psi_{\omega}(g_{j}\otimes x)|=\sum\psi_{\omega}(g_{j}\otimes\mathbbm{1})|\psi_{\omega}(\tilde{g}_{j}\otimes x)|=0

where
\tilde{g}_{j}=\psi_{\omega}(g_{j})^{-1}g_{j}
where defined.

Now, we aim to show
b(ii)
which is the only nontrivial part of the theorem statement. However, this follows easily since
[
47
, Proposition 3.4]
says that the quotient map
p_{\omega}
(as defined in Lemma
2.6
) is completely positive for all
\omega
. Notice the mapping
\mu_{a^{*}a}:(x\mapsto a^{*}a\otimes\tau_{1}(x))
is completely positive on
\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}
. We trivially have
E_{a^{*}a,\omega}\circ p_{\omega}=p_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\circ\mu_{a^{*}a}
the latter being a composition of completely positive maps, hence
E_{a^{*}a,\omega}\circ p_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}_{[0,\infty)}\to C_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}
is completely positive. But the image of
A
under
p_{\omega}
is just
C_{\omega}
.

The nontrivial part of
(b)\Rightarrow(a)
is verifying the weak-* limit property. To this end, we employ Takeda’s theorem (Theorem
1.23
above). Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
be finite and write
\Lambda=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{|\Lambda|}\}
with
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{|\Lambda|}
. Our strategy is to fill in the ‘gaps’ in
\Lambda
with compositions by
E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{p}\omega}
with
p
being a site satisfying
x_{0}<p<x_{|\Lambda|}
with
p\in\Lambda^{c}
. To this end, let
M=\max\{|x_{0}|,|x_{|\Lambda|}|\}
and set

\psi^{\Lambda}_{\omega}=\varrho_{\vartheta^{-M}\omega}\circ E^{(2M+1)}\circ e(\vartheta^{M}\omega)=\psi^{[-M,M]}_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}}.

Therefore, it is sufficient to check that inclusions of intervals are consistent. To see this, suppose that
m>n
. One has

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}^{[-m,m]}|_{\mathcal{A}_{[-n,n]}}

\displaystyle=\varrho_{\vartheta^{-m}\omega}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{-m}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{-n-1}\omega}\circ E^{(2n+1)}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\vartheta^{n+1}}\circ\cdots\circ e(\vartheta^{m}\omega)

\displaystyle=\varrho_{\vartheta^{-n}\omega}\circ E^{(2n+1)}\circ e(\vartheta^{n}\omega)=\psi^{[-n,n]}_{\omega}

where we have used
(b)(v)
and
(b)(iv)
to obtain the second equality. Thus, there exists a well-defined state
\psi_{\omega}
on
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
that restricts to the finite-volume states we have defined. Moreover, by construction, each
\psi^{\Lambda}_{\omega}
is weakly*-continuous in
\omega
on
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
from which it follows
\psi_{\omega}
is weakly-* continuous in
\omega
on the dense subalgebra
\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Verifying translation covariance of
\psi_{\omega}
amounts to checking on
\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}},
which is a straight-forward calculation. Lastly,
\psi_{\omega}
is a bounded linear functional because of the norm estimate in
(b)(ii)
, and
\psi_{\omega}
is positive since each
\psi^{\Lambda}
is a composition of completely positive maps. But
\psi_{\omega}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{C}
, the latter being an Abelian
C^{*}
-algebra, whence
\psi_{\omega}
is completely positive. To conclude, note
\psi_{\omega}
is a state because of the normalization condition in
(b)(iv)
.
∎

Remark 2.43
.

The main content of Theorem
2.42
is that one can assemble all TCVS with small correlations from bundles of Archimedean matrix ordered vector spaces and a family of completely positive transfer operators thereon. Furthermore, such constructions are weak-* dense in
\mathfrak{C}
since the argument in Theorem
2.41
did not rely upon the fibers having any additional structure. Put another way: these constructions are weak-* dense in
\mathfrak{C}
since Theorem
2.42
says
every
TCVS with small correlations can be recovered in this way.

Proposition 2.44
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
algebra and let
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated quasi-local algebra. Let
\Omega
be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with an homeomorphism
\vartheta
. Suppose that
\psi_{\omega}
is a translation co-variant state with small correlations, and denote by
(\mathcal{C},\pi,\Omega)
the canonical bundle. There exists a
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle over
\Omega
extending
(\mathcal{C},\Omega,\pi)
. Moreover, there exists a transfer apparatus for
\psi_{\omega}
on the
C^{*}
-bundle.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\Psi_{\omega})
be the GNS triple associated to
\psi_{\omega}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\mathbb{B}_{\omega}=\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})
denote the image of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
under
\pi_{\omega}
which is a
C^{*}
-algebra itself for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Now, suppose that
a\in(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})_{s.a.}
, which implies
\pi_{\omega}(a)\in(\mathbb{B}_{\omega})_{s.a}\subset B(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})_{s.a.}
for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Then,

\|\pi_{\omega}(a)\|=\sup_{\xi,\eta\in(\mathcal{H})_{1}}|\langle\xi|\pi_{\omega}(a)\xi\rangle|=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\\
\psi_{\omega}(b^{*}b)\leq 1\end{subarray}}|\langle\pi_{\omega}(b)\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(a)\pi_{\omega}(b)\Psi_{\omega}\rangle|=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}\colon\\
\psi_{\omega}(b^{*}b)\leq 1}|\psi_{\omega}(b^{*}ab)|\,.

By arguing similarly to the proof of Lemma
2.9
, one finds that this supremum is continuous in
\omega
whenever
a\in(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})_{s.a.}
. Since this set linearly generates the quasilocal algebra, we may apply the Fell-Doran theorem to
\mathbb{B}_{\omega}
and see it becomes a Banach bundle when equipped with the continuity structure
\Gamma=\{\omega\mapsto\pi_{\omega}(A(\omega))\colon A\in C(\Omega,\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})\}
.

To show this is a
C^{*}
-bundle, we need to check that the multiplication and involution on each fiber is continuous in the bundle topology. To this end let
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a net which converges to
e_{0}
. Then there is some net
\omega_{\lambda}
and
a_{\lambda}
so that
e_{\lambda}=\pi_{\omega_{\lambda}}(a_{\lambda})
. It is then clear that for any section
f\in\Gamma
one has

\|\pi_{\omega_{\lambda}}(a_{\lambda})^{*}-f(\omega_{\lambda})^{*}\|=\|\pi_{\omega_{\lambda}}(a_{\lambda})-\pi_{\omega_{\lambda}}(A(\omega_{\lambda}))\|\to\|\pi_{\omega_{0}}(a_{0})-\pi_{\omega_{0}}(A(\omega_{0}))\|=\|e_{0}^{*}-f(\omega_{0})^{*}\|

so that the involution is continuous by choice of
\Gamma
.

Turning toward the multiplication, recall from Remark VII.8.2 in
[
32
]
, we need to check that for each pair of sections
f,g
the map
\omega\mapsto f(\omega)\otimes g(\omega)
is continuous. But
f\cdot g\in\Gamma
by construction, therefore by the Fell-Doran Theorem
1.11
, the topology we have given the
\bigcup_{\omega}\mathbb{B}_{\omega}
makes the products continuous.

For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the maps

U_{k}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}

via

U_{k}\pi_{\omega}(a)\Psi_{\omega}=\pi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}(\tau_{k}(a))\Psi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\,.

It is not difficult to show these densely defined linear maps preserve the inner product, and therefore extend to unitaries on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
. Then, the translation may be implemented by the unitary maps

U_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta\omega}

which are independent of
\omega
, and therefore the triple
E_{a,\omega}(\pi_{\omega}(b)):=U_{1}^{*}\pi_{\omega}(\tau_{-1}(a))\times\pi_{\omega}(b)U_{1}
,
\varrho_{\omega}=\langle\Psi_{\omega}|\,\cdot\,|\Psi_{\omega}\rangle
, and
e(\omega)=\mathbbm{1}_{B(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})}
defines a transfer apparatus that implements
\psi_{\omega}
.

It is sufficient to show that the implementation happens with just two operators by induction. We have

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(a_{0}\otimes a_{1})

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(a_{0}\otimes a_{1})|\Psi_{\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(a_{0})\pi_{\omega}(\tau_{1}(a_{1}))|\Psi_{\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(a_{0})U_{1}^{*}\pi_{\vartheta\omega}(a_{1})U_{1}|\Psi_{\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(a_{0})E_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}(\mathbbm{1}_{B(\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta\omega})})|\Psi_{\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}|U_{1}^{*}\pi_{\omega}(a_{0})E_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}(\mathbbm{1})U_{1}|\Psi_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\varrho_{\vartheta^{-1}\omega}\circ E_{a_{0},\omega}\circ E_{a_{1},\vartheta\omega}(e(\omega))

as required. By minimality of the small correlations bundle, the extension property is verified.
∎

Remark 2.45
.

Unfortunately, we are not aware of any way to guarantee that the minimal small correlations bundle embeds into a
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle whose fibers are finite-dimensional. In fact, Fannes Nachtergaele and Werner proceed by requiring finite-dimensionality in the definition of a
C^{*}
-Finitely Correlated State (see
[
30
, Definition 2.4]
). The general question of how to embed an operator system in a
C^{*}
-algebra beyond our scope, but we point the reader to some primary sources. Apparently, the two canonical constructions are the
universal

[
48
]
and
minimal

[
37
]

C^{*}
-
envelopes
. In each case, there are simple examples of finite-dimensional operator systems where the universal/minimal
C^{*}
-algebras are necessarily infinite dimensional (see
[
48
, Example 5.3]
or
[
37
, Example 5.2]
, respectively). In a related note, it is an outstanding open question as to whether or not every FCS is a
C^{*}
FCS (see
[
85
,
86
]
).

We have shown that any TCVS admits a bundle with a transfer apparatus where the fibers are
C^{*}
-algebras. Following
[
30
]
, let us define the
Covariant Matrix Product States (CMPS)

\psi_{\omega}
to be those for which there is a
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle which implements
\psi_{\omega}
through a transfer apparatus where each fiber is finite-dimensional.

Proposition 2.46
.

The set of CMPS is a weakly-* dense convex subset of
\mathfrak{C}
as in Lemma
2.38
.

Proof.

The proof is a corollary of the proof of Theorem
2.41
gotten by noticing that the aromatic bundle of a
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle is still a
C^{*}
-algebraic bundle. The rest of the proof of Theorem
2.41
does not depend on the multiplication on the fibers.
∎

3
An Independent, Identically Distributed AKLT Model

In this section, we construct and analyze an explicit model of a Translation Co-variant State with small correlations. Certainly, one can recover from our theory the any deterministic translation-invariant MPS by taking all the transfer operators to be constant. Here, we reify a TCVS with ‘strong’ on-site disorder. We accomplish this by beginning with the one-parameter family of states introduced in
[
30
]
, and pick a random value of the parameter at each site. In general, the dimension of the small correlation bundle need not be constant, and our model admits a Banach bundle description that is not locally trivial. We build on the existing theory to show that our IID-AKLT model admits a nearest-neighbor parent Hamiltonian that is measurable in the parameter and translation co-variant. Finally we use this to show the surprising fact that with probability one, the bulk gap of our IID-AKLT model vanishes.

3.1
Preliminary Observations

An interesting family of examples comes from the one-parameter family of
C^{*}
-Finitely Correlated States studied in
[
30
, Examples 1-5, 7-8]
. Let us recall the setting, and then examine its properties from our point of view.

Consider the spin-1 chain where
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{M}_{3}
consists of
3\times 3
matrices at every site. Equip the local Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with the basis of spin states
|-1\rangle,|0\rangle,|1\rangle
. Similarly, equip
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with the spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
basis
|-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle,|+\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle
. Following
[
30
, Example 1]
, for every
\omega\in[0,\pi)
, define the mapping
V_{\omega}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
via

\begin{split}V_{\omega}|\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle&=\cos(\omega)|1,-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle-\sin(\omega)|0,\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle\\
V_{\omega}|-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle&=\sin(\omega)|0,-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle-\cos(\omega)|-1,\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle\end{split}

(3.1)

We may realize
V_{\omega}
as a
6\times 2
matrix as (with respect to the convention that
|+\frac{1}{2}\rangle=[1\,0]^{T},|-\frac{1}{2}\rangle=[0\,1]^{T},|+\rangle=[1\,0\,0]^{T}
, etc.)

V_{\omega}^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\cos(\omega)&-\sin(\omega)&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\sin(\omega)&-\cos(\omega)&0\end{bmatrix}\,.

(3.2)

We will sometimes write
s=\sin(\omega)
and
c=\cos(\omega)
for convenience. We may define the transfer operators

E_{a,\omega}=V^{*}_{\omega}(a\otimes(\,\cdot\,))V_{\omega}

(3.3)

which have continuously varying entries in
\omega
and are completely positive whenever
a\in(\mathbb{M}_{3})_{+}
. One checks

E_{\mathbbm{1},z}(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}=E_{\mathbbm{1},z}^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1})\,,

where the dagger denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint of
E_{\mathbbm{1},z}
. Thus
E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}
is unit and trace preserving for all
\delta\in[0,\pi)
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let
\vec{z}:=(z_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence of angles in
[0,\pi)
, where we identify
\pi
and
0
. Define the state
\nu_{\vec{z}}
on
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
by the equations:

\nu_{\vec{z}}(a_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m})=\frac{1}{2}\tr\left[E_{a_{n},\delta_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{a_{m},\delta_{m}}(\mathbbm{1})\right]

(3.4)

where the
E_{a,\delta}
are defined in equation (
3.3
) for
a\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
and
\delta\in[0,\pi)
.

Since
E_{\mathbbm{1},z}
is unital and trace-preserving, the formula (
3.4
) is consistent with
*
-homomorphisms associated to volumetric inclusions. By Takeda’s theorem (Theorem
1.23
), we have a well-defined bulk state on all
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
, whose coefficients in the computational basis are given by a matrix product state expansion with site-dependent angles. The map
E_{\mathbbm{1},z}
plays a distinguished role in the following and so we shall write
\phi_{z}
to emphasize this.

Recall that with respect to the Hilbert-Schmit orntho-normal basis
\{\mathbbm{1},\sigma^{z},\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-}\}\subset\mathbb{M}_{2}
formed from the Pauli Matrices, that the matrix
M
defining
\phi_{z}
is diagonal with

M=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&\sin^{2}(z)-\cos^{2}(z)&&\\
&&-\sin^{2}(z)&\\
&&&-\sin^{2}(z)\end{bmatrix}\,.

(3.5)

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\phi_{z}=E_{\mathbbm{1},z}\in B(\mathbb{M}_{2})
be the transfer operator defined above. Fix constants
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<\frac{\pi}{2}
and let
\vec{z}\in((0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi))^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Put
\Phi_{m,n}=\phi_{z_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{z_{n}}
and let
T:\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}
be the replacement channel
T(b)=\tr_{2}(b)\mathbbm{1}
, where
\tr_{2}
is the normalized trace. For every
\mu\in(0,1)
and
x\in[m,n]
, there is a a constant
c_{\mu,x}>0
so that

\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|=\max\left\{\prod_{j=m}^{n}|\cos(2z_{j})|,\prod_{j=m}^{n}\sin^{2}(z_{j})\right\}\leq c_{\mu,x}\,\cdot\mu^{n-m}

(3.6)

Proof.

The first equality is essentially immediate from the fact that
\Phi_{n,m}
is diagonalizable. To obtain the upper estimate, one first checks that
\Phi_{m,n}
is
eventually strictly positive
in the sense of
[
55
, Assumption 1]
. Fix an
\omega\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
. We aim to show that
\phi_{\omega}
is a strictly positive map in the sense that
A\in(\mathbb{M}_{2})_{+}
implies
\phi_{\omega}(A)>0
.

Recall
[
5
,
6
]
, a nonzero
2\times 2
matrix is positive semidefinite if and only if
\tr A=a+c>0
and
\det A\geq 0
, and moreover if
A
is positive definite if and only if
\det A>0
. Let
A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
b^{*}&c\end{bmatrix}
be a positive semidefinite matrix which is nonzero. Notice, since
\tr
is faithful,
a+c>0
, whence at least one of them is nonzero. Similarly,
\det A=ac-|b|^{2}\geq 0
.

One can compute explicitly

\det\phi_{\omega}(A)=(a^{2}+c^{2})\sin^{2}\omega\,\cos^{2}\omega+a\cos^{4}\omega+(ac-|b|^{2})\sin^{4}\omega>0

for any value of
\omega
in range provided one of
a,c
is nonzero. Since
\phi_{\omega}
is trace preserving, we know that
\phi_{\omega}
is a strictly positive map for any
\omega
in range. Moreover, since
\phi_{\omega}
is trace-preserving, it is faithful, thus
\ker\phi_{\omega}\cap(\mathbb{M}_{2})_{+}=\{0\}
, and similarly since
\phi_{\omega}
is unital the Schrödinger picture adjoint satisfies
\ker\phi_{\omega}^{\dagger}\cap(M_{2})_{+}=\{0\}
.

Now, the upper estimate follows from the analysis leading up to
[
55
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Notation 3.3
.

We note that with respect to the canonical bases, one may define the matrices
X^{-}(z),X^{0}(z),X^{+}(z)
as follows

[X^{(j)}(z)]_{k,\ell=1}^{2}:=\langle j,k|V_{z}|\ell\rangle;\quad j\in\{+,0,-\}

One obtains the expressions:

X^{+}(z):=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
\cos(z)&0\\
\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,X^{0}(z):=\begin{bmatrix}-\sin(z)&0\\
0&\sin(z)\\
\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,X^{-}(z):=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\cos(z)\\
0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}

(3.7)

We remark that
[
30
]
define matrices
v^{(j)}
similarly, and the relation between our notations is that
X^{(j)}(z)=[v^{(j)}(z)]^{*}
. When convenient, we will often write
X^{(j)}_{z}
instead of using function notation.

Notation 3.4
.

Consider an interval
[m,n]\subset\mathbb{Z}
. We define a family of mappings from
\mathbb{M}_{2}
into the local Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{[m,n]}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\otimes(n-m)}
via

\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(b)=\sum_{i_{m},\dots,i_{n}\in\{-,0,+\}}\,\tr[bX^{i_{n}}_{z_{n}}X^{i_{n-1}}_{z_{n-1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot X^{i_{m}}_{z_{m}}]\,|i_{m},\dots,i_{n}\rangle\,

(3.8)

where
|i_{m},\dots,i_{n}\rangle
is the notation for the computational basis in
\mathcal{H}^{[m,n]}
.

We remark that the convention of some authors is to find matrices
Y^{(j)}
that represent the state where the multiplication inside the trace is taken in the order
Y^{i_{m}}Y^{i_{m-1}}\cdots Y^{i_{n}}
(see e.g.
[
68
,
83
]
). Since we appeal to formulas in
[
30
]
, we shall conform to their conventions for the convenience of the reader.

Notice that for all
\vec{z}\in[0,\pi)^{\mathbb{Z}}
the composition of transfer operators given by

E_{a_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m},\vec{z}}^{[n,m]}:=E_{a_{n},z_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{a_{m},z_{m}}:\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}

(3.9)

for
a_{n},\dots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{3}
extends to a linear operator on
\mathcal{A}_{[n,m]}
linearly (see Lemma
2.31
, the proof is essentially the same).

We have the following lemmas.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
z\mapsto(V_{z}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{C}^{k})
be the continuous isometry-valued function defined in Equation (
3.2
). Let
m<n
be integers and
\vec{z}\in[0,\pi)^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\{|j\rangle\}_{j\in\{-,0,+\}}
denote the canoncal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
\{|k\rangle\}_{k\in\{-\frac{1}{2},+\frac{1}{2}\}}
denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. With notation as in
3.3
and
3.4
, and equation (
3.9
) above, the following hold.

a.)

For all
m<n\in\mathbb{Z}
and every
a\in\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}\cong(\mathbb{M}_{3})^{\otimes(n-m)}
the transfer operator
E^{[m,n]}_{a,\vec{z}}:\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}
may be expressed as

E^{[m,n]}_{a,\omega}(b)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{m},\cdots,i_{n}\\
j_{m},\dots,j_{n}\end{subarray}}\langle i_{m},\dots,i_{n}|\,a\,|j_{m},\dots,j_{n}\rangle(X^{i_{m}}_{z_{m}})^{*}\cdots(X^{i_{n}}_{z_{n}})^{*}\,\cdot b\,\cdot X^{j_{n}}_{z_{n}}\cdots X^{j_{m}}_{z_{m}}\,.

(3.10)

b.)

One has that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
, the following expectation value on
\mathcal{H}^{[m,n]}
has the expansion

\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x)|\,a\,|\Gamma_{\vec{z}}^{[m,n]}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{[m,n]}}=\sum_{j_{1},\,j_{2}\in\{\pm\frac{1}{2}\}}\langle j_{1}|\,E^{[m,n]}_{a,\vec{z}}\left(\,x^{*}|j_{1}\rangle\langle j_{2}|y\,\right)\,|j_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

(3.11)

In particular, putting
\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2}
, and letting
\langle x,y\rangle_{\rho}=\tr[\rho x^{*}y]
, we have the estimate

|\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x),\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle x,y\rangle_{\rho}|\leq 2\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|\,.

(3.12)

c.)

For all
\vec{z}\in((0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi))^{\mathbb{Z}}
and all
m\leq n
, and all
x,y\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
, we have

|\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{z}(x)|a|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(y)\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\nu(a)\langle x|y\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}|\leq\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|\,\|a\|\|x\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

(3.13)

More generally, if
\supp(a)=[m^{\prime},n^{\prime}]\subset[m,n]
, then, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
with
\|x\|_{\mathrm{HS}},\|y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 1
, we have
