|\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{z}(x)|\mathbbm{1}_{[m,m^{\prime}-1]}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{[n^{\prime}+1,n]}|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(y)\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\nu(a)\langle x|y\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}|\leq(\|\Phi_{m,m^{\prime}-1}-T\|+\|\Phi_{n^{\prime}+1,n}-T\|)\,\|a\|

(3.14)

The proofs are essentially the same as those in Lemma 5.1 of
[
30
]
so we will not repeat them. We do note, however, that a factor of
\tfrac{1}{2}
in
3.14
comes from the fact that
E_{\mathbbm{1},z}
is trace preserving, whence the density matrix it preserves is
\tfrac{1}{2}
.

We now aim to show that the
\Gamma^{[m,n]}
-maps are injective for every
\vec{z}\in((0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi))^{\mathbb{Z}}
whenever
n-m\geq 2
. In the language of
[
30
]
, we will show that
\nu_{\vec{z}}
has an interaction length of
2
for all
\vec{z}
which avoid
0
and
\frac{\pi}{2}
.

Recall
[
30
,
68
]
that the subspace

\Gamma^{[j,j+1]}_{\vec{z}}(\mathbb{M}_{2}):=\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}=\left\{\sum_{i_{j},i_{j+1}}\tr(bX_{z_{j+1}}^{i_{j+1}}X^{i_{j}}_{z_{j}})|i_{j},i_{j+1}\rangle\colon b\in\mathbb{M}_{2}\right\}\subset(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{j}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{j+1}\,.

(3.15)

The subspace
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
determines the support of a positive operator
P_{j,j+1}
so that the two-point restriction of
\nu_{\vec{z}}
satisfies the condition
\nu_{\vec{z}}(P_{j,j+1})=0
. We aim to show that for any
\vec{z}\in((0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi))^{\mathbb{Z}}
that
\Gamma^{[j,j+1]}_{\vec{z}}
is injective. That is, the rank of
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
is equal to four.

Rearranging equation (
3.15
) a bit, we see that

\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}=\underset{0\leq k,\ell\leq 2}{\spn}\left\{\sum_{i_{j},i_{j+1}}\langle k|X^{i_{j+1}}_{z_{j+1}}X^{i_{j}}_{z_{j}}|\ell\rangle\,\cdot|i_{j},i_{j+1}\rangle\right\}

Therefore, a basis for
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
can be constructed from the corresponding entries of the
3\times 3
array of matrix products
(X^{i_{j+1}}_{z_{j+1}}X^{i_{j}}_{z_{j}})_{i_{j},i_{j+1}\in\{-,0,+\}}
. This results in the following basis vectors which vary as functions in
\vec{z}
and
j
:

\begin{split}|\phi_{11}(j)\rangle&=s_{j}s_{j+1}|00\rangle-c_{j}c_{j+1}|+-\rangle\\
|\phi_{12}(j)\rangle&=c_{j}s_{j+1}|-0\rangle-s_{j}c_{j+1}|0-\rangle\\
|\phi_{21}(j)\rangle&=c_{j}s_{j+1}|+0\rangle-s_{j}c_{j+1}|0+\rangle\\
|\phi_{22}(j)\rangle&=s_{j}s_{j+1}|00\rangle-c_{j}c_{j+1}|-+\rangle\\
\end{split}

(3.16)

where we have written
c_{j}=\cos(z_{j})
and
s_{j}=\sin(z_{j})
for convenience. We have therefore shown the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\vec{z}
be any bi-infinite sequence taking values in in
(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
and let
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
be the ground state space associated to
\Gamma^{[j,j+1]}_{\vec{z}}
. Then the rank of
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
is identically four for
\vec{z}
in range.

As noted in
[
30
]
, injectivity of the
\Gamma^{[n,m]}
maps at a short distance does not guarantee they remain injective as
|n-m|
increases. In order to check this, we must verify that the frustration free intersection property is verified. Recall that the three-site ground-state space is given by

\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1,j+2}:=\spn\left\{\sum_{i_{j},i_{j+1},i_{j+2}}\tr\left[bX^{i_{j+2}}_{z_{j+2}}\,X^{i_{j+1}}_{z_{j+1}}\,X^{i_{j}}_{z_{j}}\right]|i_{j},i_{j+1},i_{j+2}\rangle\right\}

(3.17)

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\vec{z}=(z_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence taking values in
(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
and let
\nu_{\vec{z}}
be the corresponding state constructed as in (
3.4
). Then, for any site
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{j+2}\cap(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{x}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{j+1,j+2}=\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1,j+2}.

Thus, the interaction length of
\nu_{\vec{z}}
is
\ell=2
. Furthermore, for any
\vec{z}
in range and
n-m>2
, one has

\mathcal{G}_{[m,n]}=\bigcap_{j=m}^{n-2}\mathcal{H}_{[m,j]}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{[j+2,n]}

(3.18)

where we adopt the convention that when
j=m
, the symbol
\mathcal{H}_{[m,m]}
is interpereted to mean
(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\otimes 0}
.

Proof.

After possibly re-indexing, it is sufficient to show that the intersection property holds for three consecutive sites
x,x+1
and
x+2
which we will abbreviate as
1,2
and
3
for convenience. We therefore adopt the notational convention that
c_{1}=\cos(\delta_{x})
,
c_{2}=\cos(\delta_{x+1})
,
c_{3}=\cos(\delta_{x+2})
, and similarly for the sine values.

Observe that
\mathcal{G}_{1,2,3}\subset\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{3}\cap(\mathbb{C}^{3})_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}
. Furthermore, the explicit computation of the basis for
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}
in (
3.16
), implies
\dim(\mathcal{G}_{1,2,3})\geq\max\{\dim(\mathcal{G}_{1,2}),\dim(\mathcal{G}_{2,3})\}
. Thus, to show equality of the spaces we need to show

\dim(\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}\cap\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3})\leq\max\{\dim(\mathcal{G}_{1,2}),\dim(\mathcal{G}_{2,3})\}\,.

To do so, we aim to find a convenient decomposition of each space that will allow us to directly compute the dimension of the overlap.

First, notice
S^{z}_{3}
commutes with the total spin operator
S^{2}_{1,2}
, and similarly with
S^{z}_{1}
and
S_{2,3}^{2}
. Thus, we may decompose
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}=\bigoplus_{k=-2}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(k)}
where each subspace
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(k)}
is contained in the eigensubspace of
S^{2}_{1,2}
corresponding to
k
. However, we note that from (
3.16
) that
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(\pm 2)}=\{0\}
. Thus,

\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}=\bigoplus_{k=-1}^{1}\bigoplus_{m=-1}^{1}\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(k)}\otimes\mathbb{C}|m\rangle=\bigoplus_{k+m=-2}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(k)}\otimes\mathbb{C}|m\rangle\,.

We may perform a similar decomposition on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}
.

We have therefore written
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}
in the eigenbasis of
S_{1}^{z}\otimes S_{2}^{z}\otimes S_{3}^{z}
. But this operator commutes with the total spin
S^{2}_{1,2,3}
. Therefore, each subspace
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}|m\rangle
is contained in the eigenspace corresponding to the quantum number
k+m
, and similarly for
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}
.

All this to say that the intersection admits a direct sum decomposition

\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}\cap\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}=\bigoplus_{k+m=-2}^{2}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}_{1,2}\otimes|m\rangle\cap|m\rangle\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}^{(k)}.

Now, to estimate the dimension of
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}\cap\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}
it is sufficient to estimate the dimension of each direct summand corresponding to one of the spin values
k=\pm 2,\pm 1,0
. To do so, we will explicitly compute a basis in both the left-hand sum
L(k):=\bigoplus_{m\colon k+m\in\{\pm 1,0\}}\mathcal{G}_{1,2}^{(k)}\otimes|m\rangle
and the right-hand summand
R(k):=\bigoplus_{m\colon k+m\in\{\pm 1,0\}}|m\rangle\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2,3}^{(k)}
. We use these basis expansions to determine the largest possible dimension of
L(k)\cap R(k)
(namely, by symbolically computing the null space of an appropriate matrix). To make the resulting tables more legible, we adopt the convention that
\ell_{x,y}:=\phi_{x,y}(j)
and
r_{x,y}:=\phi_{x,y}(j+1)
, where
\phi_{x,y}
is defined in equation (
3.16
) above.

In the case of spin
+2
, the resulting subspaces
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbb{C}|+\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}|+\rangle\otimes\mathcal{G}_{j+1,j+2}^{(1)}
are one-dimensional spanned by
|\ell_{21},+\rangle=|\phi_{21}(j),+\rangle
and
|+,r_{21}\rangle=|+,\phi_{21}(j+1)\rangle
. Expanding both vectors, one obtains

\begin{split}|\phi_{21}(j),+\rangle&=c_{1}s_{2}|+0+\rangle-s_{1}c_{2}|0++\rangle\\
|+,\phi_{21}(j+1)\rangle&=c_{2}s_{3}|++0\rangle-s_{2}c_{3}|+0+\rangle\,\end{split}

(3.19)

Since
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(0,\pi)
, it must be that
c_{1}s_{2}\neq 0
, and
c_{2}s_{3}\neq 0
, whence these vectors are are linearly independent and therefore have no common vector in their intersection.

The case of spin
-2
is similar: the left-hand side is spanned by
|\phi_{12}(j),-\rangle=c_{1}s_{2}|-0-\rangle-s_{1}c_{2}|0--\rangle
and the right hand side is spanned by
|-,\phi_{12}(j+1)\rangle=c_{2}s_{3}|--0\rangle-s_{2}c_{3}|-0-\rangle
, which are linearly independent.

Now, in the case that
k+m=+1
, the allowed values of are as follows:
(k,m)=(1,0),(0,1)
. We re-organize this information by
m
-value in the following table. The resulting basis elements of
L(+1)
with total spin 1 can be written as

m

basis vector of
L(+1)
with spin 1

-

\varnothing

0

v_{1}:=|\ell_{21},0\rangle=-s_{j}c_{j+1}|0+0\rangle+c_{j}s_{j+1}|+00\rangle

+

v_{2}:=|\ell_{11},+1\rangle=s_{j}s_{j+1}|00+\rangle-c_{j}c_{j+1}|+-+\rangle

v_{3}:=|\ell_{22},+\rangle=s_{j}s_{j+1}|00+\rangle-c_{j}c_{j+1}|-++\rangle

Similarly for
R(+1)
, we get a family of basis vectors:

m

basis vector of
R(+1)
with spin 1

-

\varnothing

0

w_{1}:=|0,r_{21}\rangle=-s_{j+1}c_{j+2}|00+\rangle+c_{j+1}s_{j+2}|0+0\rangle

+

w_{2}:=|+1,r_{11}\rangle=s_{j+1}s_{j+2}|+00\rangle-c_{j+1}c_{j+2}|++-\rangle

w_{3}:=|+1,r_{22}\rangle=s_{j+1}s_{j+2}|+00\rangle-c_{j+1}c_{j+2}|+-+\rangle

Now, if there is a vector in
L(+1)\cap R(+1)
, then it is a linear combination of
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and
\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\}
. By passing to the spin-z basis we observe that such a vector is an element of the null space of the
6\times 6
matrix in Table
1
(where we have left blank the cells which contain a zero for legibility).

v_{1}

v_{2}

v_{3}

-w_{1}

-w_{2}

-w_{3}

-c_{j+1}s_{j+2}

Table 1:
Spin +1 overlap matrix

Entering this matrix into Mathematica and using the
NullSpace
command returns a null space spanned by the vector:

\nu_{1}:=2s_{3}v_{1}+c_{3}v_{2}=2s_{1}w_{1}-c_{1}w_{3}\,

(3.20)

Fully expanding we see that

\nu_{1}=-2s_{1}c_{2}s_{3}|0+0\rangle+2c_{1}s_{2}s_{3}|+00\rangle+s_{1}s_{2}c_{3}|00+\rangle-c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}|+-+\rangle\,,

from which we see that the only way for the dimension to collapse to zero is if each coefficient equals zero, which is not possible for our choices of
z_{1},z_{2}z_{3}
. Thus, we conclude
\dim(L(+1)\cap R(+1))=1
.

Repeating this process in the case of total spin
-1
, the resulting
6\times 6
matrix given in Table
2
. Performing the same computation as above, we see that the null space has dimension at most one and is generated by the vector

\nu_{-1}=-c_{3}|\ell_{22},-\rangle-s_{3}|\ell_{12},0\rangle=-c_{1}|-,r_{11}\rangle-s_{1}|0,r_{12}\rangle

(3.21)

Similarly expanding, we see that

\nu_{-1}=c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}|-+-\rangle-s_{1}s_{2}c_{3}|00-\rangle-c_{1}s_{2}s_{3}|-00\rangle+s_{1}c_{2}s_{3}|0-0\rangle\,,

for which we see the only way to make all four coefficients vanish is if
z_{1}=z_{2}=z_{3}=\frac{\pi}{2}
. Therefore
\dim(L(-1)\cap R(-1))=1
except when there is a triple of
\pi/2
’s

Finally, the case of spin 0 results in a
7\times 8
matrix given in Table
3
. Performing the null space computation here results in the following vectors which span the intersection:

\begin{split}v_{0}^{1}&:=-c_{3}s_{1}|\ell_{21},-\rangle-s_{1}s_{3}|\ell_{11},0\rangle=s_{1}^{2}|0,r_{11}\rangle-c_{1}s_{1}|+,r_{12}\rangle\\
v_{0}^{2}&:=c_{1}s_{3}|\ell_{22},0\rangle+c_{1}c_{3}|\ell_{12},+\rangle=c_{1}^{2}|-,r_{21}\rangle-c_{1}s_{1}|0,r_{22}\rangle\end{split}

(3.22)

Similarly, we see that

\begin{split}\nu_{0}^{1}&=s_{1}^{2}c_{2}c_{3}|0+-\rangle-c_{1}s_{1}s_{2}c_{3}|+0-\rangle-s_{1}^{2}s_{2}s_{3}|000\rangle+s_{1}c_{1}c_{2}s_{3}|+-0\rangle\\
\nu_{0}^{2}&=c_{1}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}|000\rangle-c_{1}^{2}c_{2}s_{3}|-+0\rangle+c_{1}^{2}s_{2}c_{3}|-0+\rangle-c_{1}s_{1}c_{2}c_{3}|0-+\rangle\end{split}

(3.23)

Therefore, in the case that
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\not\in\{0,\frac{\pi}{2}\}
, we have

4\leq\dim(\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1.j+2})\leq\dim(\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}\cap\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{j+1,j+2})\leq 1+2+1=4\,.\qed

Remark 3.8
.

In the event that there is a degenerate sequence of values, e.g.
z_{j}=0,z_{j+1}=\frac{\pi}{2},z_{j+2}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, then the dimension of the two-point ground state spaces and the three point ground state spaces can all be different. In the example we just mentioned, one calculates that
\dim(\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1})=0
and
\dim(\mathcal{G}_{j+1,j+2})=4
, while
\dim(\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1,j+2})=3
. Therefore, in the degenerate case the interaction length must be greater than 2, if it exists at all.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
\vec{z}
be a bi-infinite sequence with entries in
(0,\pi/2)\cup(\pi/2,\pi)
, and let
\nu_{\vec{z}}
be the corresponding state on
(\mathcal{A})_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, the parent Hamiltonian, h, of
\nu_{\vec{z}}
is given by

h_{j,j+1}=\text{proj}(\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}^{\perp})

(3.24)

One can explicitly calculate the vectors whose outer products define
h_{j,j+1}
and we do so in Appendix
A
.

Proof.

The existence of the parent Hamiltonian is well established in
[
68
]
. One may explicitly compute the basis of
\mathcal{G}_{j,j+1}
and the basis of
\mathcal{G}_{j+1,j+2}
in terms of the
\{|\phi_{k\ell}(j)\rangle\}_{k,\ell=1,2}
.
∎

Now that we have established that the interaction length for the
\nu_{\vec{z}}
is two for suitable
\vec{z}
, we aim to show that
\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}
maps in remains injective when
|m-n|
increases in analogy to Lemma 5.3 of
[
30
]
. We recall (see Section 5.8 in
[
41
]
) that the
condition number
of a linear map
L
is the quantity

\kappa(L):=\|L\|\|L^{-1}\|=\inf_{x\neq 0}\frac{\|Lx\|}{\|x\|}

Let

\kappa_{[m,n]}(\vec{z}):=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{M}_{2}}\frac{\|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x)\|^{2}}{\|x\|_{\rho}^{2}}

(3.25)

where
\|x\|_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
, to account for the fact that the density matrix fixed by
E_{\mathbbm{1},\vec{z}}^{\dagger}
is
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2}

Lemma 3.10
.

For a fixed
\vec{z}
in range, the following inequality holds

\kappa_{[m,n]}(\vec{z})\geq(1-\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|)

(3.26)

and furthermore,

\kappa_{[m,n]}(\vec{z})\leq\min\{\kappa_{[m-1,n]}(\vec{z}),\,\kappa_{[m,n+1]}(\vec{z})\}

(3.27)

That is,
\kappa_{[m,n]}
is non-decreasing with respect to the interval length
|m-n|
.

Proof.

Once again the proof of
[
30
, Lemma 5.3]
is robust in this setting, so we will only emphasize the one place where our proof differs. Consider the interval
[m-1,n]
. By Lemma
3.5
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Gamma^{[m-1,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{m-1},\dots,i_{n}}|\tr(xX^{i_{n}}_{z_{n}}\,\cdots X^{i_{m-1}}_{z_{m-1}}))|^{2}=\sum_{i_{m-1}}\sum_{i_{m},\dots,i_{n}}|\tr([X^{i_{m-1}}_{z_{m-1}}x]X^{i_{n}}_{z_{n}}\cdots X^{i_{m}}_{z_{m}}))|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{m-1}}\|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(X^{i_{m-1}}_{z_{m-1}}\,x)\|^{2}\geq\sum_{i_{m-1}}\kappa_{m,n}(\vec{z})\tr(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}[X^{i_{m-1}}_{z_{m-1}}x]^{*}[X^{i_{m-1}}_{z_{m-1}}x])

\displaystyle=\kappa_{m,n}(\vec{z})\sum_{i_{m-1}}\tr[\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}x^{*}X_{i_{m-1}}^{*}X_{i_{m-1}}x]=\kappa_{m,n}(\vec{z})\|x\|_{\rho}^{2},

where we have used the fact that the Kraus matrices defining
E_{\mathbbm{1},\vec{z}}
are trace preserving and unital both.
∎

Let us now conclude with a proof that the
\nu_{\vec{z}}
are pure states for suitable choices of
\vec{z}
. Recall that a state
\varphi
on a
C^{*}
-algebra
C
is pure if and only if whenever
\rho
is a positive linear functional for which
\rho(c^{*}c)\leq\varphi(c^{*}c)
for all
c\in C
, there necessarily exists a
\lambda\in[0,1]
so that
\lambda\varphi=\rho
(see
[
57
, Chapter 5]
).

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\vec{z}
be any bi-infinite sequence taking values in
(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
and let
\nu_{\vec{z}}
be the state as generated in equation
3.4
. Then
\nu_{\vec{z}}
is a pure state on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We largely follow the proof strategy of
[
30
, Theorem 5.7]
. Suppose that
\rho
is a state majorized by
\lambda\nu_{\vec{z}}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Then for every interval
[m,n]
, one has
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}\leq\lambda\nu_{\vec{z}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}
. In particular, this means that
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}(H^{[m,n]})=\sum_{j=m}^{n-1}\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}(h_{j,j+1})=0
. Therefore
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}
is supported on the intersection in the right-hand side of equation (
3.18
). But by choice of
\vec{z}
, we know equation (
3.18
) holds, and therefore
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}
is supported by
\mathcal{G}_{[m,n]}
.

Thus, the density matrix
\hat{\rho}
representing
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}
may be decomposed as

\hat{\rho}=\sum_{k}|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})\rangle\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})|

with
\sum\|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})\|^{2}=\tr\hat{\rho}=1
. Now, we see that for any
a
with finite support
[m^{\prime},n^{\prime}]\subset[m,n]
, and
|m-n|
large enough so that
m^{\prime}\neq m
and
n^{\prime}\neq n
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m,n]}}(a)-\nu_{\vec{z}}(a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{k}\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})|a|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})\rangle-\nu_{\vec{z}}(a)\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{k}\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})|a|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\vec{z}}(x_{k})\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\nu_{\vec{z}}(a)\langle x_{k}|x_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\right|+2\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|\|a\|\sum_{k}\|x_{k}\|^{2}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq(\|\Phi_{m,m^{\prime}-1}-T\|+\|\Phi_{n^{\prime}+1,n}-T\|+\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|)2\|a\|\sum_{k}\|x_{s}\|_{\rho}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|a\|\,\frac{\|\Phi_{m,m^{\prime}-1}-T\|+\|\Phi_{n^{\prime}+1,n}-T\|+\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|}{\kappa_{[m,n]}(\vec{z})}

where we have used the triangle inequality combined with inequality (
3.12
) from Lemma
3.5
part b to obtain the first inequality.

The right-hand side of this can be made arbitrarily small by sending
m\to-\infty
and
n\to\infty
. Therefore, it must be that
\rho|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m^{\prime},n^{\prime}]}}
agrees with
\nu_{\vec{z}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{[m^{\prime},n^{\prime}]}}
. But this forces
\lambda=1
, and so it must be that
\nu_{\vec{z}}
is pure.
∎

3.2
IID AKLT

In this section, we explore the consequences of having IID angles in the parameter space
[0,\pi)
on the parent Hamiltonian
\{h_{j,j+1}(\varphi)\colon j\in\mathbb{Z},\varphi\in[0,\pi)\}
that we obtained in Corollary
3.9
. To set this up, let
\{\theta_{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a family of IID random variables taking values in
[0,\pi)
. Suppose further that
\theta\sim f(x)dx
with a continuous density function
f(x)
with compact support. By the Lemma
1.3
, we can replace the
\vartheta_{j}
’s with an IID family of continuous random variables
\Theta_{j}:\Omega\to[0,\pi)
which are related by an ergodic homeomorphism
\vartheta:\Omega\to\Omega
, and
\Omega
is a compact Hausdorff space. For convenience, we shall write
\Theta_{j}(\omega)=\omega_{j}=\vartheta^{j}\omega_{0}=\Theta_{0}(\vartheta^{j}(\omega))
.

By considering
\omega:=(\omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
as some sequence taking values in
((0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi))^{\mathbb{Z}}
, define the state
\psi_{\omega}:=\nu_{(\omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
as in equation (
3.4
). Then, by Corollary
3.9
, there is a nearest neighbor parent Hamiltonian
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
for which
\psi_{\omega}
is the unique bulk ground state by Theorem
3.11
.

Lemma 3.12
.

In the above situation, the parent Hamiltonian is translation co-variant in the following sense:

h_{j,j+1}(\omega)=h_{j+1,j+2}(\vartheta\omega)\,.

(3.28)

Proof.

This follows from the coordinate expression of
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
in equation (
3.24
).
∎

We recall some notation from
[
61
]
. Let
\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathbb{Z})
denote the finite subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
and let
\Phi:\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\to\bigcup_{Z\in\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathbb{Z})}\mathcal{A}_{Z}
be a mapping with self-adjoint images
\Phi(Z)=\Phi(Z)^{*}\in\mathcal{A}_{Z}
. Recall that for our model, the single site algebra is
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{M}_{3}
. For some
\lambda>0
, the
\lambda
-norm of
\Phi
is the quantity

\|\Phi\|_{\lambda}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{X\Subset\mathbb{Z}}9^{|X|}|X|\|\Phi(X)\|e^{\lambda\diam(X)}

(3.29)

We remark that the numerical quantity comes from the fact that the dimension of the single-site Hilbert space for the AKLT model is
3
. In
[
61
]
, this is replaced with a more general bound.

Note that since our parent Hamiltonian
h
is nearest neighbor, for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sum in the
\lambda
-norm collapses to a sum over two sets, namely
\{x,x+1\}
and
\{x-1,x\}
. Then since each
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
is a projector,
\|h_{j,j+1}(\omega)\|\equiv 1
. Therefore, the
\lambda
-norm is finite and in fact we have the following.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\{h_{j,j+1}(\omega)\colon j\in\mathbb{Z},\omega\in\Omega\}
be the IID parent Hamiltonian given by equation (
3.24
). Then,

\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\|h\|_{\lambda}=324e^{2\lambda};\,\forall\lambda>0

(3.30)

Now, we consider the quantum spin system with local Hamiltonians given by

H^{\Lambda}(\omega)=\sum_{Z\subset\Lambda}h_{Z(\omega)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
\{j,j+1\}\subset\Lambda\end{subarray}}h_{j,j+1}(\omega)\in\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}\text{ where }\Lambda\Subset\mathbb{Z}\,.

(3.31)

Let
\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}:=e^{itH^{\Lambda}(\omega)}(\,\cdot\,)e^{-itH^{\Lambda}(\omega)}
be the dynamics generated by
H^{\Lambda}(\omega)
. We recall that
\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}
is a one-parameter group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
with respect to
t
. We now come to an interesting observation.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
H^{\Lambda}(\omega)
be the family of local Hamiltonians given by the IID parent Hamiltonians. Let
\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}
denote the local dynamics generated by
H^{\Lambda}(\omega)
. Then, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}=\tau_{k}\circ\alpha^{\Lambda-k}_{t,\vartheta^{k}}\circ\tau_{-k}

(3.32)

Proof.

Recall that the nearest neighbor terms satisfy

h_{j,j+1}(\omega)=h_{j+k,j+k}(\vartheta^{k}\omega)\,.

which is immediate from Lemma
3.12
. Therefore,

\tau_{k}(H^{\Lambda}(\omega))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
\{j,j+1\}\subset\Lambda\end{subarray}}\tau_{k}(h_{j,j+1}(\omega))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
\{j,j+1\}\subset\Lambda\end{subarray}}h_{j+k,j+1+k}(\vartheta^{k}\omega))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
\{j,j+1\}\subset\Lambda-k\end{subarray}}h_{j,j+1}(\vartheta^{k}\omega)=H^{\Lambda-k}(\vartheta^{k})

Now, since
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
is a matrix algebra, the unitary
e^{-itH^{\Lambda}(\omega)}
is just given by its Taylor series, and we observe that

\tau_{k}(e^{-itH^{\Lambda}(\omega)})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-it)^{n}}{n!}\tau_{k}(H^{\Lambda}(\omega))^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-it)^{n}}{n!}(H^{\Lambda-k}(\vartheta^{k}\omega))^{n}=e^{-itH^{\Lambda-k}(\vartheta^{k}\omega)}\,.

Now, for any
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\loc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and any
\Lambda\supset\supp(x)
we have that

\displaystyle\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{itH^{\Lambda}(\omega)}\qed

Furthermore, since
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
is nearest neighbor with a constant operator norm for all
\omega
,
[
59
]
says that the Thermodynamic limit
\alpha^{Z}_{t;\omega}\in\Aut(\mathcal{A}_{Z})
exists strongly and forms a strongly continuous group of automorphisms. In fact, given any increasing sequence of finite volumes
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\Lambda_{n}\subset\cdots
so that
\bigcup_{n}\Lambda_{n}=\mathbb{Z}
, then the following limit exists for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\loc}

\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\omega}(a)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha^{\Lambda_{n}}_{t;\omega}(a)

(3.33)

but by the co-variance property in equation
3.32
we obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 3.15
.

The thermodynamic limit of the local dynamics
\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}
is a strongly continuous semigroup of automorphisms in
t
, and the following co-variance condition holds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\omega\in\Omega
:

\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\omega}=\tau_{k}\circ\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\vartheta^{k}\omega}\circ\tau_{-k}

(3.34)

Moreover, the state
\nu_{\omega}
obtained by applying equation (
3.4
) to
\vec{\omega}
is a pure state satisfying

\nu_{\omega}\circ\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\omega}=\nu_{\omega},\,\forall\omega\in\Omega

Proof.

All we need to show is the ‘moreover’ part of the statement. Purity follows immediately from Theorem
3.11
. The fact that
\psi_{\omega}
is invariant for the dynamics follows by the fact that
\psi_{\omega}(h_{j,j+1}(\omega))=0
for all
j
, by construction.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\omega}
be the disordered thermodynamic limit of the local dynamics
\{\alpha^{\Lambda}_{t;\omega}\}
defined with respect to the IID nearest neighbor Hamiltonian from (
3.24
). Let
\psi_{\omega}
be the invariant state gotten by applying equation
3.4
to
\vec{\omega}
and let also
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\Psi_{\omega})
be the GNS representation of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with respect to
\psi_{\vec{\omega}}
. Let also
S_{t;\omega}
denote the one-parameter group induced on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
by
\alpha^{\mathbb{Z}}_{t;\omega}
. There exist unitaries
\{U_{k}:\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\to\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
so that the GNS Hamiltonian
H^{GNS}_{\omega}
generating
S_{t;\omega}
is
\vartheta
-covariant. That is,

H_{\omega}^{GNS}=U_{k}^{*}H_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}^{GNS}U_{k}\,;\,\forall\omega\in\Omega,k\in\mathbb{Z}

(3.35)

In particular,
\spec(H^{GNS}_{\omega})
spectrum is constant for almost every
\omega
.

Proof.

It is not difficult to show that the maps defined via

U_{k}\pi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}(a)\Psi_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\mapsto\pi_{\omega}(\tau_{k}(a))\Psi_{\omega}

(3.36)

are densely defined linear operators preserving the inner product, whence the
U_{k}
extend uniquely to a unitary
\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta^{k}\omega}\to\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
for any
\omega
. The relation in (
3.35
) follows from the covariance relation (
3.34
).

From here, the proof follows essentially the same argument as the proof of
[
49
, Theorem 1]
, though are in a case with non-constant fibers. The case of non-constant fibers is treated in a much broader context in
[
51
]
, so we will only sketch the proof.

Recall that the heart of
[
49
, Theorem 1]
is to show that given a random projection
P_{\omega}
, its trace (which is a measurable function) is
\vartheta
-invariant, then to apply this to the spectral decomposition of a covariant random Hamiltonian. In our case, we can achieve essentially the same thing as follows.

Claim 3.16.1
.

Suppose
\omega\mapsto P_{\omega}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
is a function taking values in the projections of the GNS representation of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
so that the mappings
\omega\mapsto\langle[a,\omega]|P_{\omega}|[b,\omega]\rangle
are measurable. Suppose that
U^{*}_{k}P_{\omega}U_{k}=P_{\vartheta\omega}
. Then
\tr[P_{\omega}]
is constant in
\omega
.

First, notice that since
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is an inductive limit (see
[
57
, Chapter 6]
) of tensor powers of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
, it is norm separable. It follows that
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
is separable as a Hilbert space for each
\omega
. Furthermore, the set of equivalence classes
\{[a,\omega]\colon a\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is a dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
. Therefore we may find an orthonormal basis
\{[a_{n},\omega]:a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
for each
\omega
. Notice that for any
[a,\omega]
, we have
U_{1}^{*}[a,\omega]=[\tau_{1}(a),\vartheta\omega]
. Thus the image of an orthonormal basis under
U_{1}^{*}
is necessarily an orthonormal basis again.

All this to say that, since the canonical trace is independent of a choice of orthonormal basis (
[
89
, Theorem 7.11(a)]
), we have that

\displaystyle\tr[P_{\omega}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\langle[a_{j},\omega]|P_{\omega}|[a_{j},\omega]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\omega}}=\sum_{j}\langle[\tau_{1}(a_{j}),\vartheta\omega]|U_{1}P_{\omega}U_{1}^{*}|[\tau_{1}(a_{j}),\vartheta\omega]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta\omega}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\langle[\tau_{1}(a_{j}),\vartheta\omega]|P_{\vartheta\omega}|[\tau_{1}(a_{j}),\vartheta\omega]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta\omega}}=\tr[P_{\vartheta\omega}]\,.

Now, given
H_{\omega}
, one considers the spectral projections
\chi_{(\lambda,\mu)}(H_{\omega})=:P_{\omega}
for
\lambda<\mu
rational numbers. The definition of these projections satisfies the weak-measurability condition of the claim by Stone’s formula
[
89
, Theorem 7.17]
, and the GNS condition. By the claim, these projections have constant trace and now we can employ the rest of the argument from
[
49
]
.
∎

To recap, up to this point we have shown that with respect to our nearest neighbor Hamiltonian
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
, where the parameter values are IID random variables that change spatially in
j
, then
h_{j,j+1}(\omega)
admits a translation co-variant thermodynamic limit state which is a ground state of a co-variant automorphism group of dynamics on the quasilocal algebra of observables. In this case, the spectrum of the GNS Hamiltonian is constant. In particular, we say that the GNS Hamiltonian has a
spectral gap
, if there is
\delta>0
so that the interval
(0,\delta)\subset res(H_{\omega}^{GNS})
the resolvent set. The
bulk gap
is then the constant

\gamma:=\sup\{\delta>0\colon(0,\delta)\subset\res(H_{\omega}^{GNS})\}\,.

since
\spec(H_{\omega}^{GNS})\}
is almost surely constant.

We have arrived at the following theorem

Theorem 3.17

(Bulk Gap Alternative)
.

The bulk gap of the IID AKLT model is constant almost surely.

In fact, the bulk gap closes with probability one as we will show now. We write
S_{x}^{z}
the spin-z matrix for a spin-1 particle at site
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. I.e.

S_{x}^{z}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&-1\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{A}_{\{x\}}\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{Z}

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
\nu_{\omega}
be the state on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
generated by
\varrho_{\vec{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\tr
,
e(\vec{z})=\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2}
and the transfer operators
\{E_{\vec{z},a}\}
as in equation (
3.4
). Let
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\Psi_{\omega})
denote the GNS representation of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with respect to
\nu_{\omega}
. Let
H^{GNS}_{\omega}
denote the densely defined generator of the one-parameter group
(S_{t;\omega}\colon t\in\mathbb{R})\subset B(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
induced by
(\alpha_{t;\omega}^{\mathbb{Z}}:t\in\mathbb{R})
via

S_{t;\omega}\pi_{\omega}(a)\Psi_{\omega}=\pi_{\omega}(\alpha^{Z}_{\omega;t}(a))\Psi_{\omega}\,.

Lastly, let
P_{0}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
\ker(H_{\vec{z}}^{GNS})
. Then,
P_{0}\pi_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z})\,\Psi_{\vec{z}}=0
.

Proof.

Notice that for any
\delta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{pi}{2},\pi)
, the operator
E_{S_{x}^{z},\delta}(\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2})=\cos^{2}(\delta)\sigma^{z}
where
\sigma^{z}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
is the Pauli-z matrix. Examine the inner product

\displaystyle\langle\,\Psi_{\omega}|\pi_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z})\,\Psi_{\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\psi_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z})=\frac{1}{2}\tr[E_{S_{x}^{z},\omega_{x}}(\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2})]=\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}(\omega_{x})\tr[\sigma^{z}]=0\,.

This implies that
\pi_{\omega}(S^{z}_{x})\,\Psi_{\omega}
is orthogonal to the cyclic vector
\Psi_{\omega}
. Since
\psi_{\omega}
is
surely
pure by Theorem
3.11
, we know that
\ker{H^{GNS}}
is one-dimensional. Indeed, since
\psi_{\omega}
is pure, the GNS representation is
irreducible
in the sense that the commutant
\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}})^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1}
(see
[
57
, Theorem 5.1.6]
). It is not difficult to show that if
\eta\in\ker(H^{GNS})
where another vector different from
\Xi_{\omega}
with
S_{t;\omega}\eta=0
, then there is a unitary
u\in\pi_{\omega}(A)^{\prime}
relating the vector state
\langle\eta|\cdot|\eta\rangle
and
\psi_{\omega}
so that
u\eta=\Psi_{\omega}
. But the commutant condition implies that
u
is multiplication by a point on the unit circle, and so it must be that
\eta
differs from
\Psi_{\omega}
only by a phase. Hence
\ker H^{GNS}=\mathbb{C}\Psi_{\omega}
. Thus the projection condition is satisfied.
∎

We have now verified the conditions of
[
61
, Theorem 2]
whence we obtain the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.19
.

If
H^{GNS}_{\delta}
has a spectral gap
\gamma>0
, then for any
\ell\geq 1
,
\lambda>0
and any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z}))|\leq 3e^{-\mu_{\lambda}\ell}\,.

where
\mu=\frac{\gamma\lambda}{4\|h\|_{\lambda}+\gamma}

We are now ready to state our second main result

Theorem 3.20

(Theorem
C
)
.

Suppose that
\{\Theta_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a bi-infinite family of IID random variables on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
. Assume the following

i.)

The family
\Theta_{z}\sim f(x)\frac{dx}{\pi}
and
\supp f\subset[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]
.

ii.)

For all
\delta>0
small enough, assume that
p_{0}(\delta):=\mathbb{P}[\Theta_{0}<\delta]>0
and that
\mathbb{P}[\Theta_{0}=0]=0
.

Let
\nu_{\omega}=\nu_{(\omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
where
\omega_{j}=\Theta_{j}(\omega)=\vartheta^{j}\Theta_{0}(\omega)
be the state constructed as in equation (
3.4
) where
\vartheta
is the ergodic shift. Then the following hold.

a.)

The state
\nu_{\omega}
is a translation covariant state with small correlations. In particular, the triple
(\frac{1}{2}\tr,E_{a,\omega},\mathbbm{1})
defines a transfer apparatus for
\nu_{\omega}
on the trivial bundle
[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]\times\mathbb{M}_{2}
.

b.)

Furthermore
\nu_{\omega}
admits a nearest neighbor parent Hamiltonian
h
which is translation co-variant with respect to the ergodic shift. Thus Corollary
3.16
implies that the GNS Hamiltonian has a spectrum which is constant on a set
\Omega_{s}
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{s})=1
.

c.)

Let
(\delta_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence in
[0,\frac{\pi}{4})
so that
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then,

\mathbb{P}\left(\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N},\,\exists x\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\,|\cos(2\delta_{\ell})|^{\ell}\leq|\psi_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})|\right)=1

(3.37)

d.)

Therefore, taking
a-c.)
together implies that the spectral gap of
H^{GNS}
vanishes on a set with probability one.

e.)

Then, the following limit exists almost surely:

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\log\left(|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}\,S_{x+\ell}^{z})|^{\tfrac{1}{\ell}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\log(\cos(2\omega))\leq-\frac{\pi\|f\|_{\infty}}{4}\,\log(2)

(3.38)

Proof.

a.) The covariance condition follows by construction of the probability space. Similarly, the fact that the trivial bundle implements
\psi_{\omega}
here is by construction.

b.) is immediate from Corollary
3.9
, and Lemma
3.12
.

c.) First, we note that by construction of the
\nu_{\vec{z}}
state, one has

\displaystyle|\psi_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|\tr[E_{S^{z}_{x},\omega_{x}}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega_{x+1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega_{x+\ell-1}}\circ E_{S^{z},\omega_{x+\ell}}(\mathbbm{1}_{2\times 2})]|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}(\omega_{x+\ell})|\tr[E_{S^{z}_{x},\omega_{x}}\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega_{x+1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega_{x+\ell-1}}(\sigma^{z}))]|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cos(\omega_{x+\ell})^{2}\,\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}|\sin^{2}(\omega_{x+j})-\cos^{2}(\omega_{x+j})|\,|\tr[E_{S^{z}_{x},\omega_{x}}(\sigma^{z})]|

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}(\omega_{x})\cos^{2}(\omega_{x+\ell})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}|\sin^{2}(\omega_{x+j})-\cos^{2}(\omega_{x+j})|

(3.39)

Now, since we assumed
\omega_{0}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]
, we know that
1\geq\cos(2\omega_{0})\geq 0
deterministically. Therefore by the power reduction formulas from trigonometry,

|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})|=\left(\frac{1+\cos(2\omega_{x})}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1+\cos(2\omega_{x+\ell})}{2}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}|\cos(2\omega_{x+j})|\geq\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}\cos(2\omega_{x+j})

(3.40)

Now, consider the event
E_{x,\ell}(\delta)=[|\cos(2\delta)|^{\ell}\leq|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})|]
. Notice
\mathbb{P}E_{x,\ell}(\delta)\geq\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{x},\Theta_{x+1},\dots,\Theta_{x+\ell}<\delta)=p_{0}(\delta)^{\ell}>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
sufficiently small by the IID assumption. Since we have

\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})=\nu_{\omega}(\tau_{x}\tau_{-x}S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell}^{z})=\nu_{\vartheta^{x}\omega}(S_{0}^{z}S_{\ell}^{z}),

the identity
E_{\ell,x}(\delta)=\vartheta^{x}E_{0,\ell}(\delta)
holds. Let

A_{\ell}(\delta)=\bigcup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{\ell,x}(\delta)\,

(3.41)

which we note is a
\vartheta
invariant set with
\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell}(\delta))\geq p_{0}(\delta)^{\ell}>0
. So
\mathbb{P}A_{\ell}(\delta)=1
for all
\ell\geq 0
and
\delta>0
by ergodicity. Now, for any sequence
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
we have that

\mathbb{P}\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{\infty}A_{\ell}(\delta_{\ell})=1

since this is a countable intersection of sets with full probability.

d.) Suppose that
H^{GNS}
has a positive spectral gap on a set with probability one, say
\Omega_{G}
. Let

\Omega_{s}=\bigcap_{\ell}(\Omega_{G}\cap A_{\ell}(2^{-\ell-1})).

Then, by part c.), we have that

\mathbb{P}\Omega_{s}=\mathbb{P}[\Omega_{G}\cap\bigcap_{\ell=0}^{\infty}A_{\ell}(2^{-\ell-1})]=1

By Lemma
3.19
combined with the definition of the
A_{\ell}
’s we get that for all length scales
\ell
and almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, there is some
x\in\mathbb{Z}
depending on
\omega
and
\ell
so that

|\cos(2^{-{\ell}})|^{\ell}\leq|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}S_{x+\ell})|\leq 3e^{-\mu_{\lambda}\ell}\,.

(3.42)

Taking
\underset{\ell\to\infty}{\limsup}
of both sides (noting that the upper and lower bound are deterministic) yields a contradiction. Therefore it must be that
\mu_{\lambda}=0
on
\Omega_{s}
, but this is equivalent to
\gamma=0
almost surely, since
\spec(H^{GNS})
is constant by Corollary
3.16
.

Part e.) follows by utilizing Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem
[
24
, Theorem 6.2.1]
. Explicitly, let
g(x)=\log|\cos(2x)|
for
x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]
. Under the assumption that
\omega_{0}\sim f(x)dx
where
f
is a continuous density function, then we note
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
since
f\in L^{1}(dx)
. Indeed,

\int_{0}^{\pi/4}|\log(\cos(2x))|f(x)dx\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi/4}-\log|\cos(2\pi)|dx=\|\rho\|_{\infty}\frac{\pi}{4}\log(2)<\infty\,.

Then, by (
3.39
) and (
3.39
, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell+1}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}f(\omega_{x+j})\leq\log\left(|\nu_{\omega}(S_{x}^{z}\,S_{x+\ell}^{z})|^{\tfrac{1}{\ell}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\ell-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}f(\omega_{x+j})\,.

which holds almost surely. Now, Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem implies that the right- and left-hand sides of the above converge almost surely to a constant
\mathbb{E}[f(\omega)]\leq-\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}\pi}{4}\log(2)
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

We reiterate that the mechanism by which the spectral gap closes is the fact that the probability distribution of
\omega_{0}
is allowed to take arbitrarily small values. A reasonable next question to ask is whether or not the spectral gap closes in the case when
\mathbb{P}[\pi/4>\delta_{1}\geq\omega_{0}\geq\delta_{2}>0]=1
, that is, if
\omega_{0}
is valued in
[\delta_{2},\delta_{1}]
and is not allowed to venture toward degenerate values. It seems likely in this scenario that one could leverage that
gap(E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega})
can be explicitly computed in order to show that
gap(H^{GNS}_{\omega})
is open with large probability. However we shall defer a detailed discussion of this idea for later work.

3.3
Time Reversal Symmetry and an Index Calculation

Another interesting question raised by our disordered AKLT model is whether or not the disorder affects the values of topological indices. Many authors have worked on the classification of symmetry protected states via topological indices in recent years (see the sampling of works
[
8
,
19
,
46
,
64
,
65
,
66
,
75
,
82
,
84
]
). Many (if not all) of the aforementioned works are concerned with unique
gapped
ground states of local Hamiltonians which are protected against the action of some group on a spin system. Due to their explicit nature, Matrix Product State representations have played a crucial role in many of the aforementioned works, and specifically, the AKLT model is known to be protected by several different on-site symmetries. In the following we will show that the matrix product approximation to our disordered AKLT state
\nu_{\omega}
is protected against the
Time Reversal
symmetry which can be represented as an action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on the spin chain (see
[
63
,
71
,
83
]
for more details). Furthermore, we aim to show that
\nu_{\omega}
has a nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index with high probability.

To show that our disordered AKLT state is time-reversal invariant, we find it convenient to compute the coefficients explicitly first.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\nu_{\vec{z}}
be the state from Notation
3.1
, where
\vec{z}
is a bi-infinite sequence in
(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
. Let
L\geq 2
, and let Let
\sigma=(\sigma_{-L},\dots,\sigma_{L+1})
be a spin-z configuration on the interval
[-L,L+1]
(i.e. a choice of basis vector at each site). Let
J_{\sigma}\subset[-L,L+1]
denote the set

J_{\sigma}=\{x\in[-L,L+1]\colon\sigma_{x}\neq 0\}\,.

(3.43)

Let it be understood that
J_{\sigma}^{c}=[-L,L+1]\setminus J
. Then, the vector approximates
\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})
have the following expansion:

1.

In the case that there are no excitations (i.e.
\sigma=(0,0,\dots,0)
, then the associated coefficient is

\tr[X_{0}(z_{L+1})\cdots X_{0}(z_{-L})]=2\,\prod_{x=-L}^{L+1}\sin(z_{x})

(3.44)

2.

More generally, if there is
k\geq 1
so that
|J|=2k
and the signs of
\sigma_{x}
alternate as
x\in J
increases, then

\tr[X_{\sigma_{L+1}}(z_{L+1})\cdots X_{\sigma_{-L}}(z_{-L})]=(-1)^{P_{\sigma}}\prod_{x\in J_{\sigma}}\sigma_{x}\cos(z_{x})\prod_{y\in J_{\sigma}^{c}}\sin(z_{y}),

(3.45)

where we have set

P_{\sigma}=\delta(+,\sigma_{x_{N}})(L+1-x_{N})+\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\delta_{+,j+1}(x_{j+1}-x_{j})+\delta_{+,1}(x_{1}-(-L))

(3.46)

where
\delta_{+,j}=\delta(+1,\sigma_{x_{j}})
is the Kronecker delta.

3.

Otherwise, the coefficient vanishes.

Proof.

First, let us set some notation. We declare
D=E_{22}-E_{11}
the difference of the two
2\times 2
matrix units. Now, consider the case of the zero configuration
\zeta=(0,\dots,0)
. Then each matrix in the trace:
\tr[X_{\zeta_{L+1}}(z_{L+1})\dots X_{\zeta_{-L}}(z_{-L})]
is proportional to
D
. Therefore, we may write

\tr[X_{\zeta_{L+1}}(z_{L+1})\dots X_{\zeta_{-L}}(z_{-L})]=\tr[X_{0}(z_{L+1})\dots X_{0}(z_{-L})]=\tr[D^{2L+2}]\prod_{x=-L}^{L+1}\sin(z_{x})=2\prod_{x=-L}^{L+1}\sin(z_{x})

Notice that for the sites
y\in J_{\sigma}^{c}
, the associated matrix
X_{\sigma_{y}}
is proportional to
D:=E_{22}-E_{11}
, and otherwise the matrices
X_{\sigma_{x_{j}}}
are proportional to one of the matrix units
E_{21}
or
E_{12}
. Write
J=\{x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{N}\}
in increasing order. One can factor out the constants to obtain

\tr[X_{\sigma_{L+1}}(z_{L+1})\cdots X_{\sigma_{-L}}(z_{-L})]=\left(\prod_{y\in J^{c}}\sin(z_{y})\right)\left(\prod_{x\in J}\sigma_{x}\cos(z_{x})\right)\tr Y_{J}

where we we have set

Y_{J}=D^{L+1-x_{N}}E_{\sigma_{x_{n}}}D^{x_{N}-1-(x_{N-1}+1)}E_{\sigma_{x_{N-1}}}\cdots D^{x_{2}-x_{1}}E_{\sigma_{x_{1}}}D^{x_{1}-(-L)}\,.

(3.47)

Now, notice that for any power
p
, one has

\begin{split}D^{p}E_{12}&=(-1)^{p}E_{12}\\
D^{p}E_{21}&=E_{21}\end{split}

Therefore, it is immediate that
Y_{J}
vanishes if there is a pair of subsequent equal indices
\sigma_{x_{j}}=\sigma_{x_{j+1}}
, since this would imply an
E_{12}^{2}
or
E_{21}^{2}
appears in
Y_{J}
.

Continuing in our reduction, we group the matrices in equation (
3.47
) from left to right to see that

Y_{J}=(-1)^{\delta(+,\sigma_{x_{N}})(L+1-x_{N})+\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\delta(+1,\sigma_{x_{j+1}})(x_{j+1}-x_{j})}\tr[ED^{x_{1}-L}]

(3.48)

where
E
is some alternating product of
E_{12}
and
E_{21}
. Recalling that
E_{21}E_{12}=E_{22}
and
E_{12}E_{21}=E_{11}
and that
E_{11}E_{22}=E_{22}E_{11}=0
, we see that
|J|
cannot be odd, otherwise,
E
is the zero matrix, whence the coefficient vanishes.

To recapitulate, we have shown that
\tr[X_{i_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdots X_{i_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})]
equals zero unless
|J|=2k
for some
0\leq k\leq L+1
, and the
\sigma
must be alternating.

This means there are only two options for the value of
\tr[ED^{x_{1}-(-L)}]
. Either

\tr[E_{\sigma}D^{x_{1}-(-L)}]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\tr[E_{11}D^{x_{1}-(-L)}]=(-1)^{x_{1}-(-L)}&\text{ if $x_{1}=+1$}\\
\tr[E_{22}D^{x_{1}-(-L)}]=1&\text{ if $x_{1}=-1$}\\
\end{array}\right.\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
\nu_{\omega}
be the disordered AKLT state we construct above. Then
\nu_{\omega}
is protected by time-reversal symmetry.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that the matrix product state with vectors
\Gamma^{[-l,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})
is protected by time-reversal symmetry for all
L
. Recall that the weak* limit
\lim_{L\to\infty}\langle\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})|\cdot|\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\rangle=\nu_{\omega}
almost surely by Theorem
3.5
part c.). Therefore, it is sufficient to show that the coefficients of the MPS expansion are robust under the action of the symmetry.

Recall that the matrices which generate
\nu_{\omega}
at site
j
are given by

X_{-1}(\omega_{j}):=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
-\cos(\omega_{j})&0\\
\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,X_{0}(\omega_{j}):=\begin{bmatrix}-\sin(\omega_{j})&0\\
0&\sin(\omega_{j})\\
\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,X_{1}(\omega_{j}):=\begin{bmatrix}0&\cos(\omega_{j})\\
0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}

Let
F:\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}
be the quantum channel given by
F(x)=Z^{*}xZ
where the unitary
Z=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{bmatrix}.
One readily checks that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}F(X_{\pm 1}(\omega))=-X_{\mp 1}(\omega)\\
F(X_{0}(\omega))=X_{0}(\omega)\end{array}\right.

for all values of
\omega
.

Lemma
3.22
shows that the nonzero coefficients of
\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{[-L,L+1]}
are functions of spin configurations
\sigma
which have alternating sign and obey
\sum|\sigma_{j}|=2k
for some
0\leq k\leq L+1
. For such a
\sigma
, one has

\displaystyle\tr\left[X_{\sigma_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdots X_{\sigma_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})\right]

\displaystyle=\tr\left[Z^{*}ZX_{\sigma_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdot Z^{*}ZX_{\sigma_{L}}(\omega_{L})\cdots Z^{*}ZX_{\sigma_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})\right]

\displaystyle=\tr[F(X_{\sigma_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1}))\cdots F(X_{\sigma_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|J_{\sigma}|}\tr[X_{-\sigma_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdots X_{-\sigma_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})]

\displaystyle=\tr[X_{-\sigma_{{L+1}}}(\omega_{L+1})\dots X_{-\sigma_{{-L}}}(\omega_{-L})]

Since
J_{\sigma}
is the set of points in
[-L,L+1]
where there is a
+
or a
-
spin, Lemma
3.22
says
|J_{\sigma}|=2k
.

Under the action of the time-reversal symmetry, any vector in the canonical basis expansion of
\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})
is mapped to

\tr\left[X_{i_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdots X_{i_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})\right]|i_{-L}\dots i_{L+1}\rangle\longmapsto\tr\left[X_{-i_{L+1}}(\omega_{L+1})\cdots X_{-i_{-L}}(\omega_{-L})\right]|(-i_{-L}),\dots,(-i_{L+1})\rangle

but as we demonstrated above the coefficients are equal, hence either both or neither vector appears in the sum. Thus
\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})
is invariant under time-reversal symmetry for every nondegenerate bi-infinite sequence of values
(\omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
(0,\pi/2)\cup(\pi/2,\pi)
.
∎

Recent works of Ogata
[
63
]
generalized and extended the works of Pollmann et al.
[
71
,
72
]
and Tasaki
[
82
,
83
]
showing that for time-reversal protected pure states on the spin chain, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-topological index.

A key step in Ogata’s method is the fact that injective matrix product states have a unique MPS representations (up to unitary conjugation and a phase)
[
31
,
68
]
, and that furthermore, they obey Matsui’s
split property

[
52
,
53
]
. Ogata shows in
[
64
, Theorem 6.1]
that her index agrees with the index of
[
71
]
in the case of a matrix product state. While it is generally known that string order parameters correspond to local symmetries of MPS
[
69
]
(which again utilizes the uniqueness of injective MPS representations) we do not currently know if such a uniqueness theorem exists for
ergodically disordered
injective matrix product states. We are also not aware of any literature that shows that the index calculation in
[
82
]
is the same as the index from
[
63
]
(though in the aforementioned works, both Tasaki and Ogata conclude that the AKLT state has index
-1
). For these reasons, we seek to explicitly compute the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index of
\nu_{\omega}
using the methods presented in
[
82
]
, without appealing to the methods of
[
63
,
71
,
72
]
, and
[
83
, Section 8.3.4]
.

We shall take a few lines to recall Tasaki’s method from
[
82
]
. Recall that the
Affleck-Lieb twist operator
is a pure tensor of local unitary operators in the spin chain defined as follows: for
L>1
an integer, set

T_{L}:=\bigotimes_{j\in[-L,L+1]}\exp\left\{-2\pi i\,\frac{j+L}{2L+1}\,S^{z}_{j}\right\}

(3.49)

with
S^{z}_{j}
the
j
-site spin-z matrix for a spin 1. Tasaki then goes on to show the following

Theorem 3.24

(Theorem 1
[
82
]
)
.

Suppose
\varphi
is the unique
gapped
ground state of a local Hamiltonian on the spin-1 chain. Then, the value
\varphi(T_{\ell})
is real and has constant sign for
\ell
sufficiently large. In particular, the quantity
\mathscr{O}(\varphi):=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{\varphi(T_{\ell})}{|\varphi(T_{\ell})|}\in\{\pm 1\}\,.

Even though our disordered state
\psi_{\omega}
gapless, we show in Theorem
3.36
below, that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-index
\mathscr{O}(\nu_{\omega})=-1
in probability. We take a few lines to outline the steps we take in this direction.

1.

First, we note that due to Lemmas
3.5
and
3.2
, that we have the estimate

|\|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\|^{2}-1|=|\langle\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})|\Gamma^{[m,n]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\nu_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\langle\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}|\leq\|\Phi_{m,n}-T\|\to 0

Thus, the quantity
\|\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\|^{2}\to 1
almost surely, and furthermore,
\nu_{\omega}(T_{L})
is almost surely approximated by
\langle\Gamma_{\omega}^{[-L,L+1]}(\mathbbm{1})|T_{L}|\Gamma_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\rangle
. Therefore, it is sufficient to consider the quantity
\langle\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\rangle\geq 0
and show this goes to zero for large
L
.

2.

Due to the Lemma
3.25
below, one can realize the expected value
\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma^{[L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})\rangle
as the expected value of a function
f(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{L})
, of a classical stochastic process of IID random variables
(\lambda_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
whose distribution is chosen to “simulate” the coefficients of
\mathbb{E}\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}
.

3.

Finally, we show that the expected value of
f(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{L})
(with respect to its native probability measure) converges to
0
with large probability.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
(\omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence of IID random variables taking values in
[0,\frac{\pi}{4}]
. Assume that
\mathbb{P}[\delta<\omega_{0}]>0
for all
\delta>0
in range, and that
\mathbb{P}
is non-atomic. Let also
\nu_{\omega}
be the disordered AKLT state from Theorem
3.20
. Let
L>0
and denote by
\sigma=(\sigma_{x})_{x\in[-L,L+1]}\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{2L+2}
an arbitrary spin configuration. Let
k
be an integer and let
A_{2k}
be the set of those spin configurations with alternating signs and an even number of excitations. Then the following hold.

i.)

Let
c^{2}:=\mathbb{E}\cos^{2}(\omega_{0})
and
s^{2}:=\mathbb{E}\sin^{2}(\omega_{0})
. Then,
\frac{1}{2}<c^{2}<1
and
0<s^{2}<\frac{1}{2}
and furthermore
s^{2}+c^{2}=1
.

ii.)

Let
\sigma\in A_{2k}
, and let
J_{\sigma}=\{x\in[-L,L+1]\colon\sigma_{x}\neq 0\}
. We shall write
J_{\sigma}=\{x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{2k}\}
in increasing order with the understanding that the
x_{m}
depend on
\sigma
. Then, Tasaki’s formula holds:

\sum_{x=-L}^{L+1}\frac{x+L}{2L+1}\sigma_{x}=-\sigma_{x_{1}}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{x_{2m}-x_{2m-1}}{2L+1}

iii.)

Let
L>0
and set
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma^{[-L,L+1]}_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})
for convenience. Then,

\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle=2\sum_{k=0}^{L+1}(s^{2})^{2L+2-2k}(c^{2})^{2k}\sum_{\sigma\in A_{2k}}\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{x_{2m}(\sigma)-x_{2m-1}(\sigma)}{2L+1}\right)

(3.50)

Proof.

The proofs of parts i.) and ii.) are straightforward. To see part iii.) use equation (
3.45
) to compute directly the following:

\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma:\\
\sum|\sigma_{x}|=2k\\
k=0,\dots,L+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{x\in J_{\sigma}}\cos^{2}(\omega_{x})\prod_{y\in J^{c}_{\sigma}}\sin^{2}(\omega_{y})\,\exp\left\{-2\pi i\sum_{x=-L}^{L+1}\frac{\sigma_{x}(x+L)}{2L+1}\right\}

But notice that the summand associated to a given configuration
\sigma
has a coefficient equal to the complex conjugate of the summand associated to its time-reversal,
-\sigma
. Thus,

\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in A_{k}\end{subarray}}\prod_{x\in J_{\sigma}}\cos^{2}(\omega_{x})\prod_{y\in J^{c}_{\sigma}}\sin^{2}(\omega_{y})\,\cos\left(-2\pi\sum_{x=-L}^{L+1}\frac{\sigma_{x}(x+L)}{2L+1}\right)\,.

Taking expectations and using Tasaki’s formula, and the fact that cosine is even yields equation (
3.50
).
∎

We will now construct a classical stochastic process that models the coefficients of
\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle
. The idea is to treat the spaces between a pair of excitations in some spin configuration
\sigma\in A_{2k}
as a random variable that occurs with a specific weight.

Notation 3.26
.

Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots
be a sequence of
\mathbb{N}
-valued IID random variables with distribution

\mathcal{P}[\lambda=n]=c^{2}(s^{2})^{n-1}\quad n=1,2,\dots

(3.51)

where
c^{2}
and
s^{2}
are as in Lemma
3.25
above. One easily checks
P
is a probability distribution. We let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
denote expectation with respect to
\mathcal{P}
, and notice
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\,\lambda=(c^{2})^{-1}
. It is a standard fact that one can find a measure
\mathcal{Q}
for the process
(\lambda_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
whose finite dimensional distributions are given by

\mathcal{Q}[n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k}]=\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{1}=n_{1},\lambda_{2}=n_{2},\dots,\lambda_{k}=n_{k}]=(c^{2})^{k}(s^{2})^{(\sum_{1}^{k}n_{j})-k}\,.

(3.52)

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}
the expectation with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
.

We recall the following properties of geometric random variables which are more or less standard.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\lambda
be a geometrically distributed random variable with success parameter
r\in(0,1)
. That is,

P[\lambda=n]=r(1-r)^{n-1},\forall n\geq 1\,.

Then,

1.

The mean
\mu:=E\lambda=\frac{1}{r}
,

2.

The moment generating function
m(t):=E[e^{t\lambda}]
exists for all
t<-\log(1-r)
, which implies that
\lambda
has finite moments.

3.

(
[
21
, Remark 2.2.3(c) p.27]
) If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,
are IID copies of
\lambda
then for all
x>0
, we have

P[|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}-n\mu|\geq nx]\leq 2e^{-n\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+x),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-x)\}}

(3.53)

where
\Lambda^{*}(\mu+x)
is the function

\Lambda^{*}(x+\mu)=\sup_{0\leq t<-\log(1-r)}\{t(x+\mu)-\log\left(m(t)\right)\}

In particular, the supremum is achieved at

t_{*}=\log(1-(x+\mu)^{-1})-\log(1-r)\,.

4.

The optimal both
\Lambda^{*}(\mu)
and
(\Lambda^{*})^{\prime}(\mu)=0
. Thus by Taylor’s theorem,
\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\delta)=\delta^{2}+o(\delta^{2})
as
\delta\downarrow 0
. In particular, this means that the exponential estimate above, we can replace
\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\delta),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\delta)\}
with
\delta^{2}
after some corrective constant depending on
\delta
.

The proof of the above amounts to a straightforward exercise in probability theory.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda)_{n=1}^{\infty}
denote the stochastic process defined above. We may rewrite

\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle=4\sum_{k=0}^{L+1}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[1_{\sum_{1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{j}\leq 2L+1}f_{k}(\vec{\lambda})]+

where

f_{k}(\vec{\lambda}):=\left(2L+2-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}\right)(s^{2})^{2L+2-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}}\left[\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{2L+1}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}\right)+1\right]\quad\forall k\geq 1

(3.54)

and where we set
f_{0,L}=(s^{2})^{2L+2}
in the case of zero excitations.

Proof.

To begin, let
k\in[1,L+1]
, and consider an alternating spin configuration
\sigma\in A_{2k}
with
2k
-many nonzero sites. Write
J_{\sigma}=\{x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{2k}\}
and let
n_{j}=x_{j+1}-x_{j}
for
j=1,2,\dots,2k-1
. In this way, we can re-write each
x_{j}=x_{1}+n_{1}+n_{2}+\dots+n_{j-1}
(each quantity depends on
\sigma
) for all
j=1,2,\dots,2k
. Furthermore, we may employ Tasaki’s formula for this specific
\sigma
to the cosine term to obtain:

\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{x_{2m}-x_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)=\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{n_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)\,.

This suggests we re-write our sum from the RHS of equation (
3.50
) by specifying the lengths between excitations, and the starting point for the configuration. This yields the following:

\begin{split}\sum_{\sigma\in A_{2k}}\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{x_{2m}-x_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)&=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{2k}\geq 0}1_{\sum n_{j}\leq 2L+1}\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{n_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)\times\cdots\\
&\quad\cdots\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
-L\leq y\leq L+1-\sum n_{j}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\sigma\in A_{2k}}1_{x_{j}(\sigma)=y+\sum_{1}^{j-1}n_{m},\,\forall j}(\sigma)\end{split}

(3.55)

The two innermost sums in the above expression specify the locations of the excitations in
\sigma
, but not their signs. However, we know the signs are alternating since
\sigma\in A_{2k}
. Therefore, we observe that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{Z}\colon\\
-L\leq y\leq L+1-\sum n_{j}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\sigma\in A_{2k}}1_{x_{j}(\sigma)=y+\sum_{1}^{j-1}n_{m},\,\forall j}(\sigma)=2|[-L,L+1-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}n_{m}]\cap\mathbb{Z}|=2(2L+2-\sum n_{j})\,.

Now, define the family of functions
f_{k,L}:\mathbb{N}^{2k-1}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\begin{split}f_{k,L}(n_{1},\dots,n_{2k-1})&=\chi(n_{1},\dots,n_{2k-1})\cdot(s^{2})^{2L+2-\sum n_{m}}\cdot\left(2L+2-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}n_{m}\right)\cdot\left[\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{n_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)+1\right]\end{split}

where we put
\chi_{L}=1_{\{\sum n_{m}\leq 2L+1\}}(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})
the indicator function.

Taking this all together, we apply Lemmas
3.22
, and
3.25
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=4\sum_{k=1}^{L+1}(s^{2})^{2L+2-2k}(c^{2})^{2k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{2k-1}\colon\\
\sum n_{j}\leq 2L+1\end{subarray}}(2L+2-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}n_{m})\left(\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{n_{2m-1}}{2L+1}\right)+1\right)+\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots+4(s^{2})^{2L+2}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{k=1}^{L+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{2k-1}\colon\\
\sum n_{j}\leq 2L+1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Q}[n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{2k-1}]f_{k,L}(n_{1},\dots,n_{2k-1})+4(s^{2})^{2L+2}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{k=0}^{L+1}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L}]

where we have used the definition of the probability measure
\mathcal{Q}
to obtain the last equality.
∎

We aim to show that for large values of
L
, the value of
\mathbb{E}[\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle]
is vanishingly small. To do so, we isolate six different regimes depending on
k
,
L
, and auxiliary parameters which will control how close
\sum\lambda_{j}
is to its mean. We take a few lines to describe the heuristic reasoning behind our decomposition.

Using Lemma
3.28
, we know that for various numbers of excitations, we have a sum over the random lengths
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{2k-1}
between up/down pairs. Using the Law of Large numbers, we know that for large enough
k
, the empirical sum will be close to the mean
\mu
. Thus, the leading coefficient of
f_{k,L}
is can be manipulated as follows

2L+2-\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{j}=2L+2-(2k-1)\left[\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{j}-\mu\right]-\mu(2k-1)\approx 2L+2-\mu(2k-1).

we see that in order for this coefficient to increase in
L
(which would force exponential decay of the integrand
f_{k,L}
due to the
(s^{2})
term), that we should require
L>>\mu k
. Conversely, if
k>\mu^{-1}L
, then the only part of the integrand which
could
decay is the cosine term. On the other hand, if
\sum\lambda_{j}
is far from its mean, one expects to be able to use the large deviations principle to estimate the probability of these events. This will only get us exponential decay in
k
. However, these large deviation bounds fail for small
k<<\mu^{-1}L
. In this case, we invent a procedure that compares
k
to a fractional power of
L
, to obtain stretched-exponential upper bounds.

Let us now begin the rigorous proof. First, set the notation

Notation 3.29
.

Let
\xi>0
be a real number and let
1\leq k\leq L+1
. The event when the empirical average
(2k-1)^{-1}\sum\lambda_{m}
is within
\xi
of the mean
\mu:=\mathcal{E}\lambda
is written

M_{k,\xi}:=\left\{\left|\frac{1}{2k-1}\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}-\mu\right|<\xi\right\}\,.

(3.56)

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
L>0
and let
\epsilon>0
. For any
\delta\in(0,\mu-\epsilon)
then, there is a constant
C_{1}>0
depending on
\mu
and
\epsilon
only so that

\sum_{k=0}^{(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[|f_{k,L}|,M_{k,\delta}]\leq C_{1}(L+1)^{2}(s^{2})^{2(1-\delta(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1})L}

(3.57)

Proof.

For a single
k
in range, one has the bound

-\delta(2k-1)\leq\sum\lambda_{m}-\mu(2k-1)\leq\delta(2k-1)

whence

2L+2-\sum\lambda_{m}\geq 2L+2-\delta(2k-1)\geq 2(1-\frac{\delta}{\mu+\epsilon})L+2-\mu-\epsilon>0\,.

Notice that our assumption on
\delta
ensures that
\delta(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}<1
Therefore, we may naively bound the integrand as follows:

f_{k,L}(\lambda)\leq 2(L+1)(s^{2})^{2(1-\delta(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1})L}(s^{2})^{2-\mu-\epsilon}

Whence,

\sum_{k=1}^{(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[|f_{k,L}|,M_{k,\delta}]\leq\frac{4(s^{2})^{2-\mu-\epsilon}}{\mu+\epsilon}(L+1)^{2}s^{2(1-\delta(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1})L})\to 0\text{ as }L\to\infty\,.

∎

Let us turn to the complimentary case when the small number of excitations are far from the average distance apart. Note that from now on, we will often implicitly use the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\sum\lambda\leq 2L+1}
to ensure that
(s^{2})^{2L+2-\sum\lambda_{m}}\leq 1
.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
L>0
, and pick
0<\delta<\mu-\epsilon
as in Lemma
3.30
, above. Then there is a constant
C_{2}>0
depending on
\mu,\epsilon,s^{2},c^{2}
so that for any
p\in(0,1)
, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L},M_{k,\delta}^{c}]\leq C_{2}L(L+1)\left[e^{-\delta L^{p}}+e^{-(L^{p}/\delta)}+(s^{2})^{2L-(1+\delta^{-1}\mu)L^{p}}\right]\,.

(3.58)

Furthermore, if
p<\frac{2\delta}{\delta+\mu}
, then the RHS decays to zero as
L\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
p\in(0,1)
to be chosen later and consider
L^{p}
. For any given value of
\sum\lambda_{j}
, there are three possibilities for how large
L^{p}
is in relation to the inequality
|\sum\lambda_{j}-\mu(2k-1)|\geq\delta(2k-1)
. Either
L^{p}
is smaller than both,
L^{p}
is in the middle, or
L^{p}
is bigger than both. This suggests the following decomposition of
M_{k,\delta}^{c}
:

\begin{split}M_{k,\delta}^{c}=\left[L^{p}<\delta(2k-1)\leq|\sum\lambda-\mu(2k-1)|\right]&\sqcup\left[\delta(2k-1)\leq L^{p}\leq|\sum\lambda-\mu(2k-1)|\right]\\
&\sqcup\left[\delta(2k-1)\leq|\sum\lambda-\mu(2k-1)|\leq L^{p}\right]\\
=:A_{k}\sqcup B_{k}\sqcup C_{k}\end{split}

(3.59)

In the first case, applying inequality (
3.53
) leads us to the bound

\mathcal{Q}[A_{k}]\leq 2\exp\{-\delta^{2}(2k-1)\}\leq 2\exp\{-\delta L^{p}

since here, we have
2k-1>\delta^{-1}L^{p}
. Similarly,

\mathcal{Q}[B_{k}]\leq 2\exp\left\{-(2k-1)\frac{L^{2p}}{\delta^{2}(2k-1)^{2}}\right\}\leq 2\exp\{-\delta^{-1}L^{p}\}

since
\delta(2k-1)\leq L^{p}
on this event.

In the third case, we argue similarly to Lemma
3.30
above: unwind the absolute value to see that we have the inequality

\sum\lambda_{j}\leq L^{p}+\mu(2k-1)

in particular, this yields a lower bound for the exponent on
s^{2}
as

2L+2-\sum\lambda_{j}\geq 2L+2-L^{p}-\mu(2k-1)\geq 2L+2-(1+\frac{\mu}{\delta})L^{p}

(3.60)

where we have used the fact that on
C_{k}
, we also have
\delta(2k-1)<L^{p}
.

Turning to the sum of the integrands, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L},M_{k,\delta}^{c}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\mu+\epsilon}\,L(L+1)\left(2e^{-\delta L^{p}}+2e^{-(L/\delta)}+(s^{2})^{2-\mu}(s^{2})^{2L-(1+\delta^{-1}\mu)L^{p}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu-\epsilon}L(L+1)\left(e^{-\delta L^{p}}+2e^{-(L/\delta)}+(s^{2})^{2L-(1+\delta^{-1}\mu)L^{p}}\right)

We note that a little calculus shows that taking
p<\frac{2\delta}{\delta+\mu}
is sufficient to guarantee that the exponent
2L-(1+\delta^{-1}\mu)L^{p}
is increasing in
L
.
∎

Based on the heuristic reasoning above, we expect that the “middle regime” where
(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L\leq k\leq(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}L
, that convergence to zero will be imparted by the cosine terms. Therefore we need slightly tighter control of the sum than we did before, so we introduce the following event.

Notation 3.32
.

Define the family of events

\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}=\left[\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right]\cap\left[\left|\frac{1}{k-1}\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{2m}-\mu\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right]\,,

(3.61)

which is the event that the sums of the even intervals and odd intervals are close to their means.

Notice that
\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}\subset M_{k,\epsilon}
. Indeed, by the triangle inequality,

|\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}-\mu(2k-1)|=|\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu\,k|+|\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{2m}-\mu(k-1)k|\leq\epsilon(2k-1),

however the reverse inclusion need not hold.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
L,\epsilon>0
. Fix any
\eta>\frac{1}{2}+(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}
consider the regime where
(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L\leq k\leq(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}L+\eta
on the events
\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}
defined as in (
3.61
). For any
\epsilon<\mu/7
, and any
L\geq\frac{4\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)-1}{2}
, there is a function
C_{3}(L)
for which
\limsup_{L\to\infty}C_{3}\leq 1
, so that

\sum_{k\geq\frac{L}{\mu+\epsilon}}^{\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta}\mathcal{E}_{Q}[f_{k,L},\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}]\leq C_{3}\left(\cos\left(\pi\left[\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}\right]\right)+1\right)

(3.62)

Proof.

Recall that the function
x\mapsto\cos(\pi x)
is decreasing on the interval
x\in(0,3/2)
. Our aim is to establish deterministic upper and lower bounds for
\sum_{m=1}^{k}\frac{2\lambda_{2m-1}}{2L+1}
in terms of
L
and
\epsilon
, which converge toward
1
as
L\to\infty
.

For a fixed
k
in range, on the event
\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon},
we have the we have the upper estimate

\displaystyle\frac{2k}{2L+1}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu\right)+\frac{2\mu k}{2L+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k\epsilon}{2L+1}+\frac{2\mu k}{2L+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\epsilon+2\mu)(L(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}+\eta)}{2L+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{2L+1}\frac{2\mu+\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}+\frac{\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)}{2L+2}

Notice that for any
\epsilon<\mu/7
, and any
L\geq\frac{4\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)-1}{2}
, we have that

\frac{L}{2L+1}\frac{2\mu+\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}+\frac{\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)}{2L+2}\leq\frac{5}{4}+\frac{1}{4}=\frac{3}{2}

On the flip side, we have the lower bound:

\displaystyle\frac{2k}{2L+1}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu\right)+\frac{2\mu k}{2L+1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-\epsilon k}{2L+1}+\frac{\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}\frac{2L}{2L+1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-\epsilon(L/(\mu-\epsilon)+\eta)}{2L+1}+\frac{\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}\frac{2L}{2L+1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\,\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\,\eta}{2L+1}+\frac{\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}\frac{2L}{2L+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}

In summary, for any
\epsilon<\mu/7
, and any
L\geq\frac{4\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)-1}{2}
,

\begin{split}\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}&\leq\frac{2k}{2L+1}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu\right)+\frac{2\mu k}{2L+1}\\
&\leq\frac{L}{2L+1}\frac{2\mu+\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}+\frac{\eta(2\mu+\epsilon)}{2L+2}<\frac{3}{2}\end{split}\,.

(3.63)

Since the lower bound is strictly positive for all
L
, this means that we have the upper bound

\cos\left(\pi\,\frac{2}{2L+1}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\lambda_{2m-1}\right)\leq\cos\left(\pi\left[\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}\right]\right)\to-1\text{ as $\epsilon\downarrow 0,L\to\infty$}\,.

(3.64)

Since this bound holds independently of the trajectories, we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq\frac{L}{\mu+\epsilon}}^{\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta}\mathcal{E}_{Q}[f_{k,L},\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\cos\left(\pi\left[\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}\right]\right)+1\right)\times\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\times\sum_{k\geq(\mu+\epsilon)^{-1}L}^{(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}+\eta}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[(2L+2-\sum\lambda_{m})(s^{2})^{2L+2-\sum\lambda_{m}},\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\cos\left(\pi\left[\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}\right]\right)+1\right)\mathbb{E}(\langle\Gamma_{L}|\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle),

where we have used the fact that
\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle
has an expansion similar to that found in Lemma
3.28
. Since we know that
\|\Gamma_{L}\|^{2}\to 1
almost surely as
L\to\infty
, so taking
C_{3}(L)=\mathbb{E}(\|\Gamma_{L}\|^{2})
, the reverse Fatou lemma gives us the claimed inequality.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
L,\epsilon>0
and
\eta>\frac{1}{2}(1+(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1})
. Then is a constant
C_{4}>0
depending only on
\mu
and
\epsilon
so that

\sum_{k\geq\frac{L}{\mu+\epsilon}}^{\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L},\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}^{c}]+\sum_{k\geq\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta}^{L+1}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L},M_{k,\epsilon}^{c}]\leq C_{4}L(L+1)\exp\left\{-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\mu+\epsilon}L\right\}

(3.65)

Proof.

The strategy for each summand here is to apply Cramér’s theorem for sums of independent random variables
[
21
, Theorem 2.3.3]
to estimate both of the probabilities
\mathcal{Q}[\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}^{c}]
and
\mathcal{Q}[M_{k,\epsilon}]
.

In the
\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}
case, we apply inequality (
3.53
) to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}[\tilde{M}_{k,\epsilon}^{c}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{Q}[|\sum\lambda_{2m-1}-\mu k|\geq\epsilon k]+\mathcal{Q}[|\sum\lambda_{2k}-(k-1)\mu|\geq\epsilon(k-1)]

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+e^{\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}})e^{-k\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+e^{\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}})\exp\{-\frac{\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}}{\mu+\epsilon}L\}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2\exp\{-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\mu+\epsilon}L\}

Similarly, one finds that when
\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}\leq k\leq L+1
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}[M_{k,\epsilon}^{c}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-(2k-1)\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}}\exp\{-(\frac{2\min\{\Lambda^{*}(\mu+\epsilon),\Lambda^{*}(-\mu-\epsilon)\}}{\mu-\epsilon}L\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\{-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{\mu-\epsilon}L\}

Putting these pairs of regimes together in the sum yields the claim.
∎

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
L,\epsilon>0
. Let
\eta>\frac{1}{2}(1+(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1})
then,

\sum_{k\geq\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta}^{L+1}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}[f_{k,L},M_{k,\epsilon}]=0

(3.66)

Proof.

We will show that the event
M_{k,\epsilon}\cap[\sum\lambda_{m}\leq 2L+1]
is empty whenever
k\geq(\mu-\epsilon)^{-1}L+\eta
. Given

\left|\frac{1}{2k-1}\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}-\mu\right|\leq\epsilon,

we obtain the lower bound

\sum_{m=1}^{2k-1}\lambda_{m}\geq(\mu-\epsilon)(2k-1)\geq 2(\mu-\epsilon)\left(\frac{L}{\mu-\epsilon}+\eta\right)-(\mu-\epsilon)\geq 2L+2\eta(\mu-\epsilon)-(\mu-\epsilon)>2L+1\,,

which yields a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 3.36

(Theorem
D
)
.

Let
\nu_{\omega}
be the disordered AKLT state as above. Then,

\lim_{L\to\infty}\|\nu_{\omega}(T_{L})+1\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{P})}=0\,.

(3.67)

Proof.

Let
0<\epsilon<\frac{\mu}{7}
, and fix
0<\delta<\mu-\epsilon
,
0<p<\frac{2\delta}{\delta+\mu}
, as in Lemmas
3.30
–
3.35
above. Taken together, we have shown there exists a function

0<g(L,\epsilon,\delta,\eta,p)=:g_{\epsilon}(L)

so that

\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle\leq g_{\epsilon}(L),

and for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}g_{\epsilon}(L)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{L\to\infty}\left(\cos\left(\pi\left[\left(\frac{2\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{\mu-\epsilon}\right)\frac{L}{2L+1}-\frac{\epsilon\eta}{2L+1}\right]\right)+1\right)

\displaystyle=\cos\left[\pi\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{2(\mu-\epsilon)}\right)\right]+1\,.

Putting this together, we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle\leq\cos\left[\pi\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{2(\mu-\epsilon)}\right)\right]+1

But the left-hand side does not depend on
\epsilon
at all, so we may take
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, which yields

\lim_{L\to\infty}\|\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{P})}=0

since
\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle\geq 0
.

Now, let
\Phi_{m,n}=E^{[m,n]}_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}
, be the ergodic quantum process defined by the random quantum channel
E_{\mathbbm{1},\omega}
as in Lemma
3.2
, and let
\cal{T}
denote the replacement channel defined by taking the normalized trace. Notice,
\|\Phi_{[-L,L+1]}-T\|\leq 2
almost surely thus, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem and using the upper bound from Lemma
3.2
, one obtains
\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\|\Phi_{[-L,L+1]}-\mathcal{T}\|=0

Recall from Lemma
3.5
part c.) that the following inequalities hold almost surely,

\left.\begin{array}[]{cc}|\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle-\nu_{\omega}(T_{L})|&\leq\|\Phi_{[-L,L+1]}-\mathcal{T}\|\\
|\|\Gamma_{L}\|^{2}-1|&\leq\|\Phi_{[-L,L+1]}-\mathcal{T}\|\end{array}\right\}

(3.68)

Therefore, using the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\nu_{\omega}(T_{L})+1|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}|\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle-\nu_{\omega}(T_{L})|+\mathbb{E}|\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}|+\mathbb{E}|\|\Gamma_{L}\|^{2}-\nu_{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\|\Phi_{[-L,L+1]}-\mathcal{T}\|+\mathbb{E}|\langle\Gamma_{L}|T_{L}+\mathbbm{1}|\Gamma_{L}\rangle|\to 0\text{ as }L\to\infty,

by the above arguments.
∎

Below, we compute the null space of the vectors corresponding to the spin 0 subspace of the intersection. We obtain the matrix M below by first finding a basis for
G_{12}
(resp.
G_{23}
) by computing

G_{12}:=\spn_{1\leq k,l\leq 2}\left\{\sum_{i_{j},i_{j+1}}\langle l|A^{i_{j}}_{j}A^{i_{j}}_{i_{j+1}}|k\rangle\cdot|i_{j},i_{j+1}\rangle\right\}

with the understanding that we will substitute
1
for
j
,
2
for
j+2
,
3
for
j+2
in the code.

Then, we consider the possibilities for the additional vector in
\mathbb{C}^{3}|m\rangle
allowing
m\in\{-1,0,+1\}
. So there are only certain allowed combinations with the basis we found for
G_{12}
in Lemma
3.7
. Namely, those whose spin indices in the computational basis add to
0
(e.g.
|-0+\rangle
) . In each case, we get one vector from
m=1
, two vectors from
m=0
, and one vector from
m=-1
. There are seven kets whose spin indices add to zero that appear in the expansions (they are:
|-0+\rangle,|-+0\rangle,|0-+\rangle,|000\rangle,|0+-\rangle,|+-0\rangle,|+0-\rangle
) We label the rows of
M
(in this order) by these kets, and the basis vectors on the left and right-hand sides form the columns, giving us a
7\times 8
matrix.
